The front cover of this book is bound in red <term>morocco leather</term> <note>A high-quality, durable leather made from goatskin, often dyed in vibrant colors for luxury bindings.</note>, featuring elaborate <term>gold-tooled</term> <note>A decorative technique where gold leaf is pressed into the leather using heated brass stamps or wheels.</note> borders with repeating floral and scroll patterns. In the center is a gold-stamped <term>heraldic device</term>—a <term>coat of arms</term>—consisting of a shield with diagonal bands, supported by two <term>angels</term> and topped with a crown. A banner at the base of the crest contains the following <term>motto</term>:

->**By Virtue and Faith**<-
<note>original Latin: "Virtute et Fide"</note>

<summary>This page recounts the medieval myth of the Barnacle Goose as described by Gerald of Wales, asserting that the birds grow from driftwood rather than hatching from eggs. This belief was famously used to categorize the geese as non-meat, allowing them to be eaten during religious fasts.</summary>

<meta>The top left margin contains the number 16757 written in pencil. This page details the legendary origins of the Barnacle Goose, a common myth in medieval and early modern natural history.</meta>

# Barnacles [or Clack Geese] p. 53, 54 <note>Roman numerals liii and liv refer to pages 53 and 54.</note>

In Ireland there are many fowls, which are called <term>Bernacles <note>Original Latin: *Bernacæ*. These are Barnacle Geese (*Branta leucopsis*).</note></term>, which are produced after a wonderful manner, contrary to the common course of Nature, somewhat like wild geese, but of a smaller size: they first appear like gum, upon logs of fir, which are tossed upon the surface of the Sea: after this they are covered in and shut up by the Sea Weed and small shells sticking together upon the Wood, where having room enough to be completely formed, they hang by their bills, till being in due time completely feathered, they either drop down into the Water, or else immediately fly off into the open air. 

They are supposed to receive their nourishment and growth after a most secret and wonderful manner from the sap of the Fir, and the Salt Water. I myself have often seen with my own eyes, above a thousand of these little <term>birdlings <note>young or miniature birds</note></term>, perfectly shaped and enclosed with shells, upon the sea shore, hanging together from one piece of Wood. They never produce any Eggs like other birds; they are never hatched after the common manner. They are never observed to <term>tread <note>a traditional term for birds mating</note></term>, or to have any nests, in any part of the World. From hence it is, that in some parts of Ireland the Bishops and other <term>Religious <note>members of religious orders, such as monks or friars</note></term> make no scruple of eating them on <note>Fast Days: days of religious abstinence when eating "flesh" (meat from land animals) was forbidden. Because these birds were believed to originate from wood rather than being born from flesh, they were categorized as fish or plant-matter for dietary purposes.</note> Fast Days as not being flesh, because not produced by flesh—

<summary>This page describes the mythical "spontaneous generation" of Barnacle Geese from driftwood and sea-shells in Ireland, a belief used to justify eating them during religious fasts.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introduction to a medieval Bestiary, beginning with a general definition of wild animals before focusing on the lion, traditionally the first animal described in such manuscripts due to its status as the "king of beasts." The text draws heavily from Isidore of Seville’s etymologies and the Physiologus.</meta>

The name **beasts** properly applies to lions, leopards, tigers, wolves, and foxes, as well as to large dogs and all other animals that rage with either tooth or claw—with the exception of serpents. They are called "beasts" <note>original: "bestie"</note> because of the "force" <note>original: "ui" (vis), a common medieval etymological link between *bestia* and *vis*</note> with which they rage. They are also called "wild animals" <note>original: "fere"</note> because they enjoy natural liberty and are driven by their own desires. Their wills are free; they wander here and there, and they are carried wherever their spirits lead them.

The word "lion" was brought into the Latin language from a Greek origin; for in Greek it is called *leon*. This is a "new" name because it has been partially altered from the original. For "Leo" in Greek is interpreted as "king" in Latin <note>original: "Leo enī grece latine rex interpretatur." This is a folk etymology; the author is linking the Greek word for lion to the concept of royalty</note>, because he is the prince of all beasts. 

The lion's kind is said to be "triumphant." Among them, those that are short with curly manes are peaceful, while those that are long with straight hair are courageous. The sharpness of their spirits is revealed by their forehead and their tail; their strength lies in the chest, while their steadfastness is in the head. They fear the rattling of wheels, but they fear fire even more. Whether diligent or proud, the lion does not know how to mingle his ferocity with other kinds of wild animals; instead, like a true king, he disdains the company of the many.

### The Lion

The *Physiologus* <note>A highly influential Early Christian text that provided symbolic and moral interpretations of animals</note> says that the lion has three primary characteristics. Its first nature is that it loves to roam across the mountain peaks. And if it happens that the lion is pursued by hunters, 

<summary>This page introduces the classification of "beasts" and "wild animals" based on their fierce nature and freedom, followed by an anatomical and symbolic description of the lion as the king of animals.</summary>
<keywords>bestiary, lion, etymology, wild animals, Physiologus, animal symbolism, medieval natural history</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the lion, the first entry in the medieval Bestiary. Having established the lion’s status as "king" on the previous page, the text now moves into the *Physiologus* tradition, which assigns three specific "natures" or behaviors to the lion, each serving as a Christian allegory for the life, death, and resurrection of Jesus.</meta>

### The Lion (Continued)

...the scent of hunters reaches him; and with his tail, he covers his tracks behind his back. Thus, the hunters are unable to track him. In the same way, our Savior—namely the spiritual lion of the tribe of Judah, the root of Jesse, and the son of David—covered the traces of his incarnation <note>original: "incarnationis," referring to the mystery of God becoming human</note> in the heavens until, sent by the Father, he descended into the womb of the Virgin Mary to save the human race which had perished. The devil, the enemy of mankind, was ignorant of this and dared to tempt him as if he were a mere man. Even the angels on high were ignorant of this as he ascended to the Father; they said to those who were ascending with him:

> Who is this King of Glory?

His **second nature** is this: that when he sleeps, he is seen to have his eyes open. Just so, our Lord, while sleeping physically on the cross and being buried, remained awake in his divinity <note>The medieval view was that Christ’s human body died and "slept" in the tomb, but his divine nature remained eternally active and watchful</note>, as it is said in the Song of Songs:

> I sleep, and my heart watches.

And in the Psalm:

> Behold, he who keeps Israel shall neither slumber nor sleep.

His **third nature** is this: When the lioness gives birth to her cubs, she brings them forth dead. She guards them for three days until their father, coming on the third day, breathes into their faces <note>original: "insufflat," a term echoing God breathing life into Adam in Genesis</note> and brings them to life. In this way, the Almighty Father raised our Lord Jesus Christ from the dead on the third day, as Jacob said:

> He shall sleep like a lion, and like a lion's cub he shall be awakened.

It is also the nature of lions that they cannot be provoked to anger unless they are harmed. Rational men ought to look to this example—those who grow angry without being harmed and oppress the innocent, even though Christian law commands us to let the harmful go free <note>original: "noxios dimittere liberos," likely referring to the virtue of forgiveness even toward those who have done wrong</note>. Indeed, the father of lions is constant in his examples of **mercy**; he spares those who are prostrate <note>those lying face-down in submission</note> and allows captives he encounters to return home. He vents his rage on men rather than women, and they do not kill infants except in times of great hunger. At birth...

<summary>This page details the three allegorical "natures" of the lion, representing Christ's hidden incarnation, his wakeful divinity during death, and his resurrection after three days. It concludes by praising the lion’s natural mercy as a model for human behavior.</summary>

<keywords>lion, Christology, Incarnation, Resurrection, King of Glory, Physiologus, mercy, spiritual lion, tribe of Judah</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the lion, transitioning from the symbolic "three natures" (which mirror the life of Christ) to more specific, quasi-scientific observations about the animal’s physical life, digestion, and behavior in the wild, as understood by medieval scholars.</meta>

<margin>E</margin>
All lions regulate themselves when they are satiated. First, they take drink on alternating days, and food on the others. But if digestion has not followed frequent feeding, they set aside a day from their usual mealtime. Then, if the meat they have devoured is more than is proper, they voluntarily pull it out by inserting their claws into their mouths. Indeed, they do the same when they must flee while they are full. Old age in lions is proven by the loss of their teeth. They mate facing one another <note>original: "aduersi," meaning they face each other rather than the male mounting the female from behind, which was a common belief about certain noble animals</note>. Nor is it only these, but also lynxes, camels, elephants, rhinoceroses, tigers, and lionesses. Regarding their offspring: at first, they bring forth five cubs. Then, they reduce the number by one in each of the following years. And finally, when they have come down to one cub, the womb delivers the afterbirth and the mother becomes sterile forever. The lion loathes yesterday's food and turns away from the remains of its own meal. Indeed, what wild beast would dare to stand against him, in whose voice such a natural fear exists that many animals, which could escape his attack through their own speed, are instead struck down by the sound of his roar as if by a certain force, and collapse nearby. A sick lion seeks out a monkey to devour so that he may be healed <note>In medieval animal lore, it was believed that the flesh of a monkey acted as a medicinal cure for an ailing lion</note>. The lion fears the rooster, and especially the white one. Indeed, the lion, the king of beasts, is troubled by the tiny sting of a scorpion and is killed by the venom of a serpent. We have heard of small beasts called <term>Leontophones</term> <note>From the Greek meaning "lion-killers"</note>. These, when captured, are burnt so that by sprinkling their ashes over meat and milk where lions' paths cross, they might kill the lions, even if they taste only the slightest amount. Therefore, lions despise them with a natural hatred; and when given the opportunity, they refrain from biting them, but instead crush them to death by tearing them apart with the force of their paws.

<meta>This page transitions from the physical characteristics and lifecycle of lions discussed on the previous page to the symbolic and allegorical meaning of the lioness. It combines a theological interpretation of a biblical verse from the Book of Job with a detailed, practical description of how hunters in "certain regions" use a complex double-pit trap to capture these animals.</meta>

# The Lioness.

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature within a rectangular frame bordered in red and blue. The scene depicts the capture of a lioness. On the left, two small lion cubs are curled up in a blue, circular den. Above them, a larger lioness is shown descending or falling into a pit trap. To the right, another section of the trap is shown, featuring a green vertical structure bound with white bands. The ground is depicted as a series of colorful, stylized mounds in shades of blue, red, brown, and grey.</image-desc>

By the name of the lioness, the human mind is signified, as it is said through the blessed
Job: *And because of pride, you will catch me like a lioness.* <note>original: "& propter superbiam quasi leenam capies me." This refers to Job 10:16. Medieval theologians often used the lioness as a symbol for the soul that must be humbled by God.</note> For this reason, 
divine providence <term>dispensatio; the way God manages or "dispenses" the affairs of the world</term> allows us, who serve Him with all our intent, to be 
tried by the assaults of our flesh, lest our mind 
dare to be lifted up in the pride of its own presumed security. Rather, 
while it trembles from being struck, it may place the foot 
of hope more firmly on the help of its Creator alone. 
Wherefore it is well said: *And because of pride, you will catch me like 
a lioness.* Indeed, when the lioness seeks food for her cubs, 
she rushes open-mouthed into the pit of capture. For as 
it is reported from certain regions: a pit is made in her path 
in which a domestic animal is placed, so that the lioness may be 
provoked to stretch herself toward it by her appetite. This pit 
is prepared to be both narrow and deep, so that while she might 
be able to guard the passage by pacing around it, she can 
in no way escape it by leaping. And another 
pit is dug which is joined to the first one; however, it 
is connected to the one with the animal by a very 
narrow, small opening. In this [second pit], a cage 
is placed, so that when the lioness falls in—since she 
is pressed from above by trees—and seeks to hide 
herself in the more secret part of the pit, 
she enters the cage willingly. Her ferocity then little [suffices...] <note>The text breaks off here, likely continuing on the next page to describe how the captured lioness is handled.</note>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Job 10:16. It concludes the physical description of the lioness trap—where the animal is lifted out of a pit in a cage—and applies this imagery to the human soul. The "pit" represents the fall into sin (specifically the Fall of Adam), and the "cage" represents the necessary restrictions of divine discipline that keep the soul from falling again.</meta>

is captured <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page describing the trapping of the lioness.</note>: because she is lifted up while enclosed in a cage. Since she falls into the pit of her own accord, she returns to the higher parts—from which she was lost—by means of bars. 

In this way, indeed, the human mind is captured—that mind which was established in **liberty of will** <term>libertas arbitrii; the God-given capacity to choose between good and evil</term>. While it hungers for the desires of the flesh that nourish it, it seeks food just as a lioness does for her cubs; and so it fell into the pit of its own deception when, at the persuasion of **the enemy** <note>A common medieval reference to the Devil or the serpent in Eden.</note>, it stretched out its hand toward the forbidden food. 

But in that pit, the mind also finds a cage. Because it came to death by its own choice, it soon fed upon the prison of its own corruption; yet, through the discovery of **grace** <term>gratia; the unmerited divine assistance given to humans for their regeneration or sanctification</term>, it is led out again into the free air. However, when the mind tries to do many things and lacks the strength, it is bound by the obstacle of that same corruption, as if by the bars of a cage. 

Indeed, the mind has now escaped that pit of damnation into which it had fallen, because, aided by the hand of the **Redeemer** <term>redemptor; a title for Christ, referring to the "buying back" of humanity from sin</term>, it overcame the punishment of the death that followed by returning toward forgiveness. Yet, even so, the mind endures the cage while confined, for it is also held tight by the bonds of heavenly discipline so that it does not wander through the desires of the flesh. Therefore, that which fell into the pit of its own accord returns to the free air while enclosed. This is because it fell into guilt through the freedom of its will, and yet is held back—even when unwilling—by the grace of its Creator. 

Thus, the mind suffers the cage by way of the pit, so that, having been snatched from eternal punishment, it is bound away from the impulses of its own will under the management of the **heavenly craftsman** <note>original: "celestis artificis dispensacione." This portrays God as a wise architect or artisan who structures the life of the believer for their own benefit.</note>. Rightly, then, it is said: *And because of pride, you will catch me like a lioness* <note>original: "& propter superbiam quasi leenam capies me." Job 10:16.</note>, because the free man found death as food for himself, and once brought back to forgiveness, he lives better for being enclosed under discipline. 

Thus, like a lioness, the mind is caught through pride. For now, the discipline of its own corruption weighs it down in the very place where, not fearing the impending transgression, it boldly leaped into the pit. But even if for a short while we turn our mind’s eye away from the sin of our **first parent** <note>A reference to Adam and the concept of Original Sin.</note>, we still...

<summary>This page provides a moral interpretation of the lioness trap, equating the pit to man's fall through free will and the cage to the protective "prison" of divine discipline. It argues that while humanity is redeemed by grace, we remain restricted by our own corrupt nature and God's laws to prevent further spiritual wandering.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical commentary on Job 10:16 from Gregory the Great’s *Moralia in Job*. The previous page discussed how the human mind, like a lioness, falls into the "pit" of sin through its own choice but is led out by grace. This page explains that the "cage" of our own human weakness actually serves as a divine protection against the sin of pride.</meta>

daily we are found to be captured like the lioness through the path of **pride** <term>elatio; a state of being "puffed up" or arrogant regarding one's own spiritual progress</term>. For often it happens that a person, having received certain virtues, raises himself up into the audacity of his own **presumption** <term>praesumptio; the dangerous overconfidence that one's goodness comes from oneself rather than God</term>.

But by a wondrous arrangement of divine mercy, something is set before his eyes into which he may fall; and while through his own fault he seeks after something, he desires nothing other than his own ruin in a pit. He falls into this pit by his own will, but he is not able to rise by his own strength. When he considers that he is nothing by himself, he learns without a doubt whose help he must seek.

Nevertheless, heavenly mercy carries him out of the pit as if he were a captive, because, once his weakness is recognized, it leads him back to forgiveness. Therefore, in the manner of a lioness, he returns to the higher realms in a cage on account of his ruin; for he who was puffed up by his own virtue, having fallen into earthly desires, is now bound fast in **humility** <term>humilitas; the foundational Christian virtue of acknowledging one's total dependence on God</term>. 

For because he perishes after that very presumption, it is brought about by a wondrous mercy that he now lives "enclosed" by the knowledge of his own weakness. Because the **holy man** <note>Job, the central figure of the biblical book being commented upon.</note> sees that this frequently happens to people, he speaks not for himself but on behalf of others, so that when we recognize his weeping, we may learn what things in ourselves we ought to weep for.

**The Tiger** <note>original: "Tygris"</note>

It then introduces the section on the Tiger, accompanied by an illustration of a common medieval myth where hunters distract a tigress with mirrors to steal her cubs.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a moral commentary by Gregory the Great on the nature of pride and humility using the metaphor of a captured lioness. This page shifts from that allegorical commentary into a Bestiary—a popular medieval encyclopedia of animals that combines natural history with moral lessons. We move from the Lion to the Tiger and begin the entry for the Panther.</meta>

### The Tiger

The **Tiger** <note>original: *Tigris*</note> is so named because of its swift flight, for that is what the Persians, Greeks, and Medes call an **arrow** <note>The text notes that in these ancient languages, the word for tiger and arrow shared a root, symbolizing speed.</note>. For it is a beast marked by various spots, possessing wondrous strength and speed. From its name, the **Tigris River** is called so because it is the swiftest of all rivers. **Hyrcania** <term>Hyrcania; a region southeast of the Caspian Sea in modern-day Iran and Turkmenistan, famously associated with wild tigers in antiquity</term> produces them most.

Now, when the tiger finds its lair empty of its stolen offspring, it immediately follows the tracks of the thief. But the thief, although carried on a horse for flight, seeing that he is almost overtaken by the beast's speed and that no means of escape remains, attempts a trick of this kind: 

When he sees the beast is close, he throws down a **sphere made of glass** <note>This "glass sphere" trick was a common legend in medieval bestiaries; it reflects the idea of being distracted by one's own vanity or reflection.</note>. But the mother is mocked by her own image and thinks it is her cub. She checks her pace, desiring to pick it up. Again, held back by the empty appearance, she throws herself with all her strength to catch the rider, and urged by the sting of anger, she threatens the fleeing man more swiftly. Again, he delays the pursuer by throwing a sphere; however, the mother’s devotion shuts out the memory of the trick. 

She turns the hollow image over and over, and sits down as if to suckle her young. And so, deceived by the zeal of her own motherly devotion, she loses both her revenge and her offspring.

**The Panther**

<margin>Nor does the panther wish to be tamed</margin>

<image-desc>A framed rectangular illustration depicts a panther and other animals against a blue background. On the left, a large, multicolored feline (representing a panther) stands on all fours facing right with its mouth open. On the right, a smaller reddish animal stands below a cluster of heads representing various prey animals, including a ram and a stag. At the base of the scene, a serpent or dragon with a curled tail crawls away to the right. The entire scene is enclosed in a double-lined border of red and blue.</image-desc>

<meta>Faint pencil notation below the image is illegible.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the tiger, focusing on the etymology of its name and a legendary tale of how hunters steal tiger cubs by distracting the mother with glass mirrors. It concludes by beginning the entry for the panther, accompanied by a vibrant illustration.</summary>

<keywords>tiger, bestiary, glass sphere, Hyrcania, Tigris River, panther, medieval folklore, motherly devotion</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>The previous page completed the description of the Tiger and the trick of the glass sphere used to steal its cubs. This page begins the entry for the Panther, continuing the tradition of the medieval Bestiary by moving from the fierce Tiger to the beautiful and Christ-like Panther.</meta>

There is an animal which is called the **Panther** <note>original: *pantera*</note>, having a varied color; and it is exceedingly beautiful and very gentle. The **Physiologus** <term>Physiologus; an ancient text that served as the primary source for medieval bestiaries, interpreting animal characteristics as Christian allegories</term> says of it that it has only the **dragon** as an enemy. When, therefore, it has eaten and is satisfied, it hides itself in its cave and sleeps. 

But after three days, it rises from sleep. And it emits a great roar, and from its mouth there comes a most sweet scent, like that of all spices. Moreover, when all the other animals hear its voice, they follow it wherever it goes because of the sweetness of its scent. But the dragon alone, hearing its voice, is terrified and flees into the caverns of the earth. There, unable to bear the scent, it becomes numb within itself and remains motionless as if dead. 

In this same way, our Lord Jesus Christ, the true Panther, descending from the heavens, rescued us from the power of the devil. And through His **incarnation**, He joined us to Himself as sons; He took all things, and leading captivity captive, He gave gifts to men <note>A reference to Psalm 68:18 and Ephesians 4:8, interpreting Christ’s victory over death.</note>. 

That it is a "varied" animal signifies what was said through **Solomon** concerning Christ, who is the wisdom of God the Father: a spirit of understanding, unique, manifold, true, sweet, open, merciful, firm, stable, secure, all-powerful, and all-seeing <note>This list of virtues reflects the Wisdom of Solomon 7:22-23.</note>. 

Because it is a beautiful animal, **David** says of Christ: "Beautiful in form beyond the sons of men" <note>Psalm 45:2.</note>. **Isaiah** also speaks of it as a gentle animal: "Rejoice and be glad, daughter of Zion; preach, daughter of Jerusalem, for your King comes to you gentle" <note>The text attributes this to Isaiah, though it traditionally combines Zechariah 9:9 and Matthew 21:5.</note>. 

When He was satisfied, He hid Himself amidst the mockeries of the Jews <note>original: *iudaicorum*; the text reflects the common medieval trope of blaming the Jewish people for the Passion</note>, the whips, the blows, the insults, the outrages, the spitting; suspended on the cross by hands, fixed with nails, given gall and vinegar to drink, and pierced by the lance, He fell asleep and rested in the tomb, and descended into Hell. There He bound the **great dragon**. But on the third day, He rose from sleep and emitted a great cry, fragrant with sweetness, just as David says: "The Lord was awakened as if from sleep, like a mighty man surfeited with wine" <note>Psalm 78:65.</note>. And He cried out...

<summary>This page describes the Panther as a Christ-figure, whose sweet breath draws all animals to it while terrifying the dragon (the devil). It provides an allegorical interpretation of Christ’s beauty, gentleness, Passion, and Resurrection using the Panther's three-day sleep and sweet roar.</summary>

<keywords>Panther, Christ allegory, Physiologus, Dragon, Resurrection, Passion of Christ, Solomon, David, Isaiah, medieval bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegory of the Panther. The previous page established the Panther as a Christ-figure who attracts all animals (the faithful) with its sweet breath after rising from a three-day sleep, while the dragon (the devil) flees. This page explores the scriptural parallels of this attraction and then shifts to the biological "facts" about the panther's reproduction as described by classical authorities.</meta>

...with a loud voice, so that **"his sound went out into all the earth, and his words unto the ends of the world."** <note>original: *uoce magna ita ut audiret in omni terra sonus eius & in fines orbis terre uerba eius*; a citation of Psalm 19:4, often applied to the Apostles or Christ.</note> And just as a sweet scent issues from the mouth of the panther, and all the beasts that are near and those that are far come following it, so the Jews—who once had the sense of beasts and were "near" through the Law—and those who were "far off"—that is, the Gentiles <term>Gentiles; non-Jewish people, who in medieval theology were seen as being brought into the faith from "afar"</term> who were without the Law—hearing the voice of Christ follow Him, saying with the prophet: **"How sweet are your words to my throat, sweeter than honey and the honeycomb to my mouth!"** <note>original: *Quam dulcia faucibus meis eloquia tua super mel & fauum ori meo*; Psalm 119:103.</note>

Likewise concerning the same: **"Grace is poured into your lips; therefore God has blessed you forever."** <note>Psalm 45:2.</note> And **Solomon** says: **"The fragrance of your perfumes is above all spices."** <note>Song of Solomon 4:10.</note> Likewise: **"We will run after the scent of your perfumes."** <note>Song of Solomon 1:3.</note> And a little later: **"The king has brought me into his chamber."** <note>Song of Solomon 1:4.</note> It behoves us, like the **young maidens** <note>original: *adolescentulas*</note>—that is, souls renewed by baptism—to run by the perfume of Christ’s commandments and to migrate from earthly things to heavenly ones, so that the King may lead us into His palace in Jerusalem, the city of the Lord of the righteous, and into the mountain of all the saints.

The **Panther** is a beast covered with small circles, so that it is distinguished by a variety of black or white spots within tawny, eye-like circles. This animal gives birth only once in its life; the reason for this is clear. For when the cubs have grown in the mother’s womb and possess mature strength for birth, they hate any delay. Therefore, as the womb is burdened with the offspring, they tear at it with their claws as if it were an obstacle to their birth. She pours out the birth—or rather, loses it—driven by the pain. Thus, because the reproductive organs are afterwards damaged and scarred <note>original: *acariticosis*, likely a corruption of *cicatricosis* (scarred)</note>, any seed infused there cannot take hold, but instead falls back out, useless. For **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author whose "Natural History" provided the "scientific" basis for many medieval animal descriptions.</note> says that animals with sharp claws cannot give birth frequently, because they are injured internally by the cubs moving within them.

<summary>This page concludes the Christological allegory of the Panther's sweet breath as a call to all nations and begins a natural history description of the panther's unique physical appearance and its supposedly singular, violent birth process.</summary>
<keywords>Panther, Christ allegory, Gentiles, Solomon, baptism, Pliny the Elder, medieval embryology, Jerusalem</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the Panther as a symbol of Christ's sweetness and grace. It now moves to a new entry, the Antelope (Autalops), which serves as a cautionary tale about the dangers of worldly distractions and the importance of using Holy Scripture as a defense against vice.</meta>

# The Antelope //

<image-desc>A framed manuscript miniature within a red and blue border. It depicts an antelope (the Autalops) with long, serrated horns that have become entangled in the branches of a green shrub. To the left, a hunter wearing a grey tunic and red hose approaches, raising a large broad-axe with both hands. He also appears to carry a curved horn or tool in his left hand. The background consists of horizontal bands of blue and pale beige. The animal has red streaks on its flank, possibly representing blood or stylized fur patterns.</image-desc>

There is an animal called the **Antelope** <note>original: *autalops*</note> that is exceedingly fierce, so much so that no hunter can approach it. It has long horns shaped like a saw <note>original: *serre figam*</note>, so that it can cut down high and great trees and cast them to the ground. When it becomes thirsty, it goes to the great river **Euphrates**. Now, there is a shrub there which is called in Greek *heriane* <note>original: *heriane*; likely a reference to a heath or a bramble bush</note>, having slender and long branches. The antelope begins to play with its horns in the *heriane*, and while it plays, it entangles its horns in the branches. Hearing its voice, the hunter comes and kills it.

So you also, O man, who strives to be sober and chaste and to live spiritually—your two horns are the **two Testaments** <term>Testaments; the Old and New Testaments of the Bible, which the medieval mind saw as the primary weapons against sin</term>, through which you can cut down and uproot all bodily and spiritual vices. Beware of drunkenness, lest you be bound by lust <note>original: *luxuria*; in this context, it refers to lechery or excessive physical indulgence</note> and pleasure, and be killed by the devil. For **"wine and women make men turn away from God."** <note>original: *Vinum enim & mulieres apostatare faciunt homines a deo*; a citation from the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 19:2.</note>

## The Leopard. <note>original: *Pardus*</note>

<summary>This page describes the Antelope, a fierce creature whose serrated horns represent the Old and New Testaments, warning that even the strongest can be trapped by worldly pleasures and destroyed by the devil.</summary>
<keywords>Antelope, Autalops, Euphrates, Two Testaments, Allegory, Hunter, Temperance, Vice</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page follows the description of the Antelope, which concluded with a warning against the entrapments of worldliness. It now introduces the Pard and the Leopard, exploring the medieval understanding of hybrid animals and their complex spiritual symbolism, before transitioning to the Unicorn at the bottom of the page.</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature framed in red and orange with a blue background. It depicts a leopard (pard) with a yellowish-tan body and dark spots. The creature has a fierce, slightly human-like face with bared teeth and a protruding red tongue. Its long tail curves upward and over its back, ending in a brush-like tuft. The beast is shown in profile, walking toward the right with sharp claws extended.</image-desc>

<margin>The Pard and the lion</margin>
**T**he Pard <note>original: *Pardus*; in medieval zoology, the "pard" was often considered a distinct species of spotted great cat</note> is a spotted and very swift kind of animal, headlong in its pursuit of blood; for it rushes toward death with a single leap. The Leopard is born from the adultery <margin>Pliny</margin> of a lioness and a pard, which creates a third kind of origin, just as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author whose *Naturalis Historia* was a primary source for medieval animal lore</note> says in his Natural History. He states that when a lion mates with a female pard, or a pard with a lioness, a degenerate offspring is produced from both, just as the mule and the hinny <term>hinny; original: *burdo*; the offspring of a stallion and a female donkey, considered a "debased" hybrid like the leopard</term> are born. In a mystical sense, the Pard signifies the devil, who is full of many diversities, or the sinner who is sprinkled with the various stains of crimes and diverse errors. Whence the prophet says: "The Ethiopian shall not change his skin, nor the leopard his spots" <note>original: *ethiops non mutabit pelle & pardus uarietate suam*; a reference to Jeremiah 13:23</note>. Likewise, the pard represents the malice of the Antichrist, being sprinkled with variety; as seen in the Apocalypse: "And the beast which rose out of the sea was like a leopard" <note>original: *bestia que ascendebat de mari simil pardo*; referring to Revelation 13:2</note>. Of these, it is said that they persevere in the blackness of their sins and the variety of their errors. Elsewhere, however, it is written: "The wolf shall dwell with the lamb, and the leopard shall lie down with the kid" <note>original: *habitabit lupus cum agno. & pardus cu hedo cubabit*; from Isaiah 11:6</note>. This was fulfilled at the coming of Christ; for those who were previously fierce now lead a life alongside the innocent, and those who were once polluted by the stains of errors, through performing penance, are converted to the truth of the faith. **The Unicorn.**

<summary>This page describes the leopard as a hybrid offspring of a lion and a pard, using its spotted skin to symbolize the variety of human sin and the deceit of the Antichrist, while also offering hope for redemption through Christ.</summary>
<keywords>leopard, pard, Pliny the Elder, Antichrist, symbolism of spots, redemption, prophecy, Jeremiah, Isaiah, Unicorn</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page follows the description of the Pard and the Leopard, concluding the section on spotted beasts and transitioning into the famous allegory of the Unicorn. It continues the bestiary's pattern of moving from physical descriptions to spiritual interpretations of the natural world.</meta>

<margin><unclear>The Unicorn</unclear></margin>

**T**he Unicorn, which is also called the *monoceros* <note>original: *monoceros*; derived from the Greek *monos* (single) and *keras* (horn)</note> by the Greeks, has this nature: it is a small animal resembling a young goat <note>original: *hedo*; in medieval tradition, the unicorn was often depicted as goat-sized rather than horse-sized, emphasizing the paradox of its great strength in a small body</note>, and it is exceedingly fierce. It has a single horn in the middle of its head, and no hunter is able to catch it. 

But it is captured by this method: a virgin girl is led to where it lives and is left there in the woods alone. As soon as the animal sees her, it leaps into her lap and embraces her, and in this way it is caught.

In this same way, our Lord Jesus Christ is spiritually a "unicorn," of whom it is said: "And the beloved is like the son of unicorns" <note>original: *& dilectus quēadmodū filius unicorniū*; a reference to Psalm 28:6 (Psalm 29 in modern bibles)</note>. And in another psalm: "My horn shall be exalted like that of the unicorn" <note>original: *Exaltabit̄ sicut unicornis cornu meum*; a reference to Psalm 91:11 (Psalm 92 in modern bibles)</note>. And Zacharias <note>the father of John the Baptist, whose song (the Benedictus) is recorded in the Gospel of Luke</note> said: "He has raised up a horn of salvation for us in the house of Da[vid]" <note>original: *Suscitavit nobis cornu salutis in domo da...*; a reference to Luke 1:69. The text here breaks off at the beginning of the name "David."</note>

<summary>This page describes the unicorn's fierce nature and the legendary method of its capture by a virgin, serving as a Christological allegory for the Incarnation. It supports this symbolism with several scriptural references to the "horn" as a sign of power and salvation.</summary>
<keywords>Unicorn, Monoceros, Christology, Allegory, Virgin, Bestiary, Psalm 28, Psalm 91, Luke 1, Horn of Salvation</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the spiritual interpretation of the unicorn, completing the allegory of Christ’s incarnation and victory over death, before transitioning to the description of the Lynx and its legendary gemstone.</meta>

[Da]vid <note>original: *uid*; completing the word "David" from the previous page</note> his servant. <note>original: *pueri sui*; a reference to Luke 1:69, "...in the house of his servant David."</note> Furthermore, the fact that it has one horn on its head signifies what he himself said: **"I and the Father are one,"** <note>John 10:30</note> and according to the Apostle: **"The head of Christ is God."** <note>1 Corinthians 11:3</note> It is called "exceedingly fierce" because neither the principalities, nor the powers, nor the thrones, nor the dominations <note>original: *principatus... potestates... throni... dominationes*; these are the traditional ranks or "choirs" of angels in the celestial hierarchy</note> were able to understand him; nor could hell hold him; nor was the most subtle devil able to understand or track him. Rather, by the sole will of the Father, he descended into the womb of the Virgin for our salvation. It is called a "small animal" because of the humility of his <term>Incarnation</term> <note>the Christian doctrine of God becoming human in the form of Jesus</note>, as he himself said: **"Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart."** <note>Matthew 11:29</note> The unicorn is like a young goat <note>original: *hedo*; a kid</note> because the Savior himself was made in the likeness of sinful flesh, and by sin he condemned sin. The unicorn often has a battle with elephants, and it strikes them down by wounding them in the belly.

***

### The Lynx

The **Lynx** <note>original: *Lynx*</note> is so called because it is counted among the genus of wolves. It is a beast with its back distinguished by spots like a leopard, but it is similar to a wolf. It is said that the urine of this animal hardens into the toughness of a precious stone, which is called <term>Ligurium</term> <note>also known as *lyncurium* or "lynx-stone," a legendary gemstone often identified with amber or jacinth in medieval lapidaries</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the Christological allegory of the unicorn, emphasizing Christ's humility and divinity, and introduces the lynx, noted for its spotted coat and the legendary stone formed from its urine.</summary>
<keywords>Unicorn, Jesus Christ, Incarnation, Celestial Hierarchy, Elephant, Lynx, Ligurium, Ligure, Animal Allegory</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on the Lynx, specifically the legendary properties of its urine (the gemstone lyngurium) and the moral allegory of the animal's secretive nature, before moving into the description of the Griffin.</meta>

...is so named. <note>original: *appellatur*; completing the sentence from the previous page describing the "lynx-stone" or *lyngurium*, a gemstone believed to form from the animal's urine.</note> That the lynxes themselves are aware of this property is proven by this evidence: for they cover the liquid they discharge in the sand as much as they can, out of a certain natural spite, so that such an excretion might not be put to human use. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of *Natural History*</note> says that by their nature, lynxes do not allow a companion with them. This beast represents the type of envious and deceitful people who desire to harm rather than to help; intent on earthly desires, they enviously hoard those things which are superfluous to themselves but could have been of use to others.

## Griffins

<image-desc>A manuscript illumination of a griffin (Gripes) contained within a double-lined frame of red and orange. The griffin is a hybrid creature with the front part of an eagle (dark grey feathers, hooked beak, and large spread wings) and the hindquarters of a lion (reddish-brown fur and a long tail). It is depicted in profile, lunging forward to bite the back of a grey horse-like animal. The horse is pinned to the ground on its back with its legs kicking upward in a struggle. The background inside the frame is split between a deep blue at the top and a faded pink/tan at the bottom.</image-desc>

<margin>
<unclear>Griffin</unclear>
This is the griffin
</margin>

It is called a **Griffin** <note>original: *Gripes*</note> because it is a winged and four-footed animal. This kind of wild beast is born in <term>Hyperborean</term> <note>from the Greek for "beyond the north wind," referring to a mythical land in the extreme north</note> places or mountains. In every part of its body it is like a lion, but in its wings and face it is similar to an eagle. It is violently hostile to horses, and it will even tear apart any humans it sees.

## Elephants

<image-desc>A large, colorful illumination within a red and gold decorative frame depicts a war elephant. The elephant is painted in a muted green-grey tone, with white tusks and a long, curved trunk. It is harnessed with a series of red leather straps and buckles. On its back sits a tall, rectangular wooden tower (howdah) with a lattice framework. Inside this tower, five armored soldiers are visible, wearing helmets and chainmail; they hold shields with heraldic designs (including chevrons and stripes) and brandish weapons such as axes and swords. To the right of the elephant, four additional figures are shown: an archer drawing a bow, and three others armed with spears and axes. A stylized pink and gold floral vine grows upward on the left side of the tower. The background within the frame is split between blue and red fields. To the right of the illustration, there are faint, illegible cursive pen trials.</image-desc>

There is an animal called the elephant, in which there is no desire for mating. <note>Medieval bestiaries often claimed elephants were remarkably chaste, only mating to produce offspring after consuming mandrake root.</note> The Greeks believe the elephant is named for the magnitude of its body, because it bears the form of a mountain. For in Greek, a mountain is called *elephio* <note>original: *elephio*; this is a medieval etymological error, likely confusing the animal's name with the Greek word for mountain, *lofos* or *lepos*, or perhaps an invented connection to emphasize its size.</note>. Among the Indians, however, it is called *barro* <note>original: *barro*; a term sometimes used in Latin for an elephant, thought to be of Indian or Iberian origin.</note> because of its voice. From this comes the name of its cry, the **barritus** <note>trumpeting</note>, and its teeth, which are called **ebur** <note>ivory</note>. The snout is called a **promuscida** <note>original: *p̄muscida*; a trunk</note> because it is used to move fodder to the mouth, and it is similar to a snake. It is protected by an ivory wall <note>A poetic reference to the tusks as a defensive rampart.</note>. No

<meta>This page continues the description of the elephant, moving from its naming to its behavior in war, its legendary biology, and its allegorical significance in Christian teaching.</meta>

<warning>The top portion of the page contains several dark stains and faded areas that may have originally contained an illustration or additional text, now largely illegible.</warning>

<image-desc>A large, darkened, and rectangular faded area at the top of the page suggests a missing or damaged illustration, common in Medieval Bestiaries where this text originates. Below this, there is a single block of text written in a Protogothic minuscule script.</image-desc>

...the animal's [cry]. <note>The text here is fragmentary; it likely refers to the term *barritus*, the Latin word for an elephant's trumpeting, which was mentioned on the previous page.</note> In them, indeed, the Persians and Medes place wooden towers, and they fight with javelins as if from a wall. They are powerful in much intellect and memory. They walk in herds and flee from the mouse. They mate back-to-back. They are pregnant for two years, and they do not give birth more than once; they produce no more than a single offspring. They live, however, for three hundred years.

If an elephant wishes to have children, it goes to the East near <term>Paradise</term> <note>In medieval maps and theology, the Garden of Eden was a physical location at the furthest eastern edge of the world.</note>, and there is a tree there called the **mandrake**. It goes there with its female, who first takes from the tree and gives it to her mate. She seduces him until he eats, and immediately she conceives in her womb. When the time of birthing comes, she goes out into a pond until the water reaches the mother's breasts. The [male] elephant guards her while she gives birth because the <term>dragon</term> <note>In bestiaries, the dragon is the natural enemy of the elephant, often depicted as a giant serpent that tries to suffocate the elephant or drink its cool blood.</note> is the elephant's enemy; if he finds a serpent, he kills it by trampling it until it dies.

For the elephant is formidable to bulls, yet it fears the mouse. This is its nature: if it falls, it cannot get back up. It falls when it leans against a tree to sleep, for it has no <term>joints in its knees</term> <note>original: *genualium*. A common medieval misconception held that elephants had no joints and had to sleep leaning against trees.</note>. A hunter, however, cuts the tree slightly so that when the elephant leans against it, both the elephant and the tree fall together. As it falls, it cries out loudly; immediately a large elephant comes, but it cannot lift him. Then they both cry out and twelve elephants come, yet they still cannot lift the one who fell. Finally, they all cry out, and immediately a "small" elephant <note>This "small elephant" is a Christ-figure in medieval allegory, representing how the Law (the twelve elephants) failed to save humanity, but the humble Christ succeeded.</note> comes and places its mouth with its <term>trunk</term> <note>original: *promuscida*; a Latinization of the Greek word for trunk.</note> under the large elephant and lifts it up. This small elephant has this nature: where its hair and bones have been burned, no evil thing can happen, nor can a dragon enter. This is a great mystery; the elephant and his mate represent the personas of **Adam** and **Eve**. Because when...

->.I.<-

<summary>This page describes the medieval legends of the elephant, including its use in war, its lack of knee joints, and the allegorical story of the Mandrake and the "small elephant" which mirrors the Fall and Redemption of man.</summary>
<keywords>elephant, mandrake, dragon, Adam and Eve, knee joints, medieval allegory, war towers, small elephant</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page concludes the allegory of the elephant, which serves as a symbolic retelling of the Fall of Man and the Redemption by Christ. It picks up from the previous page's description of the female elephant giving birth in a pond to protect her young from the dragon.</meta>

As long as they were in the flesh pleasing to God before their transgression, they did not know sexual intercourse, nor did they have the knowledge of sin. When, however, the woman ate from the tree—this is the spiritual <term>mandrake</term> <note>The mandrake was often associated with fertility and, in this allegory, represents the forbidden fruit of the Garden of Eden.</note>—she gave it to her husband. Then she became pregnant, and because of this, they were cast out of **Paradise**. For as long as he was in **Paradise**, Adam did not "know" <note>A common biblical euphemism for sexual intercourse.</note> his wife.

And conceiving, she gave birth upon the insurmountable waters, of which the prophet says: *Save me, O God, for the waters have entered even unto my soul.* <note>original: "Salvu̅ me fac d̄s qm̅ i̅traveru̅t aque usq; ad ani̅a̅ mea̅." This is a quote from Psalm 69:1 (Psalm 68 in the Vulgate).</note> And immediately the **dragon** follows them and made them strangers from their stronghold. This is what it means to not be pleasing to God.

Then came the "great elephant," which is the **Law**, and it did not lift him up <note>In bestiary lore, if an elephant falls, it cannot get up on its own because it has no joints in its knees; it represents fallen humanity.</note>, just as the priest [could not lift] the man who fell among thieves. These twelve elephants—that is, the choir of the **Prophets**—tried to raise him up, yet they could not lift that wounded man we mentioned. 

But the "spiritual elephant" raised him up—that is our Lord **Jesus Christ**. Although He was the greatest of all, He became the smallest; for He humbled Himself, becoming obedient even unto death, so that He might lift man up. He is the spiritual <term>Samaritan</term> who placed the man upon His own beast of burden. He, being unwounded Himself, took up our infirmities and bore our sins.

The name "Samaritan" is interpreted as "Guardian," of whom **David** says: *The Lord preserves the little ones.* <note>original: "Custodit parvulos dn̄s." From Psalm 116:6.</note> Where the Lord is present, the devil cannot approach.

Elephants, moreover, break whatever they wrap with their **trunk** <note>original: "muscada," likely a scribal variant of "promuscida."</note>; and with their foot, whatever they press down is crushed as if by a falling ruin amidst groans. They never fight over females, and they know no adultery. Finally, there is this good quality in them: if they happen to see a man wandering through the desert, they offer guidance until they reach known paths. Or, if they encounter crowded flocks, they make way for them with a very gentle hand <note>The "hand" of the elephant refers to its trunk.</note>, nor is the animal inhumane to anything it meets. If they happen to be in battle, they have no small care for the wounded; for they take the weary and the wounded into their midst to protect them.

<summary>This page concludes the elephant's entry by allegorizing its birth and fall as the story of Adam and Eve, identifying Christ as the "Small Elephant" (the Samaritan) who saves fallen humanity. It also describes the elephant's legendary virtues, including its chastity and its kindness toward lost travelers.</summary>

<keywords>Elephant, Mandrake, Adam and Eve, Fall of Man, Jesus Christ, Law and Prophets, Good Samaritan, Dragon, animal virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual interpretation of the elephant to a new chapter regarding the beaver. While the previous page focused on Christ's redemption of fallen humanity, this page uses the beaver as an allegory for personal holiness and the rejection of sin to escape the Devil.</meta>

X.

-> Castor <-

<image-desc>A circular miniature (tondo) with a thick red border illustrates the medieval legend of the beaver. On the left, two hunters stand; one wears a blue tunic and holds a sword and a spear. A third man in a pink tunic reaches toward the animals. In the foreground, a large grey-brown beaver with a broad, scaly tail is depicted biting its own testicles—a behavior described in bestiaries as a defense mechanism to escape hunters. Above it, another beaver is shown from behind while a hunter holds the severed organs. The scene is set against a plain parchment background.</image-desc>

<margin>Here is the beaver</margin>

There is an animal called the **beaver** which is exceedingly gentle. Its testicles are very well-suited for medicine <note>The substance "castoreum," secreted by beavers, was highly prized in the Middle Ages for treating various ailments, including headaches and hysteria.</note>. Concerning this animal, the *Physiologus* <note>The *Physiologus* was a famous ancient and medieval text that provided descriptions of animals, birds, and stones, accompanied by moral and spiritual lessons.</note> says that when it realizes a hunter is pursuing it, it bites off its own testicles and throws them into the face of the hunter, and thus it escapes by fleeing. 

If, however, it should happen again that another hunter pursues it, the beaver stands up and shows its genitals to the hunter. When the hunter sees that the animal lacks testicles, he departs from it. 

In the same way, every person who lives according to the commandment of God and wishes to live **chastely** cuts away from himself all vices and all acts of impurity, and throws them away from himself into the face of the **devil**. Then the devil, seeing that the person has nothing of his own <note>That is, nothing belonging to the devil’s kingdom of sin.</note>, departs from that person in confusion. But that person lives in God and is not captured by the devil, who says: *I will pursue, and I will overtake them.* <note>original: "p̃sequi & comp̃hendam eos." This is a reference to the Song of Moses in Exodus 15:9, here personified as the boast of the devil.</note> 

The beaver (*Castor*) is so named from the act of castrating (*castrando*). <note>This is a classic medieval "false etymology" popular in bestiaries, attempting to link the name of the animal to its legendary behavior.</note>

<vocab>Castor, beaver, Physiologus, medicine, allegory, castration, hunters, devil</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the legendary behavior of the beaver, which was believed to castrate itself to escape hunters seeking its medicinal organs. The text uses this as a moral allegory for the Christian believer who must cast off vices to escape the pursuit of the devil.</summary>
<keywords>beaver, castor, chastity, Physiologus, allegory, spiritual warfare, medicine, hunters, devil</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual allegory of the beaver's self-mutilation to the physical and symbolic characteristics of the ibex, followed by the introduction of the hyena. It continues the theme of using animal behavior—real or mythical—to illustrate Christian virtues and the study of scripture.</meta>

### Ibex.

<image-desc>A rectangular framed illustration with a red and blue border depicting the hunting of an ibex. Inside a central red circle, an ibex is shown upside down, leaping or falling from a stylized green tree/mountain and landing on its large, curved horns. To the left of the circle, a hunter in a red tunic blows a horn while a smaller figure in white draws a bow. To the right of the circle, a third figure stands holding an axe. The background inside the circle features a blue swirling pattern representing rocks or sky.</image-desc>

There is an animal called the **ibex** <note>The Alpine Ibex (Capra ibex). In medieval lore, it was believed the ibex could survive great falls by landing on its massive horns, which acted as shock absorbers.</note> which has two horns. Their strength is so great that if the animal is cast down from a mountain peak to the depths below, its whole body is supported by these two horns and remains unharmed. 

This animal signifies **learned men** who, through the harmony of the two **Testaments** <note>The "two horns" are a common medieval metaphor for the Old and New Testaments of the Bible.</note>, are accustomed to moderate whatever adversity happens to them, as if by a kind of healthy balance. Supported, as it were, by these two horns, they sustain the good deeds they perform by the testimony of the Old Testament and the reading of the Gospel.

<margin>Hyena.</margin>

<image-desc>A rectangular framed illustration with a red and blue border depicting a hyena. The creature, rendered in reddish-brown with a mane, is shown scavenging from a stone tomb. It has its head inside an open sarcophagus and is dragging out a pale, shrouded corpse. The tomb is decorated with architectural details. To the right of the frame, a faint marginal note is visible.</image-desc>

<margin>This is the hyena</margin>

<note>original: "ibex, yena, duo cornua, testamentum, eruditos homines, sarcophagus"</note>
<note>The text for the hyena section typically begins by describing its ability to change sex or its habit of raiding graves, though this page ends with the introductory illustration and margin note.</note>

<summary>This page describes the ibex, using its legendary ability to land on its horns to symbolize how scholars rely on the Old and New Testaments to withstand life's hardships. It also introduces the hyena with an illustration of the animal scavenging from a tomb.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the bestiary's descriptions of animals as moral allegories. Following the ibex, which symbolized the Old and New Testaments, the text now focuses on the hyena—a creature of dual nature and deceptive habits—using it to critique spiritual inconstancy and "double-mindedness."</meta>

<margin>hyena</margin>
There is an animal called the **hyena** <note>The spotted hyena. Medieval bestiaries often combined real observations of its scavenging with myths from ancient writers like Pliny and Solinus.</note> that lives in the tombs of the dead and feeds on their corpses. Its nature is such that it is sometimes male and sometimes female, and for this reason, it is considered an **unclean animal**. Because its spine is rigid, it cannot bend in a continuous curve; it can only turn by moving its whole body around. Solinus <note>original: "Solum"; referring to Gaius Julius Solinus, a 3rd-century geographer whose work was a primary source for medieval animal lore.</note> relates many wonders about it: first, that it follows the stables of shepherds and circles houses by night, and by constant listening, it learns names so that it can imitate the human voice. In this way, it might fall upon a man lured out by its trickery at night. It also mimics human vomiting and, by such false sobbing, devours the dogs it has enticed. If by chance hunting dogs should touch its shadow while following it, they lose their voice and are unable to bark. This same hyena digs up those buried in tombs by breaking apart the bodies.

The children of **Israel** are compared to this beast, for they served the living God from the beginning, but later, devoted to riches and luxury, they worshipped idols. Therefore, the prophet compared the **synagogue** to an unclean animal, saying: *"My inheritance has become to me like a hyena’s den."* <note>Original: "Facta est mihi hereditas mea quasi spelunca hyene." A reference to Jeremiah 12:9.</note> Therefore, whoever among us serves luxury and greed is compared to this beast, since they are neither men nor women—that is, they are neither faithful nor faithless. These are undoubtedly those of whom Solomon <note>The text attributes this to Solomon, though it is actually from the Epistle of James 1:8.</note> says: *"A man of double mind is unstable in all his ways."* Of such people the Lord says: *"You cannot serve God and Mammon."* <note>Original: "Non potestis deo seruire & mammone." From Matthew 6:24.</note>

This beast holds a stone in its eyes called the **hyenia**; it is believed that if someone holds this stone under their tongue, they can predict the future. Truly, any animal that the hyena circles three times is unable to move. For this reason, people have declared that it possesses magical knowledge. In parts of **Ethiopia**, it mates with a lioness, from which a monster is born called the **corocotta** <note>original: "corocote"; a mythical creature said to inhabit India or Ethiopia, described as having the ability to mimic human speech.</note>. It also strives to imitate human voices. These creatures never change their position while eating, nor do they change what they eat. In its mouth, there are no gums. It has one continuous tooth <note>Medieval writers believed the hyena/corocotta had a single, solid bone instead of individual teeth.</note> which, so that it is never blunted, closes just like a case.

## Bonnacon.
<insert>here is the bonnacon</insert>

<summary>This page describes the hyena as a rigid, deceptive, and dual-natured scavenger, using its mythical traits to symbolize the "double-minded" person and the spiritual history of Israel. It also introduces the corocotta, a hybrid monster born of a hyena and a lioness.</summary>

<keywords>hyena, corocotta, Israel, Solomon, synagogue, Ethiopia, Mammon, magic stone, animal allegory, bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the hyena. The text likely concluded that "whoever among us serves luxury and greed is compared to this beast," finishing the moral allegory before transitioning to the descriptions of the bonnacon and the monkey.</meta>

.15.

<image-desc>A miniature painting set within a lobed, quatrefoil frame. On the right is a Bonnacon, a legendary creature with a reddish-brown body, a horse-like mane, and horns that curve inward toward its head. The beast is fleeing to the right while ejecting a large stream of orange and red flame-like excrement from its hindquarters. On the left, three hunters in tunics and hose pursue the animal; the lead hunter drives a long spear into the creature's rear. The background features two large overlapping pale yellow circles.</image-desc>

In **Asia** an animal is born which they call the **bonnacon** <note>original: "bonnacon"; a legendary creature first described by Pliny the Elder. It was famous in medieval bestiaries for its unique, if somewhat unrefined, defense mechanism.</note>. It has the head of a bull, and the rest of its body is covered with a mane like that of a horse. Its horns, however, curve back upon themselves in such a many-folded way <note>original: "multiplici ritu"</note> that if anyone should run into them, they would not be wounded. But whatever protection its forehead denies to that monster, its belly provides. For when it turns to flee, it discharges a vast amount of waste from its belly across a distance of three acres <note>original: "iugerū"; a Roman unit of area roughly equal to 0.6 acres. Here it is used to suggest a massive distance.</note>, the heat of which burns whatever it touches. In this way, it drives away its pursuers with its dung.

->**Monkeys.**<- <note>original: "Simie"; in medieval Latin, this term covered a wide range of primates including apes, baboons, and monkeys.</note>

To the right, three monkeys (simiae) with brown fur and long limbs are clustered together, appearing to embrace or cling to one another. Stylized foliate branches with red and orange leaves sprout from the top and sides of the circular frame.</image-desc>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues from the description of the bonnacon, a beast that defends itself with burning dung. It transitions here to the section on monkeys (Simiae), continuing the bestiary's encyclopedic survey of the animal kingdom and its moral applications.</meta>

<margin>Monkeys.</margin>

**Monkeys** <note>original: "Simie"; in medieval Latin, this term covered a wide range of primates including apes, baboons, and monkeys.</note> are so called in the Latin language because a great likeness to human reason is perceived in them. These creatures, being keen observers of the elements, rejoice at the new moon, but are saddened during the middle and waning <note>original: "caua"; literally "hollow," referring to the crescent or vanishing moon.</note> phases. The nature of the monkey is such that when she gives birth to twin cubs, she loves one and despises the other. If it should ever happen that she is pursued by hunters, she clasps the one she loves in her arms before her, and carries the one she hates hanging from her neck. But when she becomes exhausted from walking on two feet, she is forced to drop the favored one she was carrying, and instead continues to carry the unwanted one she hates <note>In medieval moralizing, the "loved" cub represents worldly pleasures that fail us in times of trouble, while the "hated" cub represents the weight of our sins which cling to us even when we are weary.</note>.

The monkey has no tail. In this, it represents the figure of the **Devil**, who has a head but indeed has no tail. And although the monkey is unsightly in its entirety, its hind parts are particularly foul and horrible. The Devil had a beginning <note>The "head."</note> when he was an angel in heaven. But he was a hypocrite and deceitful within, and so he lost his tail <note>In medieval symbolism, the "tail" represents the "end" or "conclusion" of a life; since the Devil's end is total destruction, he is said to have no tail.</note>, for he will perish entirely in the end, as the Apostle says: *Whom the Lord Jesus shall slay with the breath of his mouth.*

The name "Simia" is Greek in origin, meaning "with pressed-in nostrils" <note>original: "preſſis naribz"; from the Greek "simos."</note>. This is why they are called snub-nosed, for they have flattened nostrils and a foul face, hideously baggy with wrinkles—though it is also common for goats to have such a flattened nose. The **Cercopitheci** <note>original: "Cercopetia"; literally "tail-apes," referring to long-tailed monkeys.</note> do possess a tail; this is the only distinction between them and those previously mentioned. The **Cynocephali** <note>original: "Cenofali"; literally "dog-heads," often identifying baboons.</note> are also counted among the monkeys. They are very common in parts of **Ethiopia**. They are violent in their leaping and fierce in their bite. They are never truly tamed, remaining instead quite rabid. Among the monkeys there are also **Sphinxes**, which are shaggy of coat and docile enough to forget their wildness.

The monkey on the right is of a lighter, beige color, also upright, and holds a long staff topped with a yellow circular object. Both creatures have long tails and human-like faces.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of primates, specifically mentioning satyrs and callitriches, before beginning a major new entry on stags. The text follows the tradition of medieval bestiaries which blend natural history with legendary lore.</meta>

There are also those they call **satyrs** <note>original: "satyros"; in this context, the author refers to a species of ape believed to inhabit the wilderness, known for their human-like faces.</note> with a very pleasing face and restless, gesturing behavior. The **callitriches** <note>original: "Callitrices"; literally "beautiful hair," likely referring to marmosets or another long-haired monkey species.</note> differ from others in almost their entire appearance. They have a beard on their face and a broad tail. To capture them is not difficult, but to transport them [alive] is rare. These live in Meroe <note>original: "mahero"; an ancient city-state on the east bank of the Nile in modern-day Sudan.</note> which is in the Ethiopian climate, that is, their own native sky.

->**Stags**<-

<margin>Here is the Stag</margin>
<image-desc>A framed manuscript miniature depicting two stags. The stag on the left is bowed down, its tongue or breath drawing a long, coiled snake out of a hole in the ground. The second stag stands behind it, resting its chin on the first stag's rump, illustrating the communal behavior described in the text. The background features stylized white foliate patterns against a divided field of red and blue.</image-desc>

**Stags** <note>original: "Cerui"</note> are named from the Greek *apo tou keraton* <note>original: "potoy ceraton"</note>, that is, from their **horns**; for in the Greek language, horns are called *kerata*. These are the enemies of serpents. When they perceive themselves to be weighed down by infirmity, they draw snakes out of their holes with the breath of their nostrils. Having overcome the peril of the venom, they are restored by feeding upon them <note>This ancient legend suggests that eating snakes acted as a medicinal purge for deer, helping them shed their old skin or illness.</note>.

They discovered the herb **dittany** <note>original: "Ditannu[m]"; an aromatic plant from Crete famous in antiquity for its healing properties.</note> themselves; for by feeding on it, they shake off arrows they have received. They are distracted <note>original: "Mutatur"; literally "changed" or "diverted."</note> by the sound of pipes. With their ears upright, they hear sharply; when their ears are lowered, they hear nothing.

Stags also have this nature: through mutual affection, they change their pastures and support one another. If ever they cross immense rivers or seas, they place their heads upon the haunches of those going before them. By succeeding each other in turn, they feel no labor from the weight. And when they find places so that they do not wander...

<summary>This page describes various primates (satyrs and callitriches) and begins a detailed entry on stags, highlighting their legendary enmity with snakes and their cooperative swimming habits.</summary>
<keywords>satyrs, callitriches, Ethiopia, stags, serpents, dittany, animal cooperation, bestiary lore</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry on stags from page 195, moving from their physical description and the legend of their enmity with serpents to the moral allegories derived from their behavior and further details of their natural history.</meta>

they crouch down and leap across very quickly. They also have another nature: that after eating a serpent, they run to a spring, and by drinking from it, they shed their hair and all their old age <note>The bestiary tradition claims stags lived for hundreds of years by rejuvenating themselves this way.</note>. These natures seem to agree with the members of the **Holy Church** <note>original: "scē ecclie"; an abbreviation for "sanctae ecclesiae"</note> by a suitable and fitting reason. For while they exchange this homeland—that is, the world—for the love of the heavenly homeland, they place themselves in a line <note>original: "incarie"; likely a scribal variation of "in acie" or "in serie," referring to the behavior where stags swim in a single file, resting their heads on the rump of the one in front to share the burden of the journey.</note>; that is, the more perfect members promote and sustain those who are less perfect by their example and good works. And if they find a place of sinning, they leap across it immediately. And after the "incorporation of the devil"—that is, through sins committed—they run to **Christ** <note>original: "xpm"; abbreviation for "Christum"</note>, who is the true spring, through confession; and by drawing in His precepts, they are renewed, having laid aside the old age of sin.

The males of this kind of animal are incited by the season of Venus <note>original: "tempus uenere-"; referring to the mating season.</note>; they rage with the madness of lust. As for the females, although they may conceive earlier, it is better if they conceive before the star of **Arcturus** <note>Arcturus is a bright star in the constellation Boötes; its rising was often used by ancient writers to mark seasonal changes.</note>. These mothers bring up their offspring with whatever care they can: just as they hide them with tender devotion, having hidden them deep within shrubs or grasses, they discipline them with a blow of the foot <note>original: "pedū ubere"; likely a corruption of "pedis ictu." Traditional bestiaries describe the mother stag kicking the fawn to make it lie still and stay hidden.</note> to keep them concealed. When their strength has matured for flight, they teach them to run through exercise, and they become accustomed to jumping over steep places.

When they are caught by the barking of dogs, they are directed by a favorable wind so that their scent recedes with them. They raise their ears, and because of this, they offer themselves as targets for the javelins of archers. Regarding their horns, the one that was on the right side is more effective for medicine. If one desires to put snakes to flight, he should burn the horn <note>The smell of burning stag horn was anciently believed to repel venomous snakes.</note>. Their teeth reveal their old age when either few or none are found. To discern their longevity, **Alexander the Great** <note>original: "alexander magn-"</note> placed collars on many stags, which, when captured after a hundred years, did not yet show any sign of old age.

**Fawns** <note>original: "Hinnuli"</note> are the children of stags, named from "beckoning" <note>original: "ab innuendo"; a common medieval etymology linking the name of the fawn to the mother's "nod" or "signal."</note>, because they hide at the mother's nod. This animal is very effective against poisons. And the rennet <note>original: "coagulū"; the curdled milk found in the stomach of a nursing animal, often used in medieval medicine.</note> of a fawn, taken from its mother's womb, prevents those who eat it from ever suffering from a fever. For which reason, a remedy made from me...
