--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical and medicinal discussion of the stag (cervus), transitioning from the physical habits mentioned on the previous page to the animal's symbolic meaning in Christian theology and its mentions in Scripture.</meta>

ointments made from them soothe the fevers of ailing men. We read that many who were accustomed to eat venison in the mornings lived to a great age without fevers; however, this benefit is lost if they are killed by a single wound.

The **Stag** <note>original: "Ceruuus"</note> represents holy men who desire **God**, just as the Prophet says: *As the stag longs for the springs of water, so my soul longs for you, O God.* <note>Psalm 42:1. In the medieval "Physiologus" tradition, the stag's thirst after eating a snake is a metaphor for the soul's need for baptism or repentance after sin.</note> For that animal is first in darkness <note>original: "in nigrum"; likely referring to a state of sin or ignorance before being renewed.</note>, then most swift, parched by a burning desire: it draws serpents out of their holes with its nostrils, and once it has swallowed them, burning with the seething venom, it hastens as fast as it can to a spring of water; for it loves sweet and pure water to be healed.

This beautiful comparison ardently stirs our own desire so that when we swallow the bitter poisons of the serpent—that is, the devil—and burn in our own throats, we should immediately hasten to the spring of divine mercy. This is so that whatever sin is contracted through adversity may be overcome by the quality of that most sweet drink.

Nor is it without reason that he said "to the springs of water" and not just "to water": for the spring of waters is **Christ the Lord**, from whom all things flow that restore us. For streams can often dry up, but a spring of water is always flowing; therefore, we must rightly hasten to the liquid of our sacred origin, where our desire can never go hungry.

Elsewhere it is also written: *The high mountains are for the stags.* <note>Psalm 104:18</note> Again, they represent the three **Patriarchs** <note>Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, the traditional ancestors of the Israelites.</note>; for in the **Song of Songs**, it is said of the **Savior**—who was born of their lineage according to the flesh: *Flee, my beloved, be like a gazelle or a young stag.* <note>Song of Solomon 8:14</note>

Stags also represent the **Apostles**; as in the Psalm: *The voice of the Lord preparing the stags.* <note>Psalm 29:9</note> The hind <note>The female deer.</note> represents the flesh of the Lord. In the writings of **Solomon**: *Let the most dear hind and most graceful fawn, her breasts, intoxicate you* <note>Proverbs 5:19</note>—that is, the doctrine of the Gospel. Stags are also the Apostles because they give birth spiritually, as seen in the book of **Job**: *Do you know the time when the mountain goats give birth? Or have you observed the hinds in labor?* <note>Job 39:1</note>

The **Stag** is, as we have said, a devourer of poisonous serpents, walking through thorny places and with the greatest agility through...

<summary>This page explores the stag as a multifaceted Christian symbol: a model for the soul seeking God, a representation of Christ and the Patriarchs, and a metaphor for the Apostles' spiritual labor.</summary>
<keywords>stag, venison, Christ, Psalm 42, spiritual renewal, serpent, Apostles, Song of Songs, holy mercy</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the allegorical interpretation of the stag (cervus) begun on the previous page, specifically focusing on its habitat in the mountains as a metaphor for the apostles and prophets. It then introduces the "Tragelaphus" (goat-stag) and the "Dammula" (doe), continuing the theme of animal symbolism in relation to Christ and the faithful.</meta>

...[mountain-dwellers] <note>The text likely completes a reference to Psalm 104:18: "The high hills are a refuge for the stags."</note>. It loves to dwell in the highest mountains; to this animal the faithful are worthily compared. They call out the devil when he is unable to come out to the praise and glory of the Lord; they convict him of his crimes, and they leap over the vices of this world like thorns through their good conduct. And they dwell in the mountains—that is, among the **Apostles** and **Prophets**—who, by their holy preaching, have deserved to be the solid peaks in this world.

### The Goat-Stag <note>original: "Tragelaphus"</note>

<image-desc>A framed illustration with a decorative red and blue rounded border. The scene depicts four deer-like animals on a reddish-brown landscape against a blue background. In the center is a large stag with branching antlers and a beard, labeled with a faint internal inscription "hic est tragelaphus". To its right, a deer-like animal (labeled "hic est dāma") is shown licking the back of a small fawn. Another smaller deer is shown above the main stag, and a small animal is curled up on the ground beneath the stag.</image-desc>

<margin>This is a wild goat <note>original: "hic est caprea"</note></margin>
The **Goat-Stag** <note>original: "Tragelafus"; from the Greek 'tragos' (goat) and 'elaphos' (stag).</note> is so named by the Greeks; while they are of the same species as stags, they nevertheless have shaggy shoulders like he-goats and their chins have hair like beards; they are known to exist nowhere else except around Phasis <note>The river Phasis in Colchis (modern Georgia), often associated in antiquity with exotic or mythical fauna.</note>. **Fawns** <note>original: "Hinnuli"</note> are the offspring of stags. The **Doe** <note>original: "Dammula"</note> is so named because she escapes from the hand <note>original: "quod de manu effugiat". A medieval folk etymology linking 'dama' to 'de manu' (from the hand).</note>. She is a timid and non-combative animal, of whom the poet **Martial** <note>Marcus Valerius Martialis, a 1st-century Roman poet.</note> says: *Let the boar fear the tusk; horns defend the stag; we are non-combative does, what are we but prey?*

These animals also signify either **Christ** or innocent men. Hence also the bride says of the bridegroom in the **Song of Songs**: *My beloved is like a doe or a young stag.* <note>Song of Solomon 2:9. This book of the Bible was frequently interpreted as an allegory for the love between Christ and the Church or the soul.</note> Therefore, **Christ** is likened to a doe in the assumption of the flesh and in His humility; but in the variety of His virtues and in His innocence, He is likened to a young stag—that of the patriarchs, of whom...

<summary>This page concludes the stag's symbolism as the Apostles and Prophets before describing the Goat-Stag and Doe, interpreting them as symbols of Christ’s humility and innocence.</summary>

# The Goat <note>original: "Caper"</note>

...drew its origin according to the flesh.

<image-desc>A framed miniature painting with a red border. Within the frame, two goats stand on their hind legs facing each other on either side of a central, stylized tree with red and green floral elements. A third goat is depicted on the left, bending its head low to the ground as if grazing or drinking. Behind the grazing goat is another stylized plant with a pinecone-shaped flower at the top. The background is divided between red and a pale parchment color.</image-desc>

There is an animal which in Latin is called the **goat** <note>original: "caper"</note> because it seeks out <note>original: "captet"</note> harsh and rugged places <note>original: "aspera"</note>. Some call it the **wild goat** <note>original: "caprea"</note> from the word for "plucking" <note>original: "carpendo"; referring to the way they browse or graze on vegetation</note>. These are the wild goats which the Greeks called *dorcas* <note>Greek: δορκάς, often used for gazelles or roe deer</note> because they see very sharply. They dwell in the high mountains and, from a great distance, they recognize those who are approaching, whether they are hunters or travelers.

In this same way, our **Lord Jesus Christ** loves the high mountains—that is, the **Prophets** and the **Apostles**—just as it is said in the *Song of Songs*: "Behold, my beloved <note>original: "fratruelis"; literally "brother's son," but used here for the Bridegroom</note> comes like a wild goat, leaping over the mountains, crossing the hills." And just as the wild goat grazes in the valleys, so our Lord grazes in the **Church**. The good works of Christians are his food, for he says: "I was hungry and you gave me to eat; I was thirsty and you gave me to drink" <note>Matthew 25:35</note>. The "valleys of the mountains" are understood to be the churches established throughout various places. So it is said in the *Song of Songs*: "Behold, my beloved has arrived; be like a wild goat and a young stag."

<summary>This page introduces the goat (caper), explaining its name through its habit of seeking rugged terrain and its keen eyesight. These traits are interpreted as symbols of Christ, who watches over the faithful from the "heights" of the Apostles and Prophets while finding sustenance in the good works of the Church.</summary>

<keywords>Goat, Caper, Dorcas, Wild Goat, Song of Songs, Christology, Allegory, Prophets, Apostles, Good Works</keywords>

...and of **stags** <note>original: "ceruorum"</note>. Because it has the sharpest eyesight and perceives all things, and recognizes them from afar, it signifies our **Lord**, who is "God, the Lord of all knowledge" <note>original: "Dominus scientiarum Deus est"; cf. 1 Samuel 2:3</note>. And as it says elsewhere: "For though the Lord is high, he looks upon the lowly, and knows the high things from afar" <note>original: "Quoniam excelsus Dominus et humilia respicit, et alta a longe cognoscat"; cf. Psalm 138:6</note>; He created and founded all things, and He rules, sees, and perceives them, and He foresees and knows before anything arises in our hearts. Finally, just as the **wild goat** <note>original: "caprea"</note> recognizes the approach of hunters from a distance, so **Christ** foreknew the treachery of his betrayer, saying: "Behold, he who betrays me is at hand" <note>Matthew 26:46</note>.

When wounded, it runs to the **dittany** <note>original: "dictannum"; a medicinal herb famous in antiquity and the Middle Ages for its supposed power to cause arrows to fall out of wounds and to heal them instantly</note>, and having touched it, the goat is healed. In this same way, **good preachers**, grazing upon the Law of the Lord and upon good works, find delight as if in a pasture. From the...

<image-desc>A manuscript illustration of a unicorn (monoceros) set within a circular red frame on a blue background, contained inside a larger square frame. The **unicorn** <note>original: "monoceros"</note> is a monster with a terrifying bellow, a horse's body, the feet of an elephant, and a tail very similar to a stag's. A horn protrudes from the middle of its forehead with a marvelous brightness, four feet in length, and so sharp that whatever it attacks is easily pierced by its blow. It never falls into the power of men alive; it can indeed be killed, but it cannot be captured.

<margin>Bear <note>original: "Ursus"</note></margin> The **Bear** is said to be so named because it fashions <note>original: "formet"</note> its offspring with its mouth, as if "beginning" <note>original: "orsus"; the author is making a linguistic connection between the Latin word for bear, "ursus," and the word for "beginning" or "weaving," "orsus," to explain the animal's nature</note> them. For they say that they give birth to unformed offspring, and a certain mass of flesh is born, which the mother, by licking, arranges into limbs. <note>This reflects a common medieval belief that bear cubs were born as formless lumps and were literally "licked into shape" by their mothers.</note> However, it is the prematurity of the birth that causes this. Ultimately, the bear gives birth on the thirtieth day; hence it happens that this hurried fertility brings forth something unformed. The head of bears is weak, but their greatest strength lies in their arms and loins; for which reason they sometimes stand upright. Their own industriousness in healing themselves does not... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the page concludes.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of the bear (Ursus) from the previous page. ...they protect themselves. If indeed they are affected by tree resin <gloss>gum</gloss> when they have been cut or injured by wounds, they know how to heal themselves. There is an herb whose name is *Phlomos* <note>original: "Eflomus"; the Greek name for the mullein plant, known for its medicinal properties</note>—as the Greeks call it—which they apply to their underlying sores so that they might cure them by touch alone. A sick bear devours ants. Bears that dwell in moist places are superior to others, provided they seek out deeper dens. For the offspring is also suited to the place where they were born.

They do not mate in the same manner as other four-legged animals; instead, they are joined together in mutual embraces like human couplings. They stir up the desire for love <note>original: "veneris"; referencing Venus, the goddess of love, used here to mean sexual urge</note> during the winter. Males respect pregnant females with a secret honor; and although they may be nurtured in the same place, they nevertheless separate themselves into individual sleeping quarters. June is the more favorable time for them.

Indeed, the thirtieth <gloss>30th</gloss> day frees the womb. From this it happens that such a hurried fertility creates unformed offspring. They bring forth very small pieces of flesh which are white in color and have no eyes. By licking <gloss>licking</gloss> these, they gradually give them shape and form <gloss>sense</gloss>, and they nurture them in a nest against their breasts so that, warmed by constant sitting upon them, they may draw the breath of life.

In those dens there is no food. Indeed, for the first fourteen <gloss>14</gloss> days, the males fall into such a deep sleep that they cannot be awakened even by wounds. They remain hidden for nearly <gloss>and finally</gloss> three months. Soon, having emerged into the open light of day, they suffer such an unaccustomed glare that they are thought to be struck with blindness.

They lie in wait for the beehives of bees; they especially crave the honeycombs. Nothing else is heard of them than that they are seeking honey. If they taste the fruit <note>literally "apples"</note> of the mandrake, they die; but they immediately take action so that the evil does not grow to a deadly strength, and they devour ants to recover their health. But when they attack bulls, they know which parts are most easily weakened; they seek nothing else than the horns or the nostrils, so that the pain may be sharper in those more tender places. According to allegory, the bear also sometimes signifies the devil.

### **Leucrota**

<image-desc>A rectangular framed miniature depicts a leucrota, a mythical beast from medieval bestiaries. The creature is shown in a running or leaping posture. It has a lean, pale greyish body, a thick striped mane, and a head resembling that of a horse or donkey with an extremely wide, grinning mouth that extends back to its ears, revealing a continuous ridge of teeth. The background consists of concentric rectangles in shades of blue, orange, and cream. The frame is drawn with simple dark ink lines.</image-desc>

In India, a beast is born by the name of the **Leucrota**. It surpasses all other wild animals in speed. In size, it is like a stag; it has the haunches, chest, and legs of a lion, but the head of a horse. It has a cloven hoof, and a mouth that gapes open as far back as its ears. In place of individual teeth, it has a single, continuous bone <note>original: "os esse perpetuo"; medieval bestiaries often described this as a single serrated bone that never dulls</note>. This creature even mimics the sounds of humans speaking.

A dog's diet is said to be very modest, because one who is in charge of others [should] stay awake for the study of wisdom and must avoid every kind of excess; for Sodom perished through the fullness of bread <note>A reference to Ezekiel 16:49, attributing the fall of Sodom to pride and "fullness of bread," or gluttony</note>. Finally, the enemy <note>The devil</note> possesses a man through no entrance so quickly as through a greedy throat.

That it leaves its meat in a river because of a deceptive shadow signifies foolish men who, because of ambition for an unknown thing, often leave behind that which is their own by right. From this it happens that while they are unable to obtain what they desire, they realize too late that they have lost in vain what they abandoned.

**Lycisci** <note>original: "Lycisci," a term used in medieval bestiaries for wolf-dog hybrids</note> are what dogs are called when they are born from wolves and dogs, when they happen to mate with each other. It is also customary for female dogs to be tied up in the woods at night so they may be joined by wild beasts, from which union very fierce dogs are born in the woods, so strong that they can strike down lions in their grip.

Therefore, whatever sinner wishes to please his [King] <note>original: "r̃ege" (rege); in this allegorical context, "the King" represents God or Christ</note> needs to seek out spiritual guides. These guides or spiritual envoys lead him with spiritual gifts toward his reconciliation before the King. When these guides and envoys are arranged with three spiritual gifts: the first envoy is the weeping of the heart; the second is true confession; the third is true penance. Their guides are the love of God, good will, and right action. The spiritual gifts are the purity of body and soul, pure prayer, and perseverance in good work. These envoys and guides, with spiritual gifts, thus proceed before the Trinity. Before God, they offer prayer preceded by weeping with the love of God. --- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the allegorical properties of the dog's healing tongue to a broader discussion of spiritual medicine and the "clothing" of the soul in virtues. It concludes the section on the dog and introduces the sheep (Ovis/Aries).</meta>

Just as potions are necessary for a weakened body to heal its infirmities, so too is a potion necessary for a sinful soul, through which its spiritual corruption may be healed. Moreover, this potion of the soul is made from four species <note>The author uses "species" here in the pharmacological sense, meaning the specific ingredients required for a medicinal mixture</note>, that is: weeping of the heart, true confession, true penance, and right action. This mixture is so fitting for healing the soul's infirmities that as soon as the soul is joined to it, it is immediately healed of its sicknesses. 

But once healed, if it were left without an honorable garment, how would it be presented in the heavenly court, where it must appear before its Maker? <note>original: "factore suo," meaning the Creator</note> Therefore, it is necessary that the man who has undertaken to rule and clothe the soul should dress her so honorably and fittingly that he may be able to present her praiseworthily before the angels in heaven. 

Indeed, the first garment with which the soul must be clothed is purity. For no one is presented in the heavenly court who is not cleansed either here or in the future life. But the other garments are: piety, mercy, and the other virtues with which she must be clothed. Truly, when the soul is led by such garments—that is: by pure thought, good word, and perfect work—she can be honorably presented in heavenly glory. There she will be rewarded with that beatitude which the angels desired, and for the obtaining of which God made man. He also assigned to man three counselors: namely, spiritual understanding, the power of doing well, and wisdom. If man had complied with these, he would not have lost the heavenly kingdom; but because he did not comply with them, he lost his inheritance.

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion on the virtues required to "clothe" the soul, moving now to the specific animal symbolism of the sheep (Ovis). While the previous page dealt with the preparation of the soul for the heavenly court, this page begins a new section on the nature and allegorical significance of the sheep and the ram.</meta>

### .XXIX.

The sheep is a gentle creature, its body defenseless but for its wool. It is placid in spirit and is said to be named from "oblation" <note>original: "ab oblatione," a medieval etymological theory connecting the word *ovis* (sheep) to *oblatio* (offering or sacrifice)</note> because among the ancients in the beginning, it was not bulls, but sheep that were slaughtered in sacrifice. Among these, some are called "bidentes" <term>bidentes: literally "two-toothed," referring to sheep that have reached the age—roughly two years—where two permanent incisors have grown in significantly larger than the others</term>; these have two teeth higher than the other eight, and the gentiles <note>non-Christians or pagans</note> were especially keen to offer these in sacrifice.

At the approach of winter, the sheep becomes insatiable for food. It ravenously snatches up grass because it foresees the coming harshness of winter; it seeks to satisfy itself with the fodder of the fields before all the greenery fails under the biting frost.

Furthermore, as we have said, the innocent and simple sheep signify the members of the Christian people. Moreover, the sheep sometimes serves as a type <note>a symbolic figure or foreshadowing in biblical interpretation</note> demonstrating the gentleness and patience of the Lord Himself. This is seen in the words of Isaiah regarding the innocent death of the Savior: "He was led as a sheep to the slaughter, and he opened not his mouth" <note>Isaiah 53:7</note>. In the Gospel, the sheep are the faithful people: "My sheep hear my voice" <note>John 10:27</note>. And in the Psalm: "Thou hast put all things under his feet, all sheep and oxen" <note>Psalm 8:7-8</note>.

The "two sheep" signify the peoples of the Gentiles, whom a "man" shall nourish—that man being Christ, of whom we read in the prophet: "In those days a man shall nourish a young cow and two sheep, and because of the abundance of milk he shall eat butter" <note>Isaiah 7:21-22</note>. Conversely, sheep can also signify the reprobate <note>those who are rejected or destined for punishment</note>, as in the Psalm: "Like sheep they are laid in hell; death shall feed upon them" <note>Psalm 49:14</note>.

**The Wether.** <note>original: "Vervex," referring to a castrated ram. In medieval bestiaries, the wether was often noted for its strength or for "butting" when its head itched.</note> That is, the devil.

<summary>This page describes the physical and behavioral traits of the sheep, providing etymological links to sacrifice and allegorical links to Christ's patience and the Christian faithful. It concludes with a brief, startling identification of the wether (ram) with the devil.</summary>

<keywords>sheep, ovis, sacrifice, oblation, bidentes, Isaiah, Christology, allegory, wether, Vervex, devil</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ovine symbolism, moving from the general characteristics of the sheep (Ovis) discussed on the previous page to the specific nature and allegorical significance of the ram (Aries). It explores the etymological origins of the word "Aries" and links the physical strength of the animal to the spiritual strength of the Apostles.</meta>

The ram <note>original: "Aries"</note> is so called because it is stronger than other sheep, or because it is a male—that is, a "man" among the flock—or because it is said to have a worm in its head. Driven by the itching caused by these [worms], they are compelled to butt one another and carry themselves with great force when they fight. Thus, the ram is also named from Ares <note>original: "a pote areos," referring to the Greek god of war, suggesting the ram's fierce or warlike nature</note>, which means to be bitter or sharp; this is why among our flocks the males are called rams. Alternatively, it is called so because this was the first animal sacrificed on altars <note>original: "ara"</note> by the pagans. Hence that verse: "The ram is slaughtered at the altar."

Rams, however, symbolize the Apostles or the leaders of the churches. This is why we read in Isaiah: "The rams of Nebaioth shall minister to you" <note>Isaiah 60:7</note>. And in the Psalter: "Bring to the Lord the sons of rams" <note>Psalm 29:1</note>. These men, acting as leaders of the flocks, have guided the Christian people into the sheepfolds of the Lord. When it is said that the "sons of rams" should be offered, it means those whom the Apostles begot through righteous preaching, rather than those outsiders who were approved through crooked or false doctrines.

Rams are aptly compared to the Apostles because, as we have said, these animals are very strong in the forehead and frequently knock down obstacles by butting them. This is exactly what the Apostles did through their preaching: they shattered various superstitions and the most firmly established idols as if by a heavenly forehead. Indeed, the ram is so named because it "rushes from the front" <note>original: "a fronte ruant," a medieval etymological pun linking "Aries" with "a fronte"</note>.

In another sense, however, rams can represent wicked leaders, of whom we read in the Book of Ezekiel: "with goats and lambs and rams" <note>Ezekiel 39:18</note>. 

**The Lamb.**

In the upper register, a grey-white ram and a brown ram with a curled, scale-like patterned coat face left. In the lower register, a white sheep faces right, and another brown patterned ram faces right with its head raised toward the others.</image-desc>

<summary>This page discusses the etymology and symbolism of the ram, primarily identifying it as a symbol for the Apostles who broke down pagan idols through the strength of their preaching.</summary>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of animals within the flock, transitioning from the ram (Aries) on the previous page to the lamb (Agnus). It focuses on the etymological link between the animal's name and its ability to "recognize" its mother, leading into an allegorical interpretation of the lamb as Christ and the faithful believer.</meta>

The lamb, which the Greeks call *amnos* <note>The OCR reads "αποτα," which is a common scribal corruption of the Greek word ἀμνός (*amnos*)</note>, is named *agnus* in Latin as if to mean "pious" <note>The text suggests an etymological link between the Latin *agnus* and the Greek *agnos*, meaning pure or holy</note>.

The Latins, however, believe it possesses this name because, more than all other living creatures, it recognizes its mother <note>The author links "agnus" (lamb) to the verb "agnoscere," to recognize</note>. So much so that even if it has strayed into a large flock, it immediately recognizes the voice of its parent by her bleating and hastens to its mother. It also seeks out the familiar sources of its mother’s milk. The mother, too, among many thousands of lambs, knows only her own son. Though the bleating of many is the same and their appearance is identical, she distinguishes her own by her senses from the others, and recognizes her only son with such a testimony of affection.

In a mystical sense, however, the lamb represents the person of our Savior, whose innocent death saved the human race, of whom John says: "Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sins of the world" <note>John 1:29</note>. Alternatively, it represents any innocent member of the Church <note>original: "catholici," here referring to an orthodox believer within the universal church</note> who, recognizing the voice of his mother—that is, the Church—clings to her side through obedience and obeys her commands.

There are "holy lambs" mentioned in the Gospel: "Feed my lambs" <note>John 21:15</note>. There are also "sacrificial lambs" frolicking in Isaiah: "My sword is drunk with the blood of lambs and goats" <note>Isaiah 34:6</note>. Finally, there is the "female lamb" as a victim representing the active life in Leviticus: "Let him perform penance and offer a female lamb from the flock, or a goat" <note>Leviticus 5:6</note>, which is a figure of the contemplative life.

-><image-desc>A square miniature with a red and gold frame set against a solid blue background. In the center is a stylized tree with a red trunk and symmetrical, curling branches ending in green and gold floral motifs. On either side of the tree, two grey goats or ibexes stand on their hind legs, facing the tree.</image-desc><-

Kids <note>original: "Edi," the plural of *haedus*, a young goat</note> are named from "eating" <note>the author links *haedi* to *edendo*, the gerund of the Latin *edere*, to eat</note>, for they are very fatty and of a pleasant flavor; from this comes both "to eat" and the word "edible." Whence Truth itself <note>A common medieval reference to Christ</note> says in the Gospel: "He will place the sheep on his right, but the kids on the left." <note>Matthew 25:33</note> Sometimes, however, even the righteous are expressed by this name because of their confession of sins, as in Jeremiah: "Depart," he says, "from the midst of Babylon; go out from the land of the Chaldeans, and be like kids before the flocks." <note>Jeremiah 50:8</note> The kid represents Christ <note>original: "xpe," a shorthand for the Greek *Christos*</note> because of the likeness of sinful flesh <note>Though sinless, Christ took on human flesh, which is subject to the same conditions as "sinful flesh"</note>; as in Deuteronomy: "You shall not boil a kid in its mother’s milk." <note>Deuteronomy 14:21</note> The kid also signifies the Antichrist <note>original: "antixpm"</note>, as in that passage in the Gospel: "You never gave me a kid so that I might feast with my friends." <note>Luke 15:29; from the Parable of the Prodigal Son</note> <margin>**The He-Goat.**</margin>

### XXVII.

"...I am able to tame iron: only the blood of this [the he-goat] may dissolve it." <note>This continues a thought from the previous page regarding "Adamant" (diamond or hardest stone). Medieval bestiaries claimed that while iron could not break adamant, the warm blood of a he-goat could dissolve it.</note> For the he-goat, which in the Law was offered for sin, represents the person of sinners; for by the shedding of blood—that is, the tears of penitence—he dissolves the hardness of sins. Whence in the Psalter the Church says to the Lord: "I will offer to you oxen with he-goats." <note>Psalm 66:15</note> He-goats represent sinners or the Gentiles. In Daniel: "Behold, a he-goat of the goats was coming from the north." <note>Daniel 8:5; the "north" was often associated with the origin of evil or the devil in medieval thought.</note> The two he-goats are both peoples, namely the Jews and the Gentiles, coming from the root of sin. In Leviticus, others have understood the two he-goats as Christ and Barabbas. <note>This refers to the ritual of the Day of Atonement (Leviticus 16), where one goat is sacrificed and the other, the "scapegoat," is released.</note> Men are understood as he-goats when, devoted to diabolical filth <note>original: "purıtateſ"—this is likely a scribal error for *spurcitias* (filth), or an ironic use of "purities" to describe the opposite</note>, they are clothed in bristly vices. For the he-goat <note>original: "Hircus"</note> is so named because it is hirsute <note>original: "hirsutus," meaning shaggy or hairy</note>. But the Church offers them along with the oxen <note>Oxen usually represent the hardworking faithful or apostles</note> when those who have been converted are preserved for Christ the Lord. For that thief who hung on the cross was a he-goat; but he soon became an ox when, repenting, he came to the confession of the truth. For he said: "Remember me, Lord, when you come into your kingdom." <note>Luke 23:42</note>

<margin>John M<unclear>itchell</unclear></margin>
...or because it filthily <note>original: "spurce"</note> gorges itself, for it plunges itself into the mire of the deep mud. As Horace says: "and the sow, friend to the mud." <note>original: "Oratius. & amica luto sus." A quote from Horace's Epistles 1.2.26.</note> From this also, "filth" <note>original: "spurcitia"</note> or "the filthy" <note>original: "spurci"</note> are named. We call the hairs of pigs "bristles" <note>original: "setas"</note>; and these bristles are named from the sow <note>original: "sue"</note>. From these, "shoemakers" <note>original: "sutores"</note> are also named, because they sew together hides using the bristles of sows.

Sows signify sinners and the unclean, or heretics. Concerning these, it is commanded in the Law <note>A reference to the dietary laws in Leviticus 11:7 and Deuteronomy 14:8.</note> that because they divide the hoof but do not chew the cud, their flesh should not be touched by the faithful. These people may indeed read both Testaments and receive the Gospel, but because they do not "chew" <note>The "chewing of the cud" was a common medieval metaphor for the deep, repetitive meditation on scripture.</note> upon spiritual food, they are unclean. 

Likewise, sows signify negligent penitents who return to those very things over which they had wept. Thus, Peter says in his epistle: "The dog is returned to its vomit, and the sow that was washed to her wallowing in the mire." <note>2 Peter 2:22</note> Therefore, when a dog vomits, it truly casts out the food that was weighing down its chest; but when it returns to that vomit, it is burdened again by that from which it had been lightened. So too, those who mourn for their committed sins truly cast out the wickedness of mind with which they were badly satiated and which weighed them down within, by confessing it; yet after confession, when they repeat the sin, they take it up again. 

But a sow washed in a wallowing-place of mud is made filthier even while being washed. And he who mourns his committed sins yet does not forsake the sin subjects himself to the guilt of a heavier punishment; for he even despises that pardon which he was able to obtain by weeping, and he involves himself as if in muddy water. Because while he withdraws the cleanliness of life by his own tears, he makes those very tears filthy before the eyes of God. 

Likewise, pigs represent unclean and luxurious men in the Gospel: "If you cast us out, send us into the pigs." <note>Matthew 8:31; the request of the demons known as Legion before being cast out of a man by Jesus.</note> Also elsewhere: "Do not throw your pearls before pigs." <note>Matthew 7:6; a warning against sharing sacred things with those who will not value them.</note> Pigs also represent those with unclean spirits in the Gospel: "and he sent him into his farm to feed pigs." <note>Luke 15:15; from the Parable of the Prodigal Son, representing the protagonist's lowest point of degradation.</note> The pig <note>original: "Porcus"</note> likewise signifies the unclean and sinners concerning...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the wild boar (aper), following the transition from the section on the domestic sow. The previous page ended with the heading "The Boar."</meta>

...lives in harsh places. The boar signifies the ferocity of the princes of this world; wherefore in the Psalm it is written concerning the vineyard of the Lord: "The boar from the forest has laid it waste, and the singular wild beast has fed upon it." <note>original: "Exterminauit eam aper de silva: et singularis ferus depastus est eam." Psalm 80:13 (Vulgate 79:14). In medieval exegesis, the "vineyard" usually represents the people of Israel or the Church.</note> "He laid it waste" means he dispersed his filth and his pride everywhere, which is what happened to the Jewish people. We should perhaps understand the "boar" to be Vespasian, <note>Vespasian (ruled 69–79 AD) was the Roman Emperor who began the siege of Jerusalem.</note> who acted with the ferocity and rage of a wild beast against that nation. Moreover, by this name it is signified that the Jews were held in contempt, as they were known to consider this animal unclean among all others. "From the forest" refers to the Gentiles, <note>Non-Jewish people, particularly the Romans in this context.</note> who are deservedly compared to squalid forests because they had not yet been grafted with the fruitful seed of faith.

The "singular wild beast" refers to his son Titus, <note>Titus (ruled 79–81 AD) completed the destruction of Jerusalem and the Second Temple in 70 AD.</note> who crushed the remnants of the war with such great destruction that he consumed the nation and the city like grassy fodder with terrible devouring. For it was necessary that this happen to the vineyard whose substance seemed to have been cast aside. Spiritually, however, the boar can be understood as the devil because of his ferocity and excessive strength. He is said to be "from the forest" because his kin are wild and do not [live according to God]. **Juvenal.** <note>The author is likely preparing to quote the Roman satirist Juvenal to provide a secular description of the animal's nature.</note>

.XXXIII.
22

The **bullock** <note>original: *iuuencus*</note> is so named because it begins to help <note>original: *iuuare*</note> in the use of men for cultivating the earth; or because among the pagans, a bullock was always sacrificed everywhere to Jove <note>The Roman god Jupiter (Jove).</note>, and never a bull. Their cleanliness was also taken into consideration. **Bull** <note>original: *Taurus*</note> is a Greek name, just as is **ox** <note>original: *bos*</note>. The **Indian bull** is of a tawny color and moves with swiftness; its hair is turned in the opposite direction <note>This is a standard feature of the legendary Indian Bull or the "Eale" in medieval bestiaries.</note>. These bulls [consume] everything they catch. They also move their horns with whatever flexibility they wish. Because of the hardness of their hide, they reject every weapon, and they possess such immense ferocity that once captured, these animals perish from their own rage. 

The bull signifies Christ; in Genesis [it says]: "and in their fury they hamstrung the bull." <note>original: "et in furore suo subneruauerit taurũ." Genesis 49:6. Medieval theologians often interpreted the "bull" in this verse as a prophecy of the Jews harming Christ.</note> Likewise, the humble common people revere the bull as the power of this world, the horn of pride; of whom it is said in Jeremiah: "you have bellowed like bulls," <note>original: "mugistis ut tauri." Jeremiah 50:11.</note> and elsewhere, "and the bulls with the powerful." <note>original: "et tauri cũ potentib;." Isaiah 34:7.</note> Bulls likewise draw their meaning toward both the good and the bad side. Toward the good, as in the Gospel: "Behold, I have prepared my banquet; my bulls and fatted calves are killed, and all things are ready." <note>original: "Ecce p̃ndium meũ paraui. tauri mei & altilia occisa sunt & omnia parata." Matthew 22:4.</note>

XXXVI.
26

...are accustomed <note>Continuing from the previous page's description of maternal instincts.</note>, and by frequent lowing she testifies to her pious affection if perhaps her calf is missing. Oxen know how to keep themselves to their stalls when rain is imminent. Likewise, when they have gathered by natural sense a change in the sky, they look outside and extend their necks beyond the stalls, all in one manner, as if to testify that they wish to go forth. **Hesiod** <note>A Greek poet (c. 700 BC) known for his agricultural manual *Works and Days*.</note> says: "Here the countryman is guided by Orion." <note>The rising and setting of the constellation Orion served as a calendar for ancient farmers to begin various seasonal tasks.</note> Those which have a breadth of skin from the chin to the shoulders are called **dewlaps** <note>original: *palearia*</note>, named from the skin itself <note>original: *pelle*</note> as if they were "skin-flaps" <note>original: *pellearia*</note>, which is a sign of noble breeding in an ox. 

In oxen, therefore, sometimes the madness of the luxurious is preached; sometimes, laborious strength; and sometimes, the humility of the herds is expressed. For that the madness of the luxurious is designated by comparison under the name of an ox, **Solomon** indicates; who, when he had first mentioned the petulance of a seductive woman, added: "And immediately he follows her like an ox led to the slaughter." <note>Proverbs 7:22.</note> Again, that the labor of a **preacher** is expressed by the name of an ox, the words of the Law testify, which say: "You shall not muzzle the ox while it treads out the grain." <note>Deuteronomy 25:4. In medieval theology, the "ox" represents the apostle or preacher who "treads" the word of God into the hearts of the people and deserves to be supported by them.</note>

xxxvi
23

...who labors <note>Continuing from the previous page's discussion of the preacher as a symbolic ox treading out spiritual grain.</note>.

There are oxen in Germany having horns of such great weight that from them, drinking vessels <note>original: *gerule*; vessels used for carrying or drinking</note> of remarkable capacity are made for royal tables. In the buffaloes or the aurochs <note>original: *uris*; a large, extinct species of wild cattle</note>, one may understand the teachers or the rulers of the people. They possess the likeness of good teachers in the rank <note>original: *gradali*</note> of the office they hold, but they are swollen with pride. Trusting in the horns of worldly power rather than in divine help, even though they are chosen for the apostolic fellowship, they lord it over the clergy <note>A reference to 1 Peter 5:3, warning leaders not to be "domineering over those in your charge."</note> instead of becoming examples to the flock. To these, the Lord threatens through the prophet, saying: "Woe to the shepherds of Israel who fed themselves! Should not the flocks be fed by the shepherds? You ate the milk and clothed yourselves with the wool, and you killed what was fat; but what was weak you did not strengthen, and what was sick you did not heal. What was broken you did not bind up, and what was cast away you did not lead back <note>original: *dyristis*, likely a scribal error for the Latin *reduxistis*</note>; what was lost you did not seek; but with harshness you ruled over them, and with power." <note>Ezekiel 34:2–4.</note>

### The Cow

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature with a red border and a blue background. The cow <term>vacca</term> is named as if it were a "she-ox" <note>original: *boacca*, a medieval wordplay linking *bos* (ox) and *vacca* (cow)</note>. For it belongs to the category of "movable names" <note>nouns that change their endings to indicate gender</note>, just as a lion becomes a lioness, or a dragon becomes a dragoness. Sometimes this animal is drawn into a good spiritual meaning, as it is in the Law <note>The Old Testament legal codes</note>, where there was a red cow <note>The "Red Heifer" described in Numbers 19:2, used for ritual purification.</note> that was whole, and which had not borne a yoke...

# XXXVI.

...[carrying no] yoke <note>Continuing from a discussion of the Red Heifer in the Book of Numbers 19:2, which specifies the animal must never have been yoked.</note>, seems to be sacrificed for the purification of the Lord's sanctuary. It is common for strength to be signified by the masculine, and weakness <note>original: *infirmitas*</note> by the feminine. What, therefore, is represented by the cow but the weakness of the Lord’s incarnation, taken up for the sake of sacrifice? Regarding this it is written: "For although he died through weakness, yet he lives by power." <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 13:4.</note> Rightly, however, is this cow called **red**, because clearly his humanity was reddened by the blood of the Passion <note>original: *rubra per cruore passionis*</note>. The humanity of the Lord was whole <note>original: *integra*; meaning pure or unblemished</note> and every action of his was perfect; in that humanity, indeed, there was no blemish, because it is truly written of him: "Who did no sin, neither was guile found in his mouth." <note>1 Peter 2:22</note>

Vitulus

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature in a blue rectangular frame with a gold border. It depicts three calves (vituli) running in profile towards the left. The calf in the foreground is reddish-orange, the middle one is greyish-tan, and the one in the background is white.</image-desc>

**Calf** <note>original: *Vitulus*</note> and **heifer** <note>original: *uitula*</note> are named from youth: that is, from a "green" <note>original: *viridi*; the text draws a linguistic connection between the youth of the calf and the "greenness" of young age</note> age or a virgin. A heifer, therefore, is small and has not yet given birth; for once she has given birth, she is called a young cow <note>original: *iuuenca*</note> or a cow <note>original: *vacca*</note>. The calf, therefore, is understood in a positive sense when it demonstrates the innocence of the faithful, or the sincere praise of pious devotion, whence it is written: "We will render the calves of our lips." <note>Hosea 14:2; a metaphor for offering prayers and praise instead of animal sacrifices.</note> And in the psalm: "Then shall they lay calves upon your altar." <note>Psalm 51:19</note> Indeed, he used "calves" to represent the innocent, whose age is in its first stage and [who are free] from the yoke of sin...

<summary>This page explores the Christological symbolism of the Red Heifer, representing the "weakness" of Christ's human incarnation and his blood. It also defines the "calf" as a symbol of youthful innocence and spiritual sacrifice through prayer.</summary>
<keywords>Red Heifer, incarnation of Christ, Passion, Vitulus, innocence, sacrifice of praise, biblical allegory, medieval etymology</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical analysis of the calf (vitulus), transitioning from its youthful nature to its various roles as a symbol for the Evangelist Luke, the martyrs, and Christ himself. The text then introduces the next animal in the bestiary: the Camel.</meta>

[...is taught and] proven to be foreign <note>original: *probatur aliena*; this is the conclusion of a sentence from the previous page regarding the nature of the calf</note>. And therefore it remained in such a word, so that it might judge that act of the former Law <note>The "former Law" refers to the Old Testament/Mosaic Law.</note> to have been an image of things to come; whether He sends forth those **preachers of the Gospel** whose image Luke the Evangelist took up in the figure of a calf <note>In traditional Christian iconography, the "Four Living Creatures" represent the four Evangelists: the Man (Matthew), the Lion (Mark), the Calf/Ox (Luke), and the Eagle (John).</note>. These preachers would not beat the air with lowing <note>original: *mugitibus*; the sound of cattle</note>, but would fill the world with the preaching of divine faith.

Or rather, we ought to recognize those calves who offered their souls as a "sacrifice of sweetness" <note>original: *hostiam suavitatis*; a phrase often used for a pleasing burnt offering to God</note> at the holy altars. For even Father Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of the Western Church.</note>, when he was dealing with those Gospel figures, in a certain place called the Lord himself a "calf," who offered himself as a sacrifice for the salvation of all. Wherefore, whether it is understood as being about youths, or preachers, or martyrs: the prophet could send ahead such "calves" to the altars of the Lord, whom he knew to be fitting for the Christian religion. 

The calf is **Christ** in the Gospel: "Bring the fatted calf and kill it" <note>A reference to the Parable of the Prodigal Son in Luke 15:23, where the fatted calf represents the celebration of the sinner's return—and, allegorically, Christ's sacrifice.</note>. And in another place: "And he will break them to pieces like a calf of Lebanon" <note>A reference to Psalm 29:6.</note>. There are holy calves of the faith, growing and having necks free from the yoke of the Law <note>A common medieval trope suggesting that Christians were "free" from the ritual requirements of Jewish Law.</note>; and there are the wanton "calves of the Jews" in the Psalm: "Many calves have surrounded me, fat bulls have besieged me" <note>Psalm 22:12. In medieval exegesis, this was interpreted as the crowds and leaders who surrounded Christ during his crucifixion.</note>.

**Camel** <note>original: *Camelus*</note>

<image-desc>A framed illumination of a dromedary camel. The camel is rendered in a tan-gold color with a long, slender neck and a single large hump decorated with stylized brown scrollwork or circular patterns. It has thin legs, cloven hooves, and a head with large pointed ears and a prominent eye, facing right in profile. The animal is enclosed within a rectangular frame with a thick red outer border and a solid blue inner background.</image-desc>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the allegorical interpretation of the calf as a symbol of sacrifice and the Evangelists to the entry for the Camel. It explores the animal's physical characteristics, its behavior in the desert, and its symbolic role in Christian teaching.</meta>

The **camel** <term>Camelus</term> gave a name to its own cause. This is either because when they are loaded, they become shorter and lower by kneeling—for in Greek, *chamai* <note>original: "cami"; likely referring to *chamai* (χαμαί) meaning 'on the ground' or 'low'</note> means humble and short—or because it has a curved back, as *camur* is a Greek word <note>The author attributes the word *camur* to Greek, though it is a Latin term for "curved" or "crooked."</note> meaning "curved." Although other regions send these animals, **Arabia** sends the most; the **Bactrian** camels are the strongest, even though Arabia produces them in great numbers.

However, these types differ: those from Arabia have two humps <note>original: *bina tubera*; Note that the medieval author has reversed the biological reality: Dromedary (Arabian) camels have one hump, while Bactrian camels have two.</note> on their backs, while those from Bactria have only one. These animals never wear down their feet. They even have certain fleshy pads or tracks on their joints <note>original: *carpis*; literally "wrists," referring to the callosities on a camel's legs that protect it when kneeling on sand.</note>, which is why their gait is hindered if they do not have the support of a guide to direct their path. They are kept for a double service: some are suited for carrying loads, while others are more skilled [at riding]. But they do not carry weight beyond what is fair, nor do they wish to travel further than their accustomed distances; they are so restrained by their own habits that they demand to be unloaded as soon as they arrive.

They hate the horse kind. They can even endure thirst for three days. But when the opportunity to drink is given, they fill themselves as much as compensates for past neglect and will benefit them for a long time in the future. They seek out muddy waters and flee from pure ones. Indeed, unless the liquid is naturally filthy, they stir up the mud with constant trampling so that it becomes turbid, which they find more pleasing. 

They live for a hundred years. If by chance they are moved to foreign lands, they contract diseases from the unfamiliar air. Females are prepared for war; it was discovered that the desire for mating weighed them down, for people believe they become stronger if they are kept from intercourse. 

The camel, however, signifies either the **humility of Christ**, who carried the burdens of our sins, or the **Gentile people** converted to the faith of Christ. For as the Lord says in the Gospel: "It is easier for a camel to go through the eye of a needle..." <note>A reference to Matthew 19:24, used here to begin an allegorical comparison between the animal and the spiritual state of believers.</note>

Take a handful of **red weed** <note>Likely referring to the common poppy or a similar field plant.</note> and <unclear>...</unclear> half of each <unclear>...</unclear> handful, and 3 or 4 pieces of **liquorice** bruised. <unclear>... and</unclear> boil them in a pottle <note>A pottle is an old liquid measure equal to two quarts (approx. 2 liters).</note> of water as fast as possible until the third part is consumed. Then take a quart of the same and <unclear>...</unclear> of water and add a spoonful of honey or more, and a piece of **alum** <note>A mineral salt used historically as an astringent and for cleaning wounds.</note>, and boil it all well together. Put a stone of salt in it <unclear>... of fine</unclear> white sugar; and when you have them, wash the mouth with it and <unclear>...</unclear> every night before going to bed.

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the camel, transitioning from its physical traits discussed on the previous page to its symbolic meaning as a figure of Christ. It then begins a new entry for the Donkey (Asinus).</meta>

.XXXV.

[It is easier for a camel] to pass through than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of heaven <note>original: "transire qum divitem intrare in regnum celorum"; a reference to Matthew 19:24</note>. This shows that it is easier for Christ <note>original: "xpm" (Christum)</note>, in His poverty, to be converted to the lovers of this world than for the lovers of the world to be converted to Christ. Furthermore, He wished Himself to be understood by the name of the **Camel**, for He voluntarily took up the burdens of our weak humility. For in whom is that which is written more clearly understood: **The greater you are, the more you should humble yourself in all things** <note>original: "Quoto magnus es humilia te in omnibus"; a quote from the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 3:18</note>. Moreover, by the **needle**, He signifies piercings; by these piercings, He means the pains endured during His Passion. Therefore, the **eye of the needle** refers to the narrow hardships of the Passion, through which He deigned to mend—that is, to restore—the garments of our nature, as if sewing them back together. Thus, having been better reformed after our fall, we may rejoice according to the testimony of the Apostle who says:
> For as many of you as were **baptized into Christ**, you have **clothed yourselves with Christ**. <note>original: "Quicunq; enim in xpo baptizati estis. xpm induistis"; a quote from Galatians 3:27</note>

The **donkey** <term>Asinus</term> and the **little ass** <term>asellus</term> are named from "sitting" <note>original: "a sedendo"; the author is using a common medieval folk etymology linking *asinus* to *sedere* (to sit)</note>, as if the word were *asedus*. However, this name was one that better suited horses; this animal took the name because humans began to ride <note>original: "presidere"; literally to sit upon or preside over</note> them before they captured horses. It is indeed a slow animal and lacking in reason...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the donkey (Asinus), transitioning from the physical descriptions of various breeds to a series of allegorical interpretations rooted in the Old and New Testaments. It focuses on the donkey as a symbol of both the Jewish Synagogue and the newly converted Gentile people.</meta>

<image-desc>A faint circular diagram or emblem is located in the left margin. It contains concentric circular lines and possible radial sections, with light remnants of red and green pigment. The image is heavily degraded by moisture damage.</image-desc>

...they will refuse. Immediately, as he wished, man saddled the animal for himself. **Arcadian donkeys** <note>original: "Asinos archadicos"; referring to the region of Arcadia in Greece, famous in antiquity for its livestock</note> are so called because they were first brought from Arcadia; they are large and tall. However, the smaller **donkey** <note>original: "asellus"</note> is more useful than the **wild ass** <note>original: "onagro"</note> because it both endures labor and almost never rejects neglect. By the name of donkeys (male or female), sometimes the **gentleness of the simple** is designated, and sometimes the **foolishness of the pagans**.

For mystically, because the donkey is a brute and lustful animal, it signifies either the **pagan people** (as we have said), whom the Lord deigned to sit upon as He traveled toward Jerusalem <note>A reference to Christ’s entry into Jerusalem on Palm Sunday, where He rode a donkey to symbolize peace and humility</note> so that He might make them subject to Himself and lead them to the heavenly homeland; or it signifies the **foolish man** who follows only the luxury of this world. Hence Jacob said to his son **Issachar**, whose name is interpreted as "reward" <note>original: "merces"; medieval scholars often used etymologies to explain the hidden meaning of biblical names</note>: **Issachar is a strong donkey lying down between borders; he saw that rest was good and the land was excellent, and he bowed his shoulder to carry, and became a servant paying tribute.** <note>original: "Yisachar asinus fortis accubans int’ termīos..."; a quote from Genesis 49:14–15</note>

Therefore, the **pagan people**, whom the Lord purchased with the price of His own blood, once appeared to be a brute and lustful animal, using no reason; but now they are strong, subjecting their hidden thoughts to our Savior. By bowing their necks to the Lord our Redeemer, they bear the **yoke of Gospel discipline**. Hence they are also made a **servant paying tribute** <note>original: "tribut’ serviens"</note>. This people offers good faith to the supreme King and His Christ, performing the duties of good works.

Again, the donkey represents the **foolish man**. In the Book of Deuteronomy <note>original: "In deuteronomio"</note> it says: **You shall not plow with an ox and a donkey together** <note>Deuteronomy 22:10</note>; that is, do not join a fool with a wise man in the preaching of the Word. The donkey also signifies the **Synagogue of the Jews**; as seen in Genesis: **Binding his foal to the vine, and his donkey's colt to the choice vine.** <note>original: "ligans ad uineã pullū suum..."; Genesis 49:11</note>

The donkey also represents the **human body**; hence it is written in the Book of Judges: **You who ride on shining donkeys.** <note>original: "Qui ascendunt sū nitentes asinos"; Judges 5:10</note> Likewise, it is said there of **Achsah**, the daughter of Caleb, who carries the image of the soul: **when she had sighed, sitting upon her donkey...** <note>original: "cumq: suspirasset sedens i asino"; Judges 1:14. Achsah dismounting her donkey was often interpreted as the soul descending from carnal desires to seek the "upper springs" of spiritual grace</note>. By the "firstborn of the donkey," the beginnings of a luxurious life...

<summary>This page explores the donkey's symbolism, contrasting its physical hardiness with its allegorical roles as the converted Gentile people, the tribe of Issachar, and the human body.</summary>
<keywords>donkey, bestiary, Issachar, Achsah, Genesis, Deuteronomy, allegory, Synagogue, Gentiles, Arcadia</keywords>

...as in **Exodus**: *You shall redeem the firstborn of a donkey with a lamb.* <note>original: "p[ri]mogenita asini redimes oue"; a reference to Exodus 13:13, where the Law of Moses requires a lamb to be sacrificed in place of a donkey, which was considered "unclean"</note> For then the **donkey** signifies the **foolish man**, when it represents one who carries the burdens of sin and exercises the lusts of the flesh until the end of life. Hence it is written that **Anah**, while he was pasturing the donkeys of his father **Zibeon**, found warm springs in the desert. <note>original: "anna... esebon"; referring to Genesis 36:24. Medieval scholars often debated the meaning of this passage, sometimes interpreting "warm springs" as the discovery of how to breed mules</note> Hence it is said of the lustful through the **Prophet**: *whose flesh is like the flesh of donkeys.* <note>A reference to Ezekiel 23:20, comparing the lust of the wayward to that of beasts</note>

### Onager.

<image-desc>A rectangular framed miniature with a red and green geometric border. It depicts an onager (wild ass), rendered in reddish-brown and white. The animal is shown in a violent act, biting the testicles of a smaller, younger onager that lies on its back beneath it. This scene illustrates the medieval belief mentioned in the text that male onagers castrate their male offspring to prevent them from breeding.</image-desc>

**Onager** <note>The wild ass</note> is interpreted as a "wild donkey" <unclear>... the Greeks</unclear> call it "wild." They are <unclear>... </unclear> and untamed, wandering in the desert <unclear>... </unclear>. Moreover, [the males] preside over the herds of females. When they find young males <unclear>... </unclear>, they cut off their testicles with a bite. Wary of this, the mothers hide them in secret places. The **Physiologus** <note>A famous ancient text that describes animals and gives them Christian moral meanings; it served as the basis for most medieval bestiaries</note> says of the wild ass that on the twenty-fifth day of the month of March, it brays twelve times in the night and likewise in the day. And from this it is known that it is the **equinox** <term>equinoctium: the two times a year when day and night are of equal length</term>; they recognize the number of hours of the day or night from the braying of the wild ass throughout the individual hours. The **devil** <note>original: "diabolus"</note> has the figure of this creature, who, when he knows the night and day are made equal—that is...

<meta>This page continues the allegorical description of the Onager (wild ass), specifically how its braying marks the equinox, which was interpreted by medieval scholars as a symbol of the struggle between light and dark, and the devil’s envy of those in the light. It then transitions into the entry for the Dromedary before concluding with a later addition concerning alchemy or palmistry.</meta>

...the people who walked in darkness <note>original: "populum qui ambulabat in tenebris"; a reference to Isaiah 9:2. In the bestiary tradition, the wild ass symbolizes the Gentiles who were once in the "darkness" of paganism but are called toward the light of God</note> on the way to God, to equal the faith of the righteous. Just as the night is equal to the day of the year <note>This refers to the spring equinox. Medieval bestiaries claimed that on the 25th of March, the onager brays twelve times to mark the equal hours of day and night</note>, the animal bellows by night and by day, seeking its food through each individual hour. For the wild ass does not bray if it lacks fodder, as it is said in **Job**: *Does the wild ass cry out if it has food?* <note>Job 6:5. The text uses this to contrast the animal's physical hunger with a spiritual hunger for God</note> Whence also the Apostle says: *Our adversary the devil goes about like a roaring lion, seeking someone to devour.* <note>1 Peter 5:8</note> **Dromedary.**

<image-desc>A rectangular framed miniature with a red border. Inside, three kings wearing gold crowns and red and blue robes are riding dromedaries (one-humped camels). <insert>The dromedary is a type</insert> of camel, though of a smaller <insert>stature, from which</insert> it also takes its name. For in <insert>Greek, "dromos"</insert> is the word for a "course" or "speed." <note>The word "dromedary" comes from the Greek "dromas" (running), emphasizing the animal's swiftness compared to the larger Bactrian camel</note> Indeed, it is accustomed to travel a hundred miles and more in a single day. This animal—just like the sheep, the ox, and the camel—ruminates <note>Chews the cud</note>. The word "rumination" is derived from the "ruina" <note>A medieval folk etymology; the text suggests the name comes from the way food is "thrown back" or "falls back" up the throat</note>, the prominent part of the throat through which food that has been swallowed is called back up by these animals. **Horses.**

<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the Dromedary (the end of which was seen on the previous page) to the entry for the Horse. It follows the standard medieval bestiary tradition, largely drawing on the etymologies of Isidore of Seville to explain the nature and characteristics of horses.</meta>

<image-desc>A framed miniature depicting a team of three horses. The dominant horse in the foreground is a dappled blue-grey, shown in a rearing or leaping posture. Directly behind its neck, the heads and foreparts of two additional horses—one reddish-brown and one tan—emerge, suggesting they are yoked together as a team (quadriga). The scene is set against a gold leaf rectangle within a blue field, surrounded by a red and black border.</image-desc>

**Horses** <note>original: "Equi"; the text begins the entry for the horse, focusing first on the origin of the name</note> are so called because when they were joined to four-horse chariots <note>original: "quadrigis"</note>, they were matched <note>original: "equabantur"; the author is using a play on words, suggesting "equi" (horses) comes from "equare" (to make equal/match), because chariot horses were matched in size and speed</note> as pairs in their form and were linked by a similar pace in their running.

The steed <note>original: "Caballus"; a common term for a horse, from which we get the word "cavalry"</note> is named from its "hollow foot" <note>original: "a pede cavo"</note>, because as it walks, its pressed hoof creates a hollow in the earth, a trait which other animals do not have; hence also the name "noisy-foot" <note>original: "sonipes"; a poetic Latin term for a horse</note>, because it sounds with its feet. 

The liveliness of horses is great. For they exult in the fields; they scent the coming of war; they are excited to battle by the sound of the trumpet; and, roused by a voice, they are urged to the race. They grieve when they have been defeated, and they exult when they have won. Some sense enemies in war to such an extent that they attack their adversaries with bites. Some even recognize their own masters, forgetting their tameness if they are changed <note>meaning if the master is replaced, the horse may become wild again</note>. Some will receive no one on their back except their master. 

We shall give an example of this: the horse of **Alexander the Great**, called **Bucephalus** <note>original: "buceuola"; from the Greek "Boukephalos," meaning "Ox-head"</note>, named either from the grimness of his look, or from the notable mark of a bull’s head branded <note>The text ends abruptly here, mid-sentence; the full tradition explains that the horse was branded with a bull's head or had a growth that resembled one</note>...

<summary>This page describes the etymology and noble characteristics of horses, emphasizing their intelligence, emotional depth, and loyalty to their masters. It highlights their role in warfare and introduces the famous example of Alexander the Great’s horse, Bucephalus.</summary>

<keywords>horses, etymology, Bucephalus, Alexander the Great, cavalry, war horses, animal behavior, medieval bestiary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of horses, focusing on famous historical horses (like Alexander’s Bucephalus and Caesar's mount) and their legendary loyalty and emotional depth. It draws heavily from Book 12 of Isidore of Seville's Etymologies and Pliny the Elder's Natural History.</meta>

...had on its shoulder <note>original: "armo"; this continues a description of Alexander the Great’s horse, Bucephalus</note>. Or perhaps because certain horn-like protuberances projected from its forehead. Although at other times it might be sat upon gently by its groom, once it had received the royal saddle-cloth, it never deigned to carry anyone except its master. There are many proofs of its character, by which it bore Alexander safely out of the most cruel battles by its aid.

The horse of Gaius Caesar <note>Julius Caesar</note> received no one on its back except Caesar. The King of the Scythians, who was exceptionally unrestrained, when he wished to despoil those opposing the Indians, was kicked and torn to death by his own horse. When King Nicomedes was killed, his horse ended its own life through starvation.

When Antiochus had subdued the Galatians in battle, he leaped upon the horse of a general named Antarea <note>original: "Antarea nomine ducis"</note>, who had fallen in the line of battle, intending to fight. But the horse was so roused by the spirit of the wolf-bits <term>lupatorum</term> <note>a "wolf-bit" was a jagged, spiked bit used to control difficult horses</note> that it intentionally threw itself headlong so as to crush both itself and its rider on every side. 

In this kind of animal, life was longer for the males. We read that a horse indeed lived to seventy years. It is also noted that a horse by the name of Opume <note>a corruption of a name, possibly referring to a specific stallion in Roman records</note> lasted forty years in his prime for breeding <term>venerem</term>.

The sexual desire of mares is extinguished if their manes are shorn. During their birthing, a love-charm <term>veneficium</term> is born, which they bear on their foreheads; it is produced in a yellow color like a dried fig, and is called the <term>hippomanes</term> <note>literally "horse-madness"; a legendary substance used in ancient love potions</note>. If this is snatched away immediately, the mother will by no means offer her teats to the foal in her haste. The greater the love and hope the horses have, the more deeply they plunge their nostrils in to whinny <note>original: "hinnitum"</note>.

When their masters are killed or dying, horses shed tears. For they say the horse alone, besides man, weeps and feels the emotion of grief. Hence also in Centaurs, the nature of horses and men is mixed. Indeed, men are accustomed to gather a divination of a future event from either the sadness or the eagerness of horses. Frequently...

<summary>This page catalogs the legendary loyalty and emotional intelligence of horses, citing historical examples like Bucephalus and Caesar's mount, while describing their capacity for grief and their use in divination.</summary>
<keywords>Bucephalus, Alexander the Great, Julius Caesar, Scythians, Nicomedes, Antiochus, Galatians, hippomanes, Centaurs, animal emotion, divination</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the medieval encyclopedic tradition of categorizing the natural world, specifically drawing from Book 12 of Isidore of Seville's Etymologies. It transitions from the historical anecdotes of famous horses on the previous page to the practical and aesthetic criteria used to judge a "noble" horse.</meta>

As the ancients say, in noble horses <note>original: "generosis equis"</note>, four things are looked for:
form, beauty, merit, and color. **Form** requires that the body be strong
and solid, with a height suited to its strength; a long flank;
tucked up, large, and round haunches; a broad, open chest;
the whole body knotted with a density of muscles <note>original: "maculor̃," likely a scribal error for "musculorum" (muscles); the text describes a rippling, muscular build</note>; a lean foot; and
solidified with a concave hoof. **Beauty** requires that the head be small
and lean, with the skin almost adhering to the bones. The ears should be short and sharp;
the eyes large; the nostrils wide; and the neck upright; the mane and 
tail thick; the solidity of the hooves a fixed roundness. **Merit**
requires that it be bold in spirit, swift of foot, and with trembling limbs—
which is a sign of courage—and one who is easily
roused from the deepest rest, and once its speed is excited,
it is not difficult to restrain. The movement of a horse is understood by its ears;
its virtue is seen in its trembling limbs. The **Color** especially sought after is bay <term>badius</term> <note>a reddish-brown or chestnut color</note>, golden,
rosy, myrtle-colored, fawn, dun <term>gilvus</term>, blue-gray <term>glaucus</term>, dappled <term>scutulatus</term>,
hoary, bright white, dull white, spotted, and black. Next in order is
the variegated, distinguished by black and bay; the remaining variegated color
or flaxen <note>original: "ameus," likely a corruption of "amineus," a pale wine-color or yellowish-white</note> is the worst. The ancients called the **bay** "strong"
because, among other animals, it walks more strongly. This same color is also
**spadix**, which they call "date-red" <note>original: "fenicatum," referring to Phoenician purple or the color of a palm branch</note>, named from the color of the palm which
the Syrians call *spadix*. **Blue-gray** <note>original: "glaucus"</note> is like those that have painted eyes
bathed in a certain brightness. **Dun** <note>original: "gilvus"</note>, however, is a better
color, being somewhat whitish. **Spotted** <note>original: "guttatus"</note> consists of white with black
dots intervening. **Bright white** <note>original: "candidus"</note> and **white** <note>original: "albus"</note> differ from
each other. For "white" <note>albus</note> is with a certain paleness. "Bright white" <note>candidus</note>, however, is
snowy and bathed in pure light. **Hoary** <note>original: "canus"</note> is so called because it is made of white
and black. **Dappled** <note>original: "scutulatus"</note> is so called because of the circles <note>original: "orbes"</note> it has, white in t... <note>the sentence breaks off at the end of the page</note>

<summary>This page outlines the four classical criteria for judging horses—form, beauty, merit, and color—and provides a detailed etymological breakdown of horse coat colors.</summary>
<keywords>horse breeding, hippology, Isidore of Seville, animal colors, medieval veterinary medicine, bay horse, dappled horse</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of horse colors and types from Isidore of Seville's Etymologies, transitioning from physical descriptions to the spiritual and allegorical meanings of horses in Christian scripture. It concludes the list of coat colors and then categorizes horses by their social utility before moving into biblical exegesis.</meta>

...purples. The **variegated** <note>original: "Varius"</note> is so called because it has paths of unequal colors. Those, however, who have only white feet are called **petuli**; those with a white forehead are called **calidi** <note>literally "hot" or "bold," referring to the "star" or "blaze" on a horse's face</note>. There is the **fawn-colored** horse, which is commonly called **gaurante**. The **dosinus** is so named because its color is like that of an ass <note>original: "asino"</note>, which is to say, ash-colored. These are wild creatures of the type called **equiferi** <term>wild horses</term>; because of this, they cannot be elevated to the dignity of urban use.

The **mauron** is black, for in Greek they call black *maurōn*. The **mannus** <note>a pony or small Gallic horse</note>, however, is a shorter horse, which the common people call a **brunum** <term>brown horse or nag</term>. The ancients used the term **veredi** <note>original: "Veredos"</note> for horses because they drew carriages <note>original: "redas," a four-wheeled traveling carriage</note>, which is to say they led them; or perhaps because they run along the public ways through which carriages were accustomed to travel.

There are three kinds of horses. The first is **noble**, excellent and suited for all tasks. The second is **common** and of the herd, suited for carrying loads but not for riding. The third is born from a **mixture** of different types, which is also called **hybrid** <note>original: "bigenerū"</note>, because it is born from two different breeds at once.

Horses are sometimes interpreted in a good sense in **figurative language** <note>original: "i n u f i g u r a t i o n ē," referring to biblical allegory</note>, and sometimes in a bad sense. An example of the good sense is from the prophet **Habakkuk** <note>original: "abacuc"</note>: "Ascending," he says, "upon your horses, and your riding is salvation." And in the book of **Job**, the divine voice says: "Will you give strength to the horse, or surround his neck with neighing?" and so forth. Here, the horses are understood to be the **holy preachers**, whose rider is the Lord; He gives them strength—that is, constancy of mind—and the sound of healthy preaching.

On the contrary, regarding the other kind of horse, it is written in the **Song of Exodus**: "The horse and his rider he has thrown into the sea." And again: "Pharaoh's chariots and his army he has thrown into the sea." There are horses that the Lord mounts, and they travel throughout the whole earth; of whom it is said, "and your riding is salvation." However, there are also horses that have the **devil** and his angels as their riders. **Judas** was a horse, certainly: so long as he had the Lord as his rider, he was part of the cavalry of salvation along with the others and with...

<summary>This page concludes a catalog of horse colors and breeds (including wild and carriage horses) before shifting to a medieval theological interpretation of horses as symbols for preachers or sinners in the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>horse colors, breeds, Isidore of Seville, veredi, biblical allegory, Habakkuk, Job, Exodus, Judas Iscariot, medieval zoology</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the horse in Christian scripture, comparing the sinner to a horse controlled by an evil rider, before transitioning into a natural history of the mule.</meta>

...the apostles being sent. Health and healing are granted to the sick and those suffering. At this point, when he withdrew himself to the devil—for after the morsel <note>original: "bucellā"; referring to the bread dipped and given to Judas at the Last Supper in John 13:27</note>, Satan entered into him—Satan became his rider, and having the lead over him, began to ride against our Lord and Savior. For all who sin neigh like horses, but they have riders by whom they are driven: evil angels. And for that reason they are fierce. Therefore, the Lord "threw the horse and the rider into the sea," <note>A reference to the Song of Moses in Exodus 15:1</note> and he became my salvation.
