The name **mule** <note>original: "Mulus"</note>, however, has a word derived from the Greek. For it is called so in Greek because, once subdued by the yoke of the millers, it draws the slow millstones in a circle <note>the author is likely connecting the Latin word "mulus" to "mola," meaning millstone</note>. The Jews assert that **Anah** <note>original: "Ana"</note>, the grandson of **Esau**, was the first to cause herds of mares to be mounted by donkeys in the desert, so that from them, mules—new animals created against nature—might be born. We also say that **wild asses** <term>onagers</term> were admitted to female donkeys for this purpose, and he discovered this kind of breeding so that the swiftest of donkeys might be born from them. Indeed, human industry forced different animals into union.

<summary>This page concludes a spiritual allegory comparing the sinner Judas to a horse ridden by Satan and begins a natural history of the mule, detailing its "against nature" origins through the biblical figure Anah.</summary>
<keywords>horse allegory, Judas Iscariot, Satan, mule, Anah, hybridization, onager, biblical history</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origin of the mule, following the mention of Anah, who was said to have first discovered the cross-breeding of horses and donkeys in the wilderness.</meta>

**a**nd thus, by an adulterous mixing, he discovered another kind. Just as **Jacob** prepared similarities of colors against nature <note>Jacob used peeled rods to influence the coats of his flock; see Genesis 30:37–43</note>. For his sheep conceived such offspring as the shadows of the rams mounting from above which they contemplated in the mirror of the waters. 
**F**inally, it is also said that this very thing happens in herds of mares: that they present noble horses to the sight of those conceiving ~~horses to the sight of those conceiving~~ <note>The scribe repeated and then struck through this phrase, a common error known as dittography</note> so that they might be able to conceive and create offspring similar to them. 
**F**or even fanciers of pigeon species place beautiful painted pigeons in those same places which the birds frequent, so that by the catching of the sight, they might generate similar ones. 
**F**rom this, it is said that certain people order pregnant women to look upon no very ugly faces of animals, such as dog-headed baboons <note>original: "kynokefalos"; refers to the *Cynocephalus*, a mythical dog-headed man or, in a natural history context, a baboon</note> and monkeys, lest, meeting their sight, they might bear similar offspring. 
**F**or this is the nature of females: that whatever kind they have looked upon or conceived in the mind in the extreme heat of pleasure when they conceive, such offspring they also bear. 
**F**or indeed an animal, in the act of mating <note>original: "usu uenerio"; literally 'the use of Venus'</note>, transmits external forms into its visage, and by the patterns of those forms, the appearance snatched from them passes into its own quality. 
**A**mong animals, they call "hybrids" <note>original: "bigenera"; literally 'of two kinds'</note> those which are born from different parents: such as the **mule** from a mare and a donkey; the **hinny** <note>original: "burdo"</note> from a horse and a female donkey; **hybrids** <note>original: "Ibride"</note> from a wild boar and a sow; the **tityrus** <note>original: "titiurus"</note> from a sheep and a male goat. 
**T**he **musmon** <note>original: "Musmo"</note> is from a female goat and a ram. A ram, moreover, is the leader of the flock. The **bialca** <note>likely a term for a wolf-dog hybrid</note> is from a wolf and a female dog. 
**F**or the mule, because it is a burden-bearing animal, signifies the followers of folly. Since **Absalom** sat upon a mule and raised a sedition against his father <note>Absalom, son of King David, rebelled against him and died when his hair caught in a tree while riding his mule; see 2 Samuel 18</note>, he rightly perished having followed it. 
**T**herefore, the **Psalmist** restrains his listeners, saying: 
"**B**e not like the horse and the mule, in which there is no understanding; with bit and bridle bind their jaws" <note>original: "Nolite fieri sic̃ equus & mulus..."; quoting Psalm 32:9 (Vulgate 31:9)</note>. 
**H**e prohibits, therefore, such men from being ensnared by diabolical frauds and of the ways...

<summary>This page explains the medieval theory of "maternal impression"—where a mother's visual experiences during conception influence her offspring—and provides a list of hybrid animals. It concludes with an allegorical warning against the "folly" of the mule, referencing the biblical story of Absalom and the Psalms.</summary>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moralizing discourse on the horse and the mule, specifically focusing on the necessity of restraint and the allegorical meaning of the bit and bridle. It then transitions into the entry for the badger.</meta>

...laden with burdens: lest by obeying poorly they are more likely to be surrendered to pride. Truly, this must be done for such as are said to be "imprudent animals" <note>original: "animalibus imprudentibus"</note>; for by these comparisons, foolish men are rebuked with a common reproach. For that which is said of the gold in the bit <note>original: "freno"</note>: it pertains to the horse. For the bit is so called from "holding the wild" <note>original: "frenum enim a fero tenendo dictum est"; the author uses a folk etymology suggesting that the word for bit (*frenum*) comes from the phrase "holding the wild horse" (*ferum*)</note>, for the ancients called the horse "the wild one." "In the muzzle" <note>original: "in chamo"; a reference to Psalm 31:9 (Vulgate), which advises using a bit and bridle to restrain those who lack understanding</note>: this refers to the mule. And these two aforementioned animals are restrained by these tethers so that they may walk at the will of the one commanding, lest they be carried away by their own desires.

The jaws, indeed, are the instruments of animals by which food is chewed so that the life of the body may be sustained. Therefore, it is said through an allegorical figure <note>original: "per figuram allegoricam"</note> that the jaws of the disobedient ought to be constricted. That is: food supplies should be given more sparingly, so that, constrained by the necessity of fasting <note>original: "ieiuniorum"</note>, they may be subjected to the command of the Creator.

## Taxus

<image-desc>A framed miniature painting with a red outer border and a green inner background. It depicts four badgers (taxus) engaged in an activity related to their burrows. One badger lies on its back inside a large, circular, golden-colored hole or mound filled with swirling patterns, clutching a wooden bar with its paws. Three other badgers are positioned outside the hole; two of them appear to be pulling or holding the wooden bar, while the third stands at the bottom. This illustrates the medieval legend of badgers using one of their own as a "wagon" to transport earth out of their burrows.</image-desc>

There is the badger <note>original: "taxus"</note>, which is also called the *melota* <note>original: "melota"; likely derived from the Greek "meles" for badger</note>. This animal is biting and unclean; it frequents mountainous and rocky places. It seeks subterranean pits as places of refuge and defenses...

<meta>This page continues the description of the badger (taxus) from the previous page, specifically detailing its communal digging habits. It then introduces the sections for the cat (musio) and the mouse (mus), following the encyclopedic tradition of Isidore of Seville.</meta>

...with their feet they scrape and dig, shaping the home for themselves. Some, indeed, ready to serve the command of nature <note>original: "nature imperio seruire parati"</note>, carry the earth that has been dug by others. Lying on their backs, they embrace the veins of the earth <note>This likely refers to the clumps of excavated soil</note> with all four feet. With a piece of wood placed crosswise in the mouth, held on both sides by the teeth of other badgers who walk backward and pull, they drag the load away—much to the wonder of those who encounter them.

<image-desc>A square miniature containing a circular medallion with a red background dotted with white clusters. Inside the circle, three cats are depicted. Two on the left are a pale grey/white color, sitting upright. The third cat on the right is yellow with darker stripes, crouched down and holding a small grey mouse in its front paws.</image-desc>

The mouse-catcher <term>Musio: a medieval name for the cat, literally "mouse-er"</term> is so called because it is hostile to mice <note>original: "muribus infestus sit"</note>. The common people call this animal a "cat" <note>original: "catum"</note> from the word for "capture" <note>original: "captura"</note>. Others say it is named because it "catches" <note>original: "captat"</note>, meaning it sees. For it perceives so keenly that it overcomes the darkness of night with the brilliance of its eyes. Hence the name comes from the Greek word for "ingenious" <note>original: "catus id: est ingeniosus"</note>, or from the phrase "to burn" <note>original: "ἀπὸ τοῦ καίεσθαι" (apo tou kaiesthai); a reference to the glowing, "burning" quality of a cat's eyes in the dark</note>. <gloss>shining/beholding <note>original: "beszay"; likely a vernacular Middle High German gloss related to "bescheinen" (to shine upon) or "besien" (to behold)</note></gloss>

---

<image-desc>A square miniature containing a circular medallion with a solid red background. A dark grey mouse is shown in profile facing right, crouching over four light-colored round objects (likely grain or bread) and nibbling on one of them.</image-desc>

**M**use <note>original: "Mus"</note> is a tiny animal. Its name is Greek in origin, but whatever is derived from it becomes Latin. Others say the mouse is so named because it is born from the moisture of the earth <note>original: "ex humore terrae nascatur"; the author is making a folk etymology linking "mus" to "humus" (earth) and "humor" (moisture)</note>. For *mus* means earth, from which we also get the word for ground <note>original: "humus"</note>. It is said that the mouse's liver grows with the fullness of the moon, just as certain creatures of the sea increase and then waste away as the moon wanes. The shrew <note>original: "Sorex"</note> is its Latin name because it gnaws <note>original: "rodat"</note> and plunders in the manner of a saw <note>original: "serrae"</note>. The ancients, however, used to say *saurice* for *sorice* (shrew), just as they said *Clodius* for *Claudius*. In a mystical sense <note>original: "Mistice"</note>, mice signify men who gape after earthly greed and plunder...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of small creatures from the previous page. It follows the common medieval tradition of combining natural history with moral allegory.</meta>

...stealing from the substance of others <note>This sentence fragment completes the description of mice from the previous page, characterizing them as thieves.</note>.

### The Weasel

The weasel is so named as if it were a "long mouse" <note>original: "mustela dicitur quasi mus longum"</note>, for the Greeks call "long" *theon* <note>The author provides a folk etymology; the Greek word for long is actually 'dolichos', but 'theon' may be a corruption found in medieval manuscripts</note>. This animal is cunning by nature. When it has given birth to its young in a house, it moves them from place to place and settles in a new spot. It even pursues serpents and women.

<image-desc>Circular miniature with a blue border and red interior depicting a white weasel biting a winged green serpent.</image-desc>

**The Weasel.** There are two types of these animals. One is the wild kind, which differs in size, and which the Greeks call *ictidas* <term>ictidas: likely referring to the marten or a similar larger mustelid</term>; the other kind wanders in houses. Some say that they conceive through the ear and give birth through the mouth, or vice versa <note>This was a common medieval myth used to symbolize those who hear the word of God but "give birth" to it incorrectly or perversely</note>. They are also said to be skilled in medicine, so that if their young happen to be killed, they are able to find them and bring them back to life. These creatures signify people who indeed listen willingly to the divine seed <note>meaning the Word of God</note>, but being held back by their love for earthly things, they pervert what they have heard and hide it. The weasel is a figure of a thief, as is shown in the Book of Leviticus <note>Leviticus 11:29 lists the weasel as an unclean animal</note>.

### The Mole

The mole is so called because it is condemned to perpetual blindness in the darkness <note>The name "talpa" was often linked to the Latin 'talpa' meaning blind, or 'tenebrae' meaning darkness</note>. It is without eyes; it always digs the earth and throws up the soil, and it eats the roots beneath the crops. The Greeks call this creature *aphala* <term>aphala: a medieval corruption of the Greek word 'aspalax', meaning mole</term>.

<image-desc>Square miniature containing a circular image of a dark mole on the ground under a small star.</image-desc>

**The Mole.** The mole, which is condemned to perpetual blindness, signifies either idols—which are all deaf and mute—or even idolaters themselves, who always wander in the darkness of ignorance and folly. Of these it is written in Isaiah <note>Isaiah 2:20</note> that they "worship moles and bats"—that is, they worship earthly and dark things. Elsewhere the Scripture says of them: "Let those who make them become like them, and all who trust in them" <note>Psalm 115:8</note>. It may also signify heretics or false teachers...

<summary>This page describes the physical traits and folk beliefs regarding the weasel and the mole, interpreting their behaviors as allegories for spiritual thievery, improper hearing of the Word, and the spiritual blindness of idolaters.</summary>
<keywords>weasel, mustela, mole, talpa, animal symbolism, medieval bestiary, Leviticus, Isaiah, allegory of blindness</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing descriptions of small animals, concluding the entry on the mole (begun on the previous page) and transitioning into the dormouse (glis) and the hedgehog (ericius). It maintains the medieval tradition of "Physiologus," where animal behaviors—real or legendary—are used to illustrate Christian vices and virtues.</meta>

...pervert what they have heard. <note>This sentence fragment concludes the description of the weasel from the previous page.</note>

**The mole** <note>original: "talpa"</note> represents such Christians through a symbolic figure. For just as the mole, being without eyes, always digs into the earth, casts up the soil, and eats the roots beneath the crops, so too do these people, deprived of the light of true knowledge, involve themselves in earthly deeds. They serve the desires of the flesh and the enticements of pleasures; they strive to gnaw at and subvert the roots of good works in whomever they can with the tooth of malice.

<margin>Dormice</margin>
<image-desc>A circular medallion framed within a blue square. Inside the circle, a small rodent-like animal with a pointed snout and visible ears (a dormouse) is depicted curled up in a sleeping posture against a gold background.</image-desc>
**Dormice** <note>original: "Glires"</note> are so named because sleep makes them grow fat <note>original: "pinguet"</note>. For we use the word *gliscere* <term>gliscere: a Latin verb meaning to grow, swell, or increase</term> to mean "to grow." During the whole winter they sleep and lie immobile as if they were dead; in the summertime they come back to life. These animals can signify those who are lazy and useless in body and do not wish to labor vigorously. Concerning them it is written: *Because of the cold, the sluggard would not plow; therefore he shall beg in the summer and it shall not be given to him* <note>A reference to Proverbs 20:4</note>. This is because he who now grows sluggish and idle during the time of this present life will beg in vain on the Day of Judgment, when it is the time of the harvest. Such a person will in no way share the joys of the heavenly life with the righteous.

At the bottom of the frame, three hedgehogs are shown in profile. They have prominent quills, and some are carrying small green grapes or fruit stuck to their spines, a common motif in medieval bestiaries illustrating how the ericius (hedgehog) gathers food.</image-desc>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the medieval bestiary's exploration of animals as moral allegories. Following the entry on the dormouse, which warned against laziness, this text introduces the hedgehog (ericius). It uses the animal's physical defenses and legendary behaviors—such as carrying grapes on its quills and predicting the wind—to discuss both the cunning of sinners and the need for spiritual refuge in Christ. The page concludes with the heading for the ant (formica).</meta>

xviij. 32

**The hedgehog** <note>original: "Hiricius"</note> is an animal covered in spines. It is named *ericius* <note>a Latin name for the hedgehog</note> because it raises itself up <note>original: "subrigit se"</note> when it is enclosed within its own thorns, by which it is protected on all sides against traps. For as soon as it senses anything nearby, it first raises its quills and, having turned into a ball, gathers itself into its own weapons. This animal possesses a certain cleverness. For when it has cut a grape from the vine, it rolls itself onto its back on top of it, and in this way, it carries the fruit to its young. It is also called an *echinus* <note>original: "echinus", a term derived from Greek meaning hedgehog or sea urchin</note>. This same hedgehog, foreseeing the future, prepares two breathing holes for itself so that when it perceives the north wind <note>original: "boreas"</note> has risen, it blocks the northern opening; when it realizes the south wind <note>original: "notus"</note> is clearing the mist from the air, it moves to the northern side so that it may avoid the opposing blasts and the nightly winds from that region. For the hedgehog mystically signifies sinners full of the prickles of vices, skilled in the cunning of wickedness, and in frauds and robberies—those who seek food for themselves through deceit from the labors of others. Of whom it is written: *There the hedgehog has its den, and shall nourish its young* <note>A reference to Isaiah 34:15</note>. Likewise, there is that which is written in the Psalm: *The rock is a refuge for the hedgehogs* <note>Psalm 104:18 (Vulgate 103:18). While modern translations often identify this animal as a rock hyrax, medieval Latin Bibles used "erinaciis" or hedgehogs.</note>. The *erinacius* is the same as the *hiricius*. It is a completely timid animal, always armed by its prudent nature, for whom the entrance to its den is most sharply and densely fortified. However, natural fortification is not enough for it; rather, so that it cannot be caught by some trick, it always has a refuge among the rocks. To this animal is fittingly compared the person who is bristly with his sins; fearing divine judgments, he should know that he has a most firm refuge in Christ, the Rock. **The ant.**

<summary>This page describes the hedgehog, detailing its defensive curling, its legendary habit of carrying grapes on its quills, and its ability to predict the wind. These traits are used as allegories for the cunning of sinners and the Christian's need for Christ as a "rock" of refuge.</summary>
<keywords>hedgehog, ericius, echinus, erinacius, symbolism, vice, Christ as the Rock, animal allegory, medieval bestiary, Isaiah 34:15, Psalm 104:18, ant</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the entry on the hedgehog to the entry on the ant (formica). It follows the typical structure of a medieval bestiary, moving from a physical description and etymology of the animal to a tripartite allegorical interpretation focused on spiritual diligence and the interpretation of scripture.</meta>

# The Ant

<margin>f</margin>
**The Ant** <note>original: "Formica"</note> is so named because it carries <note>original: "ferat"</note> crumbs <note>original: "micas"</note> of grain <note>original: "farris"</note>. It possesses great cleverness; for it provides for the future and prepares in the summer what it may eat in the winter. In the harvest, however, it chooses wheat and does not touch barley; even while the rain falls upon all the grain.

It is said that in Ethiopia there are ants shaped like dogs, which dig up golden sands with their feet; they guard these so that no one may take them away, and they pursue those who would seize them even unto death.

The ant has three natures. The **first nature** is that they walk in an orderly fashion, and each of them carries a grain in its mouth. And those that are empty-handed do not say, "Give us some of your grains," but they follow the footsteps of those who came before until they reach the place where they find grain, and they carry the grain back to their nest. Let these things said regarding the sign of prudence <note>The ant was a traditional symbol of wisdom and foresight in the Middle Ages</note> suffice, because just as the ants gather so they do not go without reward in the future.

The **second nature** is that when it stores the grains in its nest, it divides them in two, lest by chance the winter rains soak them, causing the grains to germinate and the ant to die of hunger. In the same way, O man, you should divide the Old and New Testament—that is, distinguish between the spiritual and the carnal meanings—lest the literal "letter" of the text kill you. For the law is spiritual, as the Apostle says: *For the letter kills, but the spirit gives life* <note>A quotation from 2 Corinthians 3:6</note>. Indeed, the Jews, attending only to the letter and despising the spiritual understanding, were killed by hunger <note>This refers to a spiritual hunger or lack of divine grace, a common trope in medieval Christian commentary</note>.

The **third nature** is: in the time of harvest, the ant walks among the crops, and by its mouth it knows whether a spike is barley or wheat. If it is barley, it passes to another stalk and smells it; and if it senses that it is wheat, it climbs to the top of the spike and, taking a grain from there, carries it to its dwelling. For barley is the food of brute animals. Whence Job says: *Instead of wheat, let barley grow for me* <note>Job 31:40</note>... namely the barley of doctrine...

<summary>This page describes the ant, focusing on its etymology, the legendary dog-sized ants of Ethiopia, and three "natures" that serve as allegories for spiritual wisdom and the correct interpretation of the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>ant, formica, Ethiopia, wheat, barley, allegory, Saint Paul, Job, spiritual vs. literal, prudence</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the ant's behavior. The previous page ended discussing how the ant chooses wheat over barley, which the author uses as a metaphor for distinguishing between spiritual truth and the "barley" of heresy or a purely literal (carnal) understanding of the Bible.</meta>

...of heretics. For barley grains <note>original: "Ordeacee"</note> are indeed like these teachings and must be cast far away; they break and corrupt the souls of men. Flee therefore, O Christian, all heretics, whose dogmas are false and enemies to the truth.

The Scripture says: "Go to the ant, O sluggard: imitate her ways and be wiser than her." <note>A reference to Proverbs 6:6</note> For she possesses no fields to cultivate, nor does she have anyone to compel her, nor does she act under a master; nevertheless, she prepares her food, storing up the harvest from her labors for herself.

Observe how, though you are often in need, she does not lack. For her, no granaries are locked, no guards are impenetrable, no heaps of grain are forbidden. The human guard watches the "thefts" of the ant, which he does not dare to prevent; the owner looks upon his losses and does not seek revenge. Instead, the prey is carried off in a marching line; the paths teem with the company of travelers, and the grains that cannot be gripped by their narrow mouths are carried on their shoulders. The Lord of the harvest watches this and blushes to deny the rewards of such thrifty and pious industry to such a small creature.

The ant also knows how to seek out times of clear weather. For when the rains have come and its fruits have become damp, having diligently examined the air to see when it might preserve the mildness of the atmosphere, it opens its heaps and carries the grain out from its hollows on its shoulders, so that its own corn may be dried by the constant sun. Finally, you will never see rain break from the clouds during those days until the ant has first recalled its fruits back into the granaries.

**Frogs** <note>original: "Rane"</note> are so named from their chatter <note>original: "garrulitate"</note>, because they make a noise in the narrow marshes where they are born, giving back the sound of their voice with troublesome clamors. Frogs represent demons in the Apocalypse: "I saw from the mouth of the dragon..." <note>A reference to Revelation 16:13, where frogs are linked to unclean spirits</note>

...at [that] time into the world as if they are of demons. These heretics, who dwell in the mire of the most base senses, do not cease to boast with empty chatter, as in Exodus.

<margin>Salamander.</margin>

There is an animal called a <unclear>sea</unclear> <note>This may be a scribal abbreviation or error for *salamandra* or *steli*</note>, which is called a *salamandra* in Greek, and in Latin a *stellio* <note>original: "stellio", typically referring to a spotted lizard or newt</note>. This is like a small lizard of varied color, of which Solomon says: "Just as the spotted lizard dwells in the houses of kings." <note>A reference to Proverbs 30:28</note>. The *Physiologus* <note>An ancient Greek text, popular in the Middle Ages, that provided allegorical and moral descriptions of animals</note> says of it that if by chance or in any way it should fall into a furnace of burning fire, or into a kiln of fire, or into any blaze, the fire is immediately extinguished.

In this way, the righteous are wonders to all men, just as Ananias, Azarias, and Misahel <note>The three Hebrew youths—also known as Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego—who were thrown into a furnace by King Nebuchadnezzar</note> were in the furnace of burning fire; the fire did not touch them at all. Daniel clearly declares that they emerged from the furnace of fire untouched and undefiled. And the Apostle Paul testifies, saying: "We suffer tribulations, but not distress; we are cast down, but we do not perish." <note>original: "Tribulationes patimur sȝ ñ angustiam. detamur sed non inimimur," a variation of 2 Corinthians 4:8-9</note> Also: "They stopped the mouths of lions and extinguished the force of fire." <note>Hebrews 11:33-34</note>.

So also everyone who believes in God with their whole faith and perseveres in good works shall pass through the fire of <term>Gehenna</term> <note>A term for hellfire or a place of purification</note>, and the flame shall not touch them, as the prophet Isaiah says: "If you pass through the fire, the flame shall not burn you." <note>Isaiah 43:2</note> Also concerning the same: it is called a "salamander" because it is strong against burning. Among all poisons, its power is the greatest. For others indeed [strike] individuals... <note>The text breaks off here at "fer", likely the beginning of "ferire" (to strike/hit) or "ferunt" (they carry)</note>

<summary>This page describes the salamander as a lizard-like creature capable of extinguishing fire, comparing it to the biblical figures Ananias, Azarias, and Misahel. It uses this physical trait as an allegory for how the faithful can endure the "fires" of tribulation and hell without being harmed.</summary>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page concludes the entry on the salamander, describing its legendary poisonous nature and its immunity to fire, before transitioning into a general introduction to the nature of birds. The previous page established the salamander as a symbol of the righteous who remain unharmed by the "fires" of temptation or persecution.</meta>

...[these creatures] kill many people in a similar way, but in vain. For if a salamander creeps into a tree, it infects all the fruit with poison, and it kills those who eat from that same fruit. And if it should even fall into a well, its poison kills those who drink from it. These creatures alone among animals fight against and extinguish fires; for they live in the midst of flames without pain or being consumed. Not only are they not burned, but they actually put out the blaze.

**Birds.** <gloss>Here begins the section on birds</gloss>

There is, however, only one name for birds, but their kind is diverse. For just as they differ from one another in appearance, so they do in the diversity of their nature. For some are simple, like doves; others are clever, like the partridge. Some submit themselves to the hand of man, like the hawk; others are terrified of it, like the Garamantes <note>likely referring to a bird associated with the Garamantes, a North African tribe often mentioned in ancient geographies</note>. Some delight in the company <note>original: "gūlatione", likely a scribal error for "conversatione" or "cohabitatione"</note> of men, like the swallow; others love their secret places in the deserts, like the turtle-dove. Some feed only on seeds they find, like the goose; others eat flesh and live by plunder, like the kite <term>milvus</term> <note>a medium-sized bird of prey in the hawk family</note>.

Some are congregational—that is, flying in flocks—like starlings and quails. Others are solitary wanderers—that is, they fly alone because of their predatory ambushes—like the eagle and the hawk and others of that kind. Some make a noise with their voices, like the swallow; others produce the sweetest songs, like the swan <note>The OCR reads "agnus" (lamb), but the context of birds and bestiary traditions suggests "cygnus" (swan)</note> and the blackbird. Some imitate the words and voices of men, like the parrot and the magpie. But other types are innumerable, both in their kind and in their habits. For no one is able to discover just how many kinds of birds there are. Indeed, who could penetrate all the deserts of Scythia, India, or Ethiopia to know their species or their differences?

Birds <note>Latin: *aves*</note> are so named because they do not have fixed paths, but run through "pathless ways" <note>Latin: *avia*; a medieval folk etymology linking *aves* (birds) to *avia* (pathless places)</note>. They are called "winged ones" <note>Latin: *alites*</note> because they seek the heights and ascend to sublime places with the "rowing of their wings" <note>original: "remigio alarum", a poetic metaphor for flight</note>. "Flying creatures" <note>Latin: *volucres*</note> comes from "flying" <note>Latin: *volando*</note>. For from "to fly" comes also "to walk"...

<summary>This page concludes the description of the salamander's legendary poisonous and fire-extinguishing traits before providing an encyclopedic overview of the diverse natures, habits, and etymologies of birds.</summary>
<keywords>salamander, birds, dove, partridge, hawk, swallow, turtle-dove, goose, kite, eagle, swan, blackbird, parrot, magpie, etymology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of birds following the introduction of the category. It transitions from physical descriptions and etymologies of anatomical parts (wings, feathers) to the allegorical and scriptural interpretations of birds as symbols of angels, men, and the Gospel.</meta>

<margin>
John Bullock 
of Maiden. <note>This marginal note appears to be a later ownership mark or signature, unrelated to the Latin text.</note>
</margin>

...let us speak again of these things. The **hollow** <term>vola</term> <note>original: "vola"; referring to the palm of the hand or sole of the foot</note> is said to be the middle part of the foot or the hand; and in birds, it is the middle part of the wings, by whose movement the feathers are set in motion. From this comes the word for **birds** <term>volucres</term> <note>literally "flyers," related to the verb "volare," to fly</note>. The young of all birds are called **chicks** <term>pulli</term>; however, the young of four-footed animals are also called chicks. Even a small human is called a "little chick" <note>original: "homo paruus pullus"</note>. They are called chicks <term>pulli</term> because they are "polluted" <note>original: "polluti"; a medieval etymological play linking "pullus" (chick) to "pollutus" (stained/moist), referring to the state of a newborn</note> when they are freshly born. From this, black clothing is also called **dark** <term>pulla</term>. 

**Wings** <term>alae</term> are those parts in which the feathers are arranged in order to provide the use of flying. They are called wings <term>alae</term> because birds nourish <note>original: "alant"; the author links "ala" (wing) to "alere" (to nourish/rear)</note> and cherish their chicks by embracing them with these. The **feather** <term>penna</term> is named from "hanging" <note>original: "pendendo"</note>, or rather from "flying away" <note>original: "avolando"</note>; hence the word "to hang." For birds move by the help of feathers when they entrust themselves to the air. **Plumage** <term>pluma</term> is, as it were, "hair-like" <note>original: "piluma," a made-up term linking "pluma" (feather) to "pilus" (hair)</note>. For just as there are hairs on the body of quadrupeds, so there is plumage on birds. 

The names of many birds are formed from the sound of their voice, such as the **crane** <note>original: "grus"</note>, the **crow** <note>original: "corvus"</note>, the **swan** <note>original: "agnus" in OCR, but likely "cygnus" in the source manuscript given the context of bird names</note>, the **owl** <note>original: "bubo"</note>, the **kite** <note>original: "milvus"</note>, the **screech-owl** <note>original: "ulula"</note>, the **goldfinch** <note>original: "aciulus," likely "acalanthis"</note>, the **jackdaw** <note>original: "graculus"</note>, and others. Indeed, the variety of their voices teaches that they should be so named by men.

**Birds represent angels**, just as it is said in the Book of Job regarding the origin of wisdom: "The birds of the air have nests" <note>referencing Job 28:21 and Matthew 8:20</note>. Birds also represent **proud men**, as in the Psalter: "Sheep and oxen and all the beasts of the field, the birds of the heaven and the fish of the sea" <note>Psalm 8:7-8</note>. 

"Flying things" <term>volatilia</term> represent the **Apostles** or all the saints in the Gospel: "My bulls and my fatted calves <note>original: "altilia"; referring to fattened birds or animals</note> are killed" <note>Matthew 22:4</note>; these are the preachers of the New and Old Testaments. The flight of birds is the **ascent to God**, as understood in the Scriptures and in the Psalter: "I will fly away and be at rest" <note>Psalm 55:6</note>. 

Furthermore, birds or flyers have diverse meanings. For sometimes they signify **angels**, as in that passage: "The birds of heaven shall announce his voice" <note>Ecclesiastes 10:20</note>. Secondly, they sometimes signify **men**, as in the Lord’s testimony concerning the grain of mustard seed, which is the smallest of all herbs but becomes a tree, so that "the birds come and dwell in its branches" <note>Matthew 13:31-32</note>. The preaching of the Gospel is the smallest [of seeds], and before the eyes of the wise [worldly] disciplines...

...then [those] of this world. But it grows into a tree: so that the birds of heaven—whom we should understand as either the souls of believers or the heavenly powers devoted to the service of God <note>The author refers to angels as "fortitudines," or powers</note>—

# The Eagle.

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature from a medieval bestiary framed within a decorative border of red, orange, and white bands. A dark sun with stylized red rays is positioned at the top center of the frame. The interior background of the frame features a blue diapered pattern of small quatrefoils. The image depicts three eagles: one stands at the top right with wings partly raised; another dives head-first toward green wavy mounds at the bottom; a third eagle on the left is shown in profile holding a fish in its talons. The miniature is followed by a paragraph of text beginning with a large decorative initial 'A' in blue and red filigree.</image-desc>

The Eagle <term>Aquila</term> is so named because of the sharpness <term>acumine</term> of its eyes. For its gaze is said to be so great that when it is carried above the seas on motionless wings, invisible to human sight, it can see little fish swimming from such a great height. Then, descending like a missile <term>tormenti instar</term> <note>original: "tormenti"; referring to a catapult or engine of war</note>, it drags its seized prey to the shore with its wings. But when the eagle grows old, its wings become heavy and its eyes are shrouded in a mist <term>caligine</term>. Then...

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the entry for the Eagle, specifically detailing the legendary process by which it renews its youth and how it tests the legitimacy of its offspring using the sun. It concludes by transitioning to the entry for the Barnacle Goose.</meta>

<page-num>.XII.</page-num>

...it seeks a spring, and flying toward it, it soars high up into the very atmosphere of the sun. There, it sets its wings on fire and similarly burns away the heavy mist from its eyes in the sun’s rays. Then, finally descending to the spring, it plunges itself three times into the water, and immediately it is renewed with great strength in its wings and brilliance in its eyes. So it is for you, O man, who wear the "old garments" <note>An allusion to the "old man" or the sinful self in Pauline theology (Ephesians 4:22).</note> and find the eyes of your heart clouded: seek the spiritual spring of the Lord and lift the eyes of your mind to God, who is the fountain of justice. Then your youth shall be renewed like the eagle’s. <note>A reference to Psalm 103:5: "so that your youth is renewed like the eagle's."</note>

It is also asserted that the eagle holds its chicks up to the rays of the sun, suspending them by a claw in mid-air. If any chick, by the reflected light of the sun, maintains an intrepid gaze with unhurt and vigorous sight, it is proven to have demonstrated the truth of its nature. But those whose eyes turn away from the sun’s rays are rejected as degenerate and unworthy of so great a father; nor is a chick deemed worthy of being raised if it was found unworthy of its initial acceptance. Thus, the eagle condemns its young not through a bitterness of nature, but through the integrity of judgment. It does not truly abdicate its own, but rather refuses them as if they were alien. This ruthlessness of the "royal bird" <term>regalis auis</term> <note>The eagle was traditionally considered the king of birds in medieval animal lore.</note>, as it may seem to some, is excused by the mercy of a common bird. <note>Medieval bestiaries often claim the Coot (fulica) or the Osprey would adopt the chicks rejected by the eagle.</note>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of the eagle, transitioning into one of the most famous and debated sections of Gerald of Wales’s work: the nature of the Barnacle Goose. The "Nothoker" inscription mentioned in the OCR metadata may refer to a later owner or librarian of the manuscript.</meta>

There are in Ireland many birds which are called "barnacles" <note>original: "bernace"; these are Barnacle Geese (*Branta leucopsis*). In the Middle Ages, they were famously thought to grow from driftwood rather than hatch from eggs.</note> which nature produces in a wonderful way against the usual laws of nature. They are birds indeed similar to marsh-geese <note>The OCR reads "plaustribus," but this is likely a scribal variant or error for "palustribus," meaning marsh-dwelling birds.</note>, but smaller. From fir-wood cast upon the sea, they are born at first like gum. From then on, as if from seaweed adhering to the wood, and enclosed in shells for a freer formation, they hang by their beaks. And thus they remain until, in the fullness of time, they put on a firm covering of feathers and either fall into the waters or transfer themselves into the freedom of the air by flight. From the juice of the wood and the sea, by a hidden and most wonderful process of generation, they receive both nourishment and growth. 

I have many times seen with my own eyes more than a thousand minute little bodies of birds of this kind on the seashore, hanging from a single piece of wood, enclosed in shells and already formed. Eggs are not produced from their mating as is usual. No bird ever sits on eggs in their preparation. In no corner of the earth are they seen either to indulge in lust or to build nests. Wherefore, in some parts of Ireland, bishops and religious men are accustomed to eat these birds without distinction during times of fasting, as if they were not flesh because they are not born of flesh. <note>This was a controversial practice. Since the geese were thought to be "fish-adjacent" or born from wood, some argued they could be eaten on Fridays and during Lent. Later Church rulings eventually banned this.</note>

Come to your senses, unhappy Jew! <note>Gerald here shifts into a "polemic," a common medieval style of argument using nature to prove Christian doctrine. He is addressing a perceived Jewish rejection of the Virgin Birth.</note> Come to your senses, even if late! You do not dare to deny the truth of the Law regarding the first generation of man from clay without male and female <note>A reference to the creation of Adam.</note>. The second generation was from the male without the female <note>A reference to Eve being formed from Adam’s rib.</note>. The third, only from male and female, because it is usual, you approve and affirm with a stiff neck. But the fourth, in which alone is salvation—namely, from the female without the male <note>The Virgin Birth of Jesus.</note>—you detest in your obstinate malice to your own destruction. Blush, wretch, blush! And at least return to nature: which, for arguments of faith and for our instruction, daily prepares and produces new animals without any male or female. The first generation, therefore, was from clay.

<summary>Gerald of Wales describes the mythical origin of Barnacle Geese from driftwood and shells, using their "fleshless" birth to justify eating them during fasts and as a theological argument for the Virgin Birth.</summary>
<keywords>Barnacle Geese, Ireland, Gerald of Wales, medieval biology, Lenten fasting, Virgin Birth, Adam and Eve, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the Barnacle Goose, which the author uses as a springboard for a philosophical reflection on the nature of "wonder." It then transitions into a description of the Osprey (Aurifrigius), a bird the author claims has two different types of feet—one for grasping and one for swimming.</meta>

.Lxxxj.

...and this last one is born from wood. The former <note>Likely referring to the miracles of the Bible (like Adam from dust) mentioned on the previous page.</note> indeed is an astounding event because it was performed by the Lord of Nature only once. This one [the bird], however, is no less wonderful, yet it is less to be wondered at because "nature the imitator" <note>original: *imitatrix natura*; a common medieval idea that nature mimics divine creation through physical processes.</note> provides it often. For human nature is so composed that it considers nothing to be worthy of wonder unless it is unusual, rare, or precious. The sun, whether at its rising or setting—than which nothing in the world is more beautiful, nothing more worthy of amazement—passes by without any wonder because we see it daily. Yet at an eclipse of the sun, because it happens rarely, the whole world is struck with awe.

->**The Osprey**<-
<note>original: *Aurifrigius*; likely a corruption of the Latin *ossifragus* (bone-breaker), but used here to describe the Osprey or Sea-hawk.</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular illustration framed by a thick red border depicting three ospreys (aurifrigii) interacting with a body of water. The birds are rendered in shades of grey and brown with detailed plumage. The background is a pale peach color, decorated with small red and white six-pointed star motifs. At the bottom, stylized green wavy lines represent water containing three visible fish. The bird on the left is diving downwards with wings spread, clutching a fish in its talons. The central bird is flying away from the viewer with its back and wings exposed. The bird on the right stands or floats on the water's surface with its wings partially extended. The scene is likely a depiction of the osprey's hunting behavior as described in medieval bestiaries.</image-desc>

There are also many birds there of a dual nature <note>original: *biformis nature*</note> which they call "ospreys." They are indeed smaller than an eagle but larger than a hawk. They have one foot armed with claws, fit for grasping and rapacious, but the other is webbed <note>original: *clausum*, literally "closed."</note> and peaceful, suitable only for swimming: a work of "nature at play" <note>original: *nature ludentis*; the idea that nature creates biological oddities for its own amusement.</note> that is deliberately wonderful. It is a wonder concerning these birds that they see so much with their eyes. For at the very height of the air above the sea waves, so that they may freely fix the sharpness of their eyes on the depths, they hold and sustain themselves by the rowing of their wings. And when they [see] fish lurking in the depths of the waves, through such a turbulence of air and water...

<summary>Gerald of Wales reflects on how familiarity breeds indifference to nature's daily wonders before describing the Osprey as a bird with one predatory clawed foot and one webbed foot for swimming.</summary>

<keywords>Osprey, nature the imitator, nature at play, sun, eclipse, medieval zoology, Ireland, vision</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical description of the Osprey (Aurifrigius) from the previous page, focusing on its hunting methods as a metaphor for spiritual danger, before introducing the Kingfisher (Martineta) found in Ireland.</meta>

they are spotted by the thinness of their outline: they cast themselves downward with their own characteristic speed. And while they dive and emerge, they steer themselves in the waters with a swimming foot <note>Referring to the webbed foot mentioned on the previous page.</note>. However, with their grasping foot, they pull out and carry away their prey as victors. In this same way, the ancient enemy <note>The "ancient enemy" (original: *hostis antiquus*) is a standard medieval term for the Devil.</note> watches us in the turbulent waves of this world, regarding whatever hidden things we do with a sharp and clear gaze. And while he enters into the successes of temporal life smoothly, as if with a peaceful foot, he finally seizes, bloodies, and scatters wretched souls like prey with a grasping foot.

->**The Kingfisher.**<-
<note>original: *Martinete*; likely referring to the Common Kingfisher, *Alcedo atthis*.</note>

<image-desc>A framed miniature depicts two stylized, multi-colored trees on green mounds against a pale background decorated with small orange and white star-like patterns. Several birds, colored in shades of blue and brown, are shown perched on the branches or flying between the trees. The frame has a simple blue border.</image-desc>

**T**here are small birds in Ireland <note>original: *Hybernia*</note> which they call kingfishers, which are smaller than blackbirds <note>original: *merulis*</note>. They are common there, though rare elsewhere, and live by rivers. In the manner of a quail <note>original: *coturnicis*</note>, they dive into the water after the tiny little fish upon which they feed. And although in all other respects they maintain a single nature, they vary in color alone. For they are conspicuous and beautiful, some with a white belly and black [back], and others shining with the greenness of a painted peacock <note>original: *pauo*</note>. If these birds are kept in a dry place after they have died, they do not know how to rot; and when placed among clothes or any other fabrics, they keep them safe from moths <note>original: *tinea*</note> and make them fragrant <note>original: *odoriferas*</note>. And what is even more... <note>The text here draws from Giraldus Cambrensis's 12th-century work *Topographia Hibernica* (The Topography of Ireland).</note>

<summary>This page concludes a moral allegory comparing the hunting tactics of the Osprey to the Devil's entrapment of souls, then begins a description of the Irish Kingfisher, noting its vibrant colors and the medieval belief that its dried carcass prevents rot and moths.</summary>

...ously worthy of amazement. When suspended by their beaks in a dry place, they are renewed every year by the revival of their plumage and a renovation. It is as if a vital spirit, remaining in part, still animates the hidden remains. Thus, those who are dead to the whole world, kindled by the fire of charity <note>original: *caritas*</note> and placed, as it were, in a dry place, soon put on incorruption. They purge and refine the vices from their deeds through zeal for good works, both for themselves and those joined to them, and make them praiseworthy by the good scent of virtues. And while they are suspended on high with the whole intention of the mind, casting off the old and with new increases of virtues, they are renewed for the better day by day and are transformed—utterly stripping off the **old man** and putting on the **new** <note>A reference to the Pauline epistles (Ephesians 4:22-24), symbolizing spiritual rebirth.</note>. Indeed, the greatest progress of praise is whenever the following times run together.

### The Coot.

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature with a red and black border. It depicts two white birds, identified as fulicae (coots), in a stylized green and yellow wavy body of water. The background is a dark red field decorated with small white circles. The bird on the left is swimming, while the bird on the right is shown in a similar environment, possibly near a nest or resting. Both birds have long, curved necks and sharp, pointed beaks.</image-desc>

**T**he bird whose name is the coot <note>original: *fullica*</note>, which is called *phene* <note>original: *fene*; likely from the Greek *phene*, often referring to a bearded vulture or osprey in classical texts, but here used as a synonym for the coot</note> in Greek, joins the eagle chick—whether it has been taken up, rejected, or not recognized—with its own brood and mixes it with its own. With the same duty of maternal diligence that she performs for her own, she feeds and nourishes it with an equal supply of food. Therefore, the *phene* nourish strangers, but we, in truth, deprive our own with harsh cruelty. For if the eagle tests the chick, it is not as if she takes care of her own, but as if she does not recognize it as being of her own kind. We, which is worse, renounce those whom we recognize as our own.

<vocab>fullica: coot, aquila: eagle, fene: Greek term for a bird of prey (here coot), caritas: charity/divine love, rediviva: revived/living again, renovatione: renewal, corruptionem: decay/corruption, susceptionem: taking up/adoption</vocab>

The vulture <note>original: *vultur*</note> is named from "flight" <note>original: *volatu*</note>; or it is thought [to be named] from its "gaze" <note>original: *vultus*</note> during slow flight. Indeed, because of the great size of its body, it does not have [swiftness] in its flight. Vultures, it is said, and even eagles, can sense carcasses even across the seas. Since they fly very high in the sky, they are often hidden by the shadow of the mountains, yet they are seen there by their movement. For it is

<image-desc>A circular miniature illustration set into the right side of the text block. Inside the circle, two stylized vultures with grey and white plumage are depicted against a deep blue background speckled with small red and white dots resembling stars. The circle is bordered by a red and blue frame.</image-desc>

denied that vultures indulge in intercourse, or are joined by the conjugal use of the marriage bond; and thus they conceive without any seed of males and give birth without union. Their offspring proceed into a great longevity of age, so that the course of their life reaches up to a hundred years, and they do not easily expire from the distress of any limit. Why then do those who are accustomed to laugh at our mysteries—when they hear that a virgin has given birth—judge the birth of an unmarried woman (whose modesty no habit of man had violated) to be impossible? It is considered impossible in the mother of God, yet it is not denied to be possible in vultures. A bird gives birth without a male, and no one refutes it; yet because the betrothed Virgin Mary <note>original: *Maria Virgo*</note> gave birth, they make her modesty a matter of dispute. From this let us add that the Lord had established truth from her very nature <note>original: *natura*</note>. Vultures were accustomed to announce the death of men by certain signs. **THE CRANE**

<image-desc>A square miniature with a blue border depicting three blue cranes against a dark red circular background decorated with a pattern of white dots. The background of the square outside the circle is blue with a white filigree pattern. One crane on the left is prominently raising its right foot, clutching a small green stone, a traditional iconographic symbol of vigilance. Two other cranes stand behind and to the right of it.</image-desc>

Cranes <note>original: *Grus*</note> take their name from their own voice, for they murmur with such a sound <note>The name is onomatopoeic, mimicking the "gru-gru" cry of the bird</note>. 

When traveling a long distance, it is remarkable how carefully they direct their expeditions. They march under a kind of military signal. And so that the force of the winds does not blow them back as they head toward their intended land, they swallow sand and weight themselves down to a moderate heaviness with small stones <term>Saburrantur: to be ballasted like a ship; from the Latin 'saburra' meaning sand or ballast</term>. 

Then they strive for the highest heights so that, from a loftier watchtower, they may see which lands they seek. While they hasten, they follow a single leader in a lettered formation <note>original: *litterato*. This refers to the V-shape of migrating cranes, which ancient writers like Palamedes claimed inspired the shapes of certain letters</note>. They proceed with a confident gait ahead of the other flocks. The leader punishes laziness in flight and compels the line with its voice. When it becomes hoarse, another bird takes its place. They perform their duties with such harmonious care that if any bird falters, the others support the tired and weary ones with collective strength until they recover their vigor through rest. 

At night, cranes maintain a vigilant watch. You may see sentries stationed; while the rest of the flock’s companions sleep, others circle and scout, lest any snares or ambushes crawl in from any side, and they maintain their vigilant guard with tireless energy. Later, when the time for their watch has been completed, then...

<summary>This page concludes the entry on vultures, noting their presence at battles, and begins the entry on cranes, focusing on their organized migration, ballast-swallowing for stability, and communal defense.</summary>
<keywords>vultures, cranes, Grus, migration, bird formation, animal symbolism, Isidore of Seville, vigilance</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the crane's communal habits, specifically focusing on how they share the burden of guarding the flock at night, before moving to a new chapter on the parrot (Psittacus). The text continues to follow the natural history traditions of authors like Isidore of Seville and Pliny the Elder.</meta>

...having shared the duty, they settle themselves into sleep once a signal has been given, so that one may watch over the sleepers. The duty of the watch is handed over in turn. But the crane, willingly taking up her lot, does not—according to our human custom—neglect the exchange or succumb to sleep through laziness, but instead diligently carries out her turns. She performs her duty; and because they have received this grace of nature, they are filled with an equal sense of care and obligation. 

Therefore, there is no desertion among them because their devotion is natural. There is but a single guard for the flock, because their will is free. Moreover, at night, cranes divide their watches and establish the order of vigils in sequence and turns. They hold little stones <term>lapillos: small stones or pebbles used as a weighted alarm</term> in their raised toes, which serve to "refute" sleep—if the bird begins to doze, the stone falls and the noise wakes her. If there is something to be feared, a cry indicates the danger. Their age is revealed by their color, for they grow black in their old age.

# Only India sends parrots

<image-desc>A circular miniature (tondo) is inset into the right side of the text block. It features a solid blue background and depicts two bright green parrots perched on a branch that has red floral or budding tips. Both birds have a thin, distinct red collar (the "torque punicee" mentioned in the text) around their necks. One parrot faces left with its back partly turned, while the other faces right, looking toward the first bird.</image-desc>

The parrot <note>original: *psittacus*</note> is green in color, with a scarlet collar <term>torque punicee: a reddish-purple ring around the neck</term> and a large tongue, which is wider than that of other birds. Because of this, it can express articulated words; indeed, if you did not see the bird, you would think a human was speaking. By its nature, it greets people by saying "Hail" or "Welcome" <note>original: *Ave* or *Kere* (the latter from the Greek *chaire*)</note>. Other names it learns through training. From this comes the saying: "I, a parrot, will learn other names from you; this I have learned to say by myself: 'Hail Caesar!'" <note>This refers to a famous epigram by the Roman poet Martial, describing a parrot's ability to learn imperial greetings</note>. 

The hardness of its beak is so great that if it is cast down from a high place onto a rock, it catches itself by the strength of its mouth, using its beak as a kind of foundation because of its firm shape. Its head is so strong that if it ever needs to be corrected with blows while learning—for it strives to speak what humans speak—it must be beaten with an iron rod <note>Medieval bestiaries often claimed parrots were so thick-skulled that only iron could get their attention during training</note>. While it is still a chick, and up to the second year of its life, it learns what it is shown more quickly and retains it more tenaciously.

<vocab>parrot, cranes, india, hail caesar, scarlet collar, pebbles, watches</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the account of the crane's nocturnal vigilance using pebbles to stay awake and introduces the parrot, noted for its ability to mimic human speech and its physical durability.</summary>
<keywords>cranes, vigilance, parrots, India, mimicry, animal behavior, training, Caesar</keywords>

# Caladrius.

<image-desc>A rectangular illuminated miniature with a red and blue border. It depicts a king with a golden crown and halo-like radiance reclining in a bed. He wears a green tunic and is covered by a grey and white patterned blanket. The bed is ornate with turned posts and a red pillow. A large white bird, the Caladrius, stands at the foot of the bed, its head turned away from the king. In the background, a yellowish vessel sits on a shelf. Below the bed, the base is decorated with four-lobed floral motifs.</image-desc>

The Caladrius, as the *Physiologus* <term>Physiologus: an early Christian text that served as the primary source for medieval bestiaries, moralizing the traits of animals</term> says, is a white bird with no part of it being black. Its discharge <note>original: *mictura*; likely referring to a secretion or waste product used in traditional medicine</note> cures the dimness of the eyes. This bird is found in the courts of kings. If someone is suffering from an illness, it can be known from this Caladrius whether they will live or die. If the man’s infirmity is unto death, as soon as the bird sees the sick person, it turns its face away from him, and then everyone knows that he is destined to die. If, however, his infirmity leads toward life, the bird gazes intently into his face and takes all the man’s sickness into itself; it then flies into the air toward the sun, burns up the illness, and scatters it, and the sick man is healed.

The Caladrius represents the person of our Savior <note>original: *salvatoris nostri*</note>. Our Lord is entirely white, having no blackness, for He committed no sin, nor was deceit found in His mouth. But the Lord, coming from on high, turned His face away from the Jews <note>original: *iudeis*</note> because of their unbelief, and He turned Himself toward us Gentiles, taking away our infirmities and carrying our sins. Exalted on the wood of the cross and ascending on high, he led captivity cap... <note>The text ends mid-word, likely *captivitatem*, part of the biblical phrase "led captivity captive" from Psalm 68 or Ephesians 4:8.</note>

Caladrius, Physiologus, Savior, Jews, Cross, Sickness

<summary>This page describes the Caladrius, a mythical white bird that can predict the outcome of a king's illness and heal the sick by carrying their disease toward the sun. The text provides a Christological interpretation, likening the bird's white feathers to Christ's sinlessness and its behavior to the rejection of unbelievers.</summary>

But let it be said that because the Caladrius is "unclean" according to the Law <note>Refers to the Old Testament dietary laws in Leviticus 11:13–19, where the Caladrius—often identified as a plover or heron—is listed as forbidden</note>, it ought not be compared to Christ. The lion and the eagle are also "unclean" animals, yet they are compared to Christ according to their royal dignity, because the lion is the king of beasts and the eagle is the king of birds.

# The Heron

The **Heron** <insert>Heron</insert> is so named because it is "lofty" <note>original: *ardua*; the text links the bird's name, *Ardea*, to the word for high or steep, *ardua*</note> on account of its high flights. For it fears rainstorms and flies above the clouds so that it cannot feel the tempests of rain. When it flies at its highest, it signifies a coming storm. Many call this bird the *tantalus*. Concerning this bird, Rabanus <note>Rabanus Maurus (c. 780–856), a famous Benedictine scholar who wrote about the symbolic meanings of the natural world</note> says: This bird can signify the souls

<image-desc>A circular miniature with a thick red and gold border. Inside, against a red background decorated with white floral/star motifs, a grey heron (Ardea) stands in green water. The heron has a long, curved neck and is holding a small, wriggling eel or snake in its yellow beak. The heron’s head and tail feathers slightly overlap the circular frame.</image-desc>

of the saints or the elect. Fearing the disturbances of this world—lest they be caught in the storms of persecution instigated by the devil—they raise their intention above all temporal things toward the serenity of the heavenly homeland. There, they lift their minds to behold the face of God. Although the heron seeks the heights, it nevertheless places its nest in the woods and in trees. This is because the just man, though his soul is delighted by eternal things, still exists among transitory matters. In the nest, the heron strives to defend its chicks with its beak so that they are not snatched by other birds. In the same way, the just man is prepared with strong rebukes when he knows the perverse are attempting to deceive those under his care. Some herons have a white color, while others are ash-grey. Yet both colors are interpreted positively: purity <term>mundicia</term> is represented by white, and penance <term>penitencia</term> by the grey. For those who do penance and those who live purely are of the same kind. Therefore, the color, the ardor, and the manner of life of this bird provide an exam- <note>The word *exemplum* (example) is cut off at the end of the page</note>

lxIII.
### Swan

...saves the soul for God by religious means.
The **Swan** <note>original: *Olor*</note> is so called because the Greeks call it "pure" <note>original: *agnum*; likely a Latinization of the Greek *hagnon*, meaning pure or holy</note>. It is called *Olor* because it is entirely white in its feathers; for no one remembers a black lamb <note>original: *agnum nigrum*; the text draws a comparison between the white bird and the purity of a white lamb</note>. For in Greek, "all" or "whole" is called *olor* <note>original: *olōn*; a reference to the Greek *holos*</note>. It is also called a **Cygnus** from "singing" <note>original: *canendo*</note>, because it pours forth its voice in the modulated sweetness of song.

<margin>Swan</margin>
<image-desc>A circular miniature framed by a thick red border and a gold inner line. Inside, a white swan stands on three stylized green mounds. The swan has a long, curved neck and holds a small reddish-orange fish in its beak. The background is a blue wash textured with horizontal white streaks.</image-desc>

They say it sings so sweetly because it has a long and curved neck; it is necessary for the voice, struggling out through the long and winding path, to give back various modulations. It is said that in the Hyperborean <note>the mythical "Far North"</note> regions, when the cithara-players <note>musicians playing a lyre-like instrument</note> perform, many swans are summoned and join in harmony with the music. *Olor* is the Latin name, while in Greek it is called *agnus*. They say that sailors consider this bird a good omen for themselves; as Emilianus <note>Aemilius Macer, a Roman poet</note> says: "The swan is always the most joyful bird in omens; sailors desire it because it does not submerge itself in the waves."

**Morally.** The swan, snowy in its feathers, designates the state of pretense <note>original: *simulationis*</note>, by which black flesh is covered; for the sin of the flesh is covered by a feigned appearance. While the swan swims in the river, it carries its neck and head erect; this is because the proud, even as they are dragged along by transitory things, boast for a time in the possession of fleeting goods. The story that many swans fly to the singing harpers in the Hyperborean regions and harmonize aptly signifies that those who live for pleasures, their desire in the gaping opening <note>the "opening" refers to the bird's beak or the sinner's appetite</note> as the pleasure flies toward them...

<summary>This page describes the swan, using medieval etymology to link its names (Olor and Cygnus) to its white color and its famous "swan song." It concludes with a moral allegory where the swan's white feathers over black skin represent hypocrisy, and its upright neck represents the pride of those obsessed with worldly wealth.</summary>

<keywords>swan, Olor, Cygnus, singing, Hyperboreans, sailors' omens, hypocrisy, pride, moral allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing description of the Swan and begins the entry for the Ibis. ...they harmonize. But it is also said that even at the end, when the **swan** <note>original: *cygnus*</note> is dying, it sings very sweetly while moving. Similarly, when the proud man <note>original: *super[bus]*; the text implies the exit of a worldly or proud person</note> departs from this life, he still delights in the sweetness of the present world, and as he dies, he recalls the evil things he has done. But when the swan is stripped of its white feathers and, placed on a spit, is roasted at the fire: similarly, when the rich of this world die, they are stripped of worldly glory, and descending to the flames of hell, they will be tortured by torments. That which had been accustomed to seek only one thing <note>likely referring to worldly pleasure or singular focus on the self</note>, descending into the abyss, becomes food for the fire.

->**The Ibis**<-

<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in red and white against a blue background. It depicts a large white bird with grey-textured patterns on its wings and neck, representing an Ibis. The bird is bowing its long neck to the left, inserting its beak into a small green circle (representing a nest or water hole) which contains two small, dark serpent-like creatures. To the right of the bird, within the main circular frame, a human figure’s head and bare shoulder are visible, looking upward toward the bird.</image-desc>

There is a bird called the **Ibis** <note>original: *ybis*</note>. With its own beak, it purges its bowels <note>original: *purgat aluum*; medieval bestiaries often claimed the ibis gave itself an enema with its long beak</note>, and they eat the eggs of serpents and die <note>likely referring to the serpents or the toxic nature of the food</note>, and from them they bring back the most heavy <note>or foul; original: *grauissimum*</note> food for their chicks. These birds avoid the water more than others because the ibis does not know the nature of swimming; instead, it walks about by the shore day and night, seeking either dead little fish or any corpses that have been cast out from the water. This signifies **carnal men** <note>original: *carnales homines*; those focused on physical rather than spiritual desires</note> who live, as it were, on deadly works, by which their wretched souls are nourished for punishment. But you, O Christian, who have been reborn of water and the **Holy Spirit** <note>original: *spirituque sancto*</note>: enter into the spiritual waters of the **mysteries of God** <note>original: *misteriorum dei*</note>, and take from there the purest foods which the Apostle lists, saying...

...But the fruit of the Spirit <note>original: *fructus... spiritus*; a reference to Galatians 5:22</note> is love, joy, peace, patience, long-suffering <note>original: *longanimitas*</note>, and the rest. So also you, O man, if you do not fortify yourself with the sign of the cross and extend the wings of the jewels of love, you will not be able to cross through the storms of this world to the most peaceful harbor of the heavenly homeland. Finally, when Moses lifted his hands, Israel prevailed; but when he let down his hands, Amalek prevailed <note>A reference to Exodus 17:11, where Moses's raised arms symbolize the cross and prayer leading to victory</note>.

There is an animal called the **assida** <note>a medieval name for the ostrich, derived from the Hebrew 'chasidah', meaning 'pious' or 'stork'</note>, which the Greeks call the *struocamelon* <note>original: *struocamelon*; from the Greek *strouthiokamelos*, literally 'sparrow-camel'</note> and the Latins call the **ostrich** <note>original: *struccionem*</note>. It has wings, indeed, but it does not fly. Its feet, however, are like those of a camel. When the time comes for it to lay its eggs, it raises its eyes to

heaven and watches to see if that star called the **Pleiades** <note>original: *virgilia*; the Latin name for the Pleiades constellation, whose rising signaled the start of summer</note> appears. When it leaves that place, it immediately forgets them and never returns to its eggs. ### 261 xxxvj

...the sand, warmed by the summer heat, hatches its eggs and brings forth its chicks. 

If, therefore, the **ostrich** <note>original: *assida*</note> recognizes its proper time and forgets its own offspring to follow heavenly things rather than earthly ones: how much more should you, O man—who must strive toward the crossroads of the higher calling <note>original: *biuium supne uocationis*; referring to the spiritual choice between two paths</note>, and for whose sake God was made man—so that He might snatch you from the power of darkness and place you with the princes of His people in His kingdom of glory.

<margin>**The Coot** <note>original: *fulica*</note></margin>
<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in red and blue. Inside, a grey bird with a white beak and frontal shield (a fulica or coot) stands on a stylized wave-like rock of red and white. The background is dark blue, decorated with small white four-pointed stars.</image-desc>

The **coot** <note>original: *fulica*; a water bird known in bestiaries for its perceived wisdom and "clean" habits</note> is a bird that is an exceptionally sensible and prudent creature. It does not feed on carcasses, nor does it fly away or wander to other places; rather, it stays in one single place and remains there until the end. There it finds its food and there it rests. 

This signifies that every faithful person should preserve themselves and live likewise. They should not wander here and there through diverse errors as the heretics do, nor should they delight in worldly desires and pleasures. Instead, they should always keep themselves in one place and remain within the **Catholic Church** <note>original: *ecclia catholica*</note>, where the Lord makes those of one mind dwell in a house <note>A reference to Psalm 68:6, *God sets the lonely in families* or *makes the like-minded dwell in a house*</note>. There, the faithful have their daily food: that is, the **bread of immortality** <note>original: *panē immortalitatis*; the Eucharist</note> and the drink of the precious blood of Christ. They shall refresh themselves—more than with honey or the honeycomb <note>original: *sup mel & fauū*; a reference to Psalm 19:10</note>—with the most sweet words of the Lord.

<margin>**The Gargalus** <note>original: *gargal*</note></margin>
<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in red and blue. It depicts four small birds perched on the branches of a highly stylized tree with a central trunk and symmetrical scrolling branches bearing red flowers. The background is gold.</image-desc>

The **gargalus** <note>original: *gargalus*; likely referring to the *galgulus* or Golden Oriole, though often conflated with other chattering birds in medieval texts</note> is named for its **chattering** <note>original: *garrulitate*; the Latin *garrulus* means talkative or chirping</note>. It is not named, as some would have it, because they fly in flocks <note>original: *gregariū*</note>, but it is manifest that they are named for their voice. For it is a most talkative genus, and with their voices...

<margin>No 67</margin>

...continuous, which can signify the vain loquacity of philosophers or the harmful intent of heretics. Furthermore, there is another thing to say about the nature of the **Jay** <note>original: *gargalus*; a bird often associated in medieval bestiaries with chattering and mimicry</note>. The **Jay** signifies those who are talkative and gluttonous. For those who are devoted to gluttony, after their over-indulgence, their bellies repeat rumors and they lend their ears to slander. The jay lives in the forests; it flits from one tree to another, chattering as it goes. In the same way, the talkative man never ceases to tell others about the things he has heard here and there. The jay also watches for anything and everything; it chatters when it sees someone, and when it finds people in hiding, it does the same. This is because a talkative man not only gossips in the open sunlight, but even in those places where **religion** <note>original: *religionis*; here referring to the secluded life of a monastery</note> is hidden. 

Sometimes a captured jay is caged so that it may be taught to speak articulate words. Similarly, when a **secular person** <note>original: *saeculari*; someone living in the world rather than under religious vows</note> comes to **conversion** <note>original: *conversionem*; the act of joining a religious order</note>, 
<margin>Religion</margin>
he learns the words of religion so that the tongue of a bird may speak the words of men—so that he who was accustomed to speaking in a disorderly way might henceforth become accustomed to speaking in an orderly fashion. However, when it happens that a jay who was kept caged escapes, the bird that was a jay before cries out even more loudly after its exit. In the same way, a talkative man who has taken up the religious life can scarcely restrain his tongue; and if by chance he should leave and go back outside, having abandoned the monastic habit, he turns the good language of religion into a source of slander through his chattering. Therefore, let the teacher be warned when he must receive someone: let him at least provide a restraint beforehand. 

I have learned from a certain religious and discreet man that there are certain **types** <note>original: *divinitates*; likely a scribal error for *diversitates* (sorts) or *dignitates* (classes)</note> of men who can hardly be kept in an orderly fashion within the religious life. If you ask who these are, they can be listed. These are:
<margin>
painters.
doctors.
minstrels.
</margin>
**painters**, **doctors**, **minstrels** <note>original: *ioculatores*; wandering entertainers or jesters</note>, and certain others who are accustomed to wandering through various regions. Men of this kind can hardly be **stable** <note>original: *stabiles*; referring to the monastic vow of *stabilitas loci*, or staying in one place</note>. The art of painters is especially detestable [in this regard]. For when a painter has finished painting a church, a chapter house, a refectory, or any other workshops, he moves on to another...

<summary>This page uses the Jay (Gargalus) as a moral allegory for the dangers of gossip and the difficulty of training talkative secular people for the disciplined silence of monastic life. It also identifies painters, doctors, and minstrels as professions naturally ill-suited to the monastic vow of stability due to their wandering nature.</summary>
