# XLVIII.

...to the monastery, if it is granted to him, he goes across when requested by someone for the purpose of painting. He paints the **works of Christ** <note>original: *Opa xpī*; an abbreviation for *Opera Christi*</note> on the wall; but it is [better] to hold those things in the mind and to clothe them with the colors of one’s own example and character.

The **art of medicine** <term>medicine; here referring to the practice of a physician, which was often discouraged for monks because it required interacting with the secular world</term> is an area that requires many things, and those who practice it are hardly or never without **private property** <note>original: *proprieta*; referring to the "peculium" or personal assets forbidden to monks</note>. Whoever would practice this art must necessarily abound in **aromatics and spices** <note>original: *aromatibus & speciebus*; the raw materials of medieval pharmacy</note>. When someone within the church is weighed down by illness, the doctor is asked to come to the sick person. If, however, the **abbot** does not permit him to go, he incurs anger. Sometimes the doctor touches things that it is not lawful for him to see in an orderly way, and which it is not fitting for a religious person to touch. Then he speaks of certain **experiments** <note>original: *experimenta*; in a medieval context, this refers to medical trials or remedies that were often seen as unreliable or "tricks"</note>. But because experimentation is deceptive, he is often deceived. This is not profitable for a religious person, who should speak nothing but the truth. 

He promises profit to his church if he goes to the sick person, but he remains silent about the **scandal** and the damage to his own soul. Perhaps an enemy is made of the monk-physician, who is "just" in name only, but whose work is vain; he is one who hides **gold pieces** <note>original: *aureos*; gold coins</note> within his medicine. The **Blessed Gregory** <note>Saint Gregory the Great, who tells this story in his *Dialogues* (Book IV, Ch. 55) regarding a monk named Justus</note> speaks of such a man: though he had served Gregory in his illness, Gregory nevertheless did not spare him in correction. Before the man's death, Gregory forbade anyone to speak to him, and after his death, he ordered him to be buried in a **dung heap** <note>original: *sterquilinio*; a pile of manure, intended as a sign of disgrace for violating the vow of poverty</note>. However, he was absolved after death with the words: "May your money be with you in perdition" <note>original: *pecunia tua tecū sit in p̄ditionē*; a quote from Acts 8:20, used here as a ritual of cleansing the monastery from the sin of greed</note>. 

Even **jesters** <term>ioculatores; itinerant entertainers, minstrels, or tumblers</term> who were flighty before their **conversion** <note>original: *conuersionē*; the act of joining a religious order and changing one's life</note>, when they come to the religious life, they often depart as easily as their flightiness suggests. Indeed, those who are accustomed to running through various regions, if they are weighed down by the **boredom of the cloister** <note>original: *tedio claustri*; similar to the concept of *acedia* or spiritual listlessness</note>, find reasons to leave the enclosure because they know the diversity of the lands outside.

<summary>This page warns against monks who maintain worldly professions, such as painters and doctors, arguing that their skills and the need for materials lead to greed, scandal, and a breach of the vow of poverty.</summary>

<margin>The Halcyon</margin>

## The Halcyon

The **Halcyon** <note>original: *Alcion*; typically identified as the kingfisher in medieval lore</note> is a maritime bird that is accustomed to bringing forth its young upon the shores, laying its eggs in the sand in the middle of winter. This time of year is assigned for the brooding of its offspring, even when the sea rises up most violently. At that moment, when the waves strike with greater vehemence, the grace of this bird shines forth through a sudden, solemn stillness. For when the sea has been wavy, once the eggs are laid, it suddenly grows mild and all the storms of the winds subside; the gusts of the breezes grow gentle, and the sea remains calm for the winds until the Halcyon has hatched its eggs. There are seven days for brooding, during which time the chicks are led out and the birth is completed. Immediately, it adds another seven days to nourish its offspring until they begin to grow strong. You may wonder at such a short time for nurturing, since the completion of the brood takes so few days. Nevertheless, by the grace of God, this bird has been granted a miracle so that sailors observe these fourteen days of presumed serenity, which they call the **Halcyon Days** <note>original: *alcionidas*; the origin of the modern phrase "halcyon days," referring to a period of peace and calm</note>, during which no motion of a stormy gale is feared.

<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in red with a gold leaf background. It depicts a Halcyon (kingfisher), a bird with white and grey plumage and green-tinted wings, standing on a pale, nest-like mound floating upon stylized green waves. The bird has a prominent yellow-orange beak and feet. This illustration is placed within the text block describing the bird.</image-desc>

## The Phoenix

The **Phoenix** <note>original: *fenyx*</note> is a bird of Arabia, so named either because it has a **phoenician color** <term>phoenician color; a deep reddish-purple or crimson dye highly prized in antiquity</term>, or because it is singular and unique in the whole world. Living for more than five hundred years, when it sees that it has grown old...

<summary>This page describes the legendary Halcyon bird, which brings a miraculous fourteen-day calm to the winter seas to hatch its young, and begins the description of the Phoenix's unique nature and long life.</summary>
<keywords>Halcyon, Kingfisher, Halcyon Days, Phoenix, Bestiary, maritime lore, Arabian bird, divine miracles, medieval nature study</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Bestiary" section of the manuscript. Having previously discussed the Halcyon, the text now concludes the entry on the Phoenix, transitioning from its physical habits to its deep theological symbolism regarding the Resurrection of Christ.</meta>

<image-desc>In the upper right, a circular medallion framed in red contains a grey phoenix with a long neck and curved beak, head turned backwards. The bird stands on a gold background decorated with red and green scrolling foliate patterns.</image-desc>

...having collected branches of aromatic spices <note>original: *aromatū virgultis*; medieval bestiaries often specify these as cinnamon, myrrh, and frankincense</note>, it builds a funeral pyre for itself. And turning toward the rays of the sun, with a flapping of its wings, it nourishes a voluntary fire for itself.

Afterwards, on the third day, a new bird rises from its own ashes.

Our Lord Jesus Christ <note>original: *dñs nř ih's xps* (Dominus noster Iesus Christus)</note> bears the image of this creature, who says: "I have the power to lay down my soul and to take it up again" <note>original: *potestatē habeo ponendi animam meā & iterū sumendi eam*; a reference to John 10:18</note>.

If, therefore, the **phoenix** <term>Phoenix: a mythical bird that symbolizes rebirth and immortality</term> has the power of putting itself to death and bringing itself back to life: why do foolish men rage against the Word of God, who is the true Son of God, and who says: "I have the power," and so forth? 

For our Savior descended from heaven; He filled His wings with the sweet fragrances of the **New and Old Testament**, and on the altar of the cross He offered Himself for us to God the Father, and on the third day He rose again.

The Phoenix.

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Phoenix from the previous page. Having established the bird as a symbol for Christ's sacrifice, the text now describes the biological process of its rebirth from a worm and transitions into a moral lesson (tropology), urging the reader to prepare their own "casket" of faith and good works for the afterlife.</meta>

The **phoenix** <term>Phoenix: a mythical bird that symbolizes rebirth and immortality</term> is also said to live in the regions of Arabia, and to reach a great age of up to five hundred years. When it comes to the end of its life, it makes for itself a **casket** <note>original: *theca*; here referring to the aromatic nest or funeral pyre that holds the body</note> of frankincense and myrrh and other spices, into which, when the time of its life is fulfilled, it enters and dies. From the moisture of its flesh a worm <note>original: *vermis*; medieval bestiaries often describe the bird's rebirth as beginning with a small larva or maggot arising from the remains</note> breaks forth and gradually grows up. In the course of the appointed time, it is clothed in the rowing-feathers of its wings and is restored to the likeness and form of the former bird.

Therefore, let this bird teach us by its own example to believe in the **resurrection**. This bird, without a previous example and without the instruction of reason, restores for itself the sign of resurrection. Surely birds exist for the sake of men, not man for the sake of birds. Let this be an example to us, because the Author and Creator of birds did not allow His holy ones to perish forever; He willed that the bird should be restored from its own seed.

Who, then, tells the bird the day of its death, so that it may make its casket and fill it with good fragrances, and enter into it and die where the heavy stench of death can be wiped away by the scents? You too, O man, make your casket, and stripping off the **old man** <note>a reference to the "old self" in Ephesians 4:22, representing a life of sin</note> with his deeds, put on the **new**. Your casket and your sheath is **Christ** <term>xp̃e: an abbreviation for Christ (Christe)</term> who protects you and hides you in the <unclear>evil</unclear> <unclear>day</unclear>. For He saw that the casket is precious; "my <unclear>burden</unclear>," He said, "<unclear>is</unclear> <unclear>rich</unclear>." <note>A variation on Matthew 11:30, "My burden is light"</note>

Therefore, your casket is **faith**. Fill it with the good fragrances of your righteous works—<term>H. i. ẽ.: an abbreviation for *hoc est enim* (for this is)</term> of chastity, mercy, and justice. And enter into those inner chambers of faith, smelling of the scent of your excellent deeds, O bird. Let the certain garment of faith be found, so that in the <unclear>future</unclear> your bones may grow rich. And as the bird becomes full, it is <unclear>said</unclear> <unclear>for</unclear> the seeds are raised up. Know, then, the day of your death, just as **Paul** <note>The Apostle Paul</note> knew it, who said: "I have fought the good fight, I have finished the course..." <note>Quoting 2 Timothy 4:7: "I have fought the good fight, I have finished the race, I have kept the faith."</note>

<summary>This page describes the phoenix's rebirth from a worm following its death in a spice-filled nest, using the bird's life cycle as a moral allegory for human resurrection and the importance of faith.</summary>
<keywords>phoenix, resurrection, Arabia, spices, frankincense, myrrh, Saint Paul, faith, old man and new man, allegory</keywords>

### Lex. m. <note>likely an abbreviation for *Lex Moralis* or "Moral Law"</note>

...nourishing me, I do the same for you. If, however, irrational creatures <note>meaning animals, which were thought to lack the human faculty of reason</note> do this for each other, how much more should **rational men** return the nourishment of their parents? For the law says: *He who curses father or mother shall die the death* <note>original: "Qui maledixit patri uel matri morte morietur"; a reference to Exodus 21:17</note>: and he is, as it were, a **parricide** and a **matricide** <term>parricide and matricide: the specific crimes of killing one's father or mother</term>. <insert>pelican.</insert>

<image-desc>A rectangular illumination with a red and orange border. The scene is set against a blue background with a green vine bearing orange flowers emerging from the top left corner. It depicts two parts of the medieval pelican legend. On the left, a large grey pelican is pecking at its young, which has red blood on its neck. On the right, the pelican is shown standing over three dead chicks, pecking its own breast to shed blood onto them for their revival. The birds are detailed with fine grey and white plumage.</image-desc>

The **pelican** <term>Pelicanus: a bird that, in medieval bestiaries, serves as a primary symbol for Christ's Passion and the Eucharist</term> is an Egyptian bird dwelling in the solitude of the **Nile** river, from which it also took its name <note>The text suggests an etymological link between the bird and the Nile or its regions that is common in medieval thought, even if linguistically obscure today</note>. For it is called *canapos* <note>original: "canapos"; likely a reference to Canopus, a coastal city in the Nile Delta</note> by the Egyptians. It is an exceeding lover of its children. When it has fathered young and they begin to grow, they strike their parents in the face. But the parents, striking back, kill them. On the third day, however, their mother, striking her own rib-cage, opens her side and leans over her chicks and pours out her blood upon the bodies of the dead; and so she raises them from death. In this way, our Lord **Jesus Christ** <note>original: "ihc xpc," the Greek-derived shorthand for Iesous Christos</note>, the author and creator of every creature, begot us.

.lxxvi.

...and before we even existed, He made us. ## The Owl is so named because it flies about at night and cannot see during the day. 
For when the brightness of the sun rises, its sight is dimmed. The owl (*noctua*), however, is not the same as the eagle-owl (*bubo*). For the eagle-owl is larger. The *nycticorax* <term>Nycticorax: literally "night-raven," a Greek-derived name for a night-owl</term> is the owl itself, because it loves the night. It is indeed a bird that flees the light <note>original: "lucifuga"</note> and does not endure seeing the sun. This bird carries the figure of the **Jews**, who, when the Lord Savior came to save <unclear>them</unclear>, rejected Him, saying: *We have no king but Caesar* <note>original: "Non habemus regem nisi cesarem"; see John 19:15</note>, and they loved darkness more than light. Then the Lord turned Himself to us, the **Gentiles** <term>Gentiles: "gentes," referring here to non-Jewish people who converted to Christianity</term>, and illuminated us as we sat in darkness and the shadow of death. Of us it is said: *A people whom I have not known has served me* <note>original: "populus quem non cognoui seruiuit michi"; see Psalm 18:43</note>. And in another prophet: *I will call my people "not-my-people,"* etc. <note>See Hosea 2:23</note> And concerning the people of the Jews: *Strange children [have lied to me]*...

## The Eagle-Owl, and so forth.

It is a bird of ill omen, heavily laden with feathers, but often held back by a heavy sluggishness; it lingers in tombs day and night, and always dwells in caverns. Regarding which—

# The Eagle-Owl.

**Ovid** says: *And it comes as a messenger of grief with its foul plumage, the sluggish eagle-owl, a dire omen for mortals* <note>original: "foedasque situ volucris uenit nuntia luctus ignavus bubo, dirum mortalibus omen"; from Ovid's *Metamorphoses*. The poet describes the bird as a harbinger of doom.</note>. Finally, among **augurs** <term>Augurs: ancient Roman religious officials who interpreted the will of the gods by observing the flight and behavior of birds</term> it is said to be a sign of evil. The eagle-owl signifies those surrendered to the darkness of sins and fleeing the light of justice. For this reason, it is reckoned among the unclean animals in **Leviticus** <term>Leviticus: the third book of the Hebrew Bible and the Christian Old Testament, which contains laws regarding ritual purity and "unclean" animals</term> <gloss>depu**tated**</gloss>. 

From this, we can understand by the eagle-owl any sinner. The **bubo** is named from the sound of its voice, because "the mouth speaks from the abundance of the heart" <note>This is a reference to Matthew 12:34. The author suggests the owl's hooting is like a sinner blurting out their internal corruption.</note>; for what it thinks on the inside, it brings forth. It is said to be a filthy bird because the place in which it lives is defiled by its dung; just so, the sinner dishonors those with whom he lives by the example of his perverse work. 

The bird is burdened with feathers, that is: the superfluity of the flesh and levity of the mind. But indeed, it is held back by a heavy laziness. Deservedly are sinners held by a heavy laziness, because they are unskilled and lazy toward doing good. Day and night they linger in a tomb, because sinners delight in sin, which is the stench of human flesh. It also lives in caves and does not come out through confession, but avoiding the light, it hates it. 

When it is seen by other birds, it is harassed by their great cries and betrayals <note>This refers to "mobbing" behavior, where smaller birds gather to scream at and harass a predator to drive it away.</note>. For if a sinner comes to the light of knowledge, he provides a great occasion of mockery to those who do good. And when he has been openly caught in sin, he hears reproaches from others. They pluck his feathers and tear him with their beak, because even carnal...

<summary>This page describes the eagle-owl (bubo) as a symbol of the sinner who dwells in darkness, avoids confession, and is burdened by the "feathers" of worldly excess. It draws on Ovid and biblical law to present the bird as an omen of evil and a figure of moral corruption.</summary>

Therefore, the eagle-owl is unhappy because it works at those things which we preach against.

<margin>The Siren.</margin>

## The Siren
The Siren, as the **Physiologus** <term>Physiologus: a famous early Christian text that used descriptions of animals to teach moral and religious lessons</term> says, is a deadly creature. From the head to the navel, they have the form of humans, but their lower parts down to the feet have a winged form <note>original: "uolatilē"; while later traditions often depict sirens as mermaids, many medieval bestiaries described them as bird-like women</note>. They emit a certain musical sound and a most sweet song of melody, such that by the sweetness of their voice, they soothe the hearing of men sailing from afar and draw them toward themselves. By the excessive sweetness of this music, they strike the ears and the senses of these men and turn them toward sleep. Then, when they see the men are in a deep sleep, they suddenly attack and tear their flesh. Thus, by the persuasion of their voice, they deceive and kill ignorant and unwary men.

<image-desc>A rectangular framed miniature depicts a siren and two sailors. The siren has the upper body of a woman with long golden hair and a blue fish tail. She floats on stylized green waves. To her left, two men occupy a small wooden boat; one man is slumped over the side as if asleep. The background is red and decorated with white stars.</image-desc>

In this same way, those who take pleasure in delights, displays, and lusts are deceived. Dissolved by comedies, tragedies, and various musical melodies, they lose all the vigor of their mind as if in sleep, and suddenly they become the prey of their adversaries <note>original: "adversarioz̄"; referring to spiritual enemies or demons</note> most greedily.

## The Partridge
The partridge takes its name from its voice: **perdix** <note>The author believes the Latin name "perdix" mimics the sound the bird makes</note>. The partridge is a deceitful and unclean bird. For the male rises up against the male, and lust takes hold of them, causing them to forget their sex. So much so...

### The Magpie.

Magpies are like poets <note>original: "Pice quasi poete"; a play on the idea that both repeat words without necessarily understanding the underlying divine truth</note> because they express vocal words indistinctly like a human. For, hanging among the branches of trees, they sound with an importunate chatter; and even if they cannot unfurl their tongues in speech, they nevertheless imitate the sound of the human voice. Concerning this, [the poet] indeed aptly says: "I, a talkative magpie, greet you, my master, with a distinct voice; if you did not see me, you would deny I was a bird" <note>original: "Pica loquax certa dominum te voce salutat. si me non videas esse negabit avem"; this is a quote from the Roman poet Martial, Epigrams 14.76</note>. The Woodpecker <note>original: "Picus"; the text shifts here from the magpie (pica) to the woodpecker (picus) based on their similar names</note> took its name from the son of Faunus, because he was seen in auguries <note>In Roman mythology, Picus was a king and augur who was turned into a woodpecker by Circe</note>. For they say this bird has something divine about it, evidenced by the fact that in whichever tree it has nested, a nail or whatever else is fixed there cannot remain for long, but immediately falls out when the bird has settled there. But let each person think what they wish about this. For the sound of its voice can signify the loquacity of heretics or philosophers, just as we said above regarding the talkative person.

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of the magpie to the entry for the hawk (accipiter). <image-desc>A circular miniature with a red border and a gold-leaf background. Inside the circle, the background is deep blue and decorated with small white stars. ### The Hawk

The hawk <note>original: "accipiter"</note> takes its name from the act of taking <note>original: "accipiendo," a play on words linking the name of the bird to the verb "to seize"</note>, which is to say it comes from the word for "seizing." For it is a bird eager for seizing other birds. Therefore it is called a "taker" <note>original: "accipiter"</note>, which is to say a "ravisher" <note>original: "raptor"</note>. Whence Paul also says <margin>2 Cor. 11</margin>: "For you endure it if someone takes <note>original: "accipit"</note> from you" <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 11:20, where Paul uses the verb to describe someone taking advantage of others</note>. 

It is said that the hawk is unkind toward its own chicks. For when it sees that they are able to attempt flight, it offers them no food, but beats them with its wings and drives them from the nest; from a tender age it compels them toward prey, so that they do not grow lazy when they are adults. It takes care lest they become sluggish in their youth, or be weakened by luxuries, or wither in idleness, or learn to wait for food rather than seek it, or lay aside the vigor of their nature. The parents stop their nursing care so that they might compel the chicks to dare [to hunt] through the practice of seizing. 

The hawk signifies the holy man, as he "seizes" the kingdom of God <note>This refers to the Gospel of Matthew 11:12, suggesting that the kingdom of heaven is taken by force or great zeal</note>. Concerning this, it is written in Job <margin>Job 39</margin>: "Does the hawk grow feathers by your wisdom, spreading its wings toward the south?" <note>Job 39:26</note>. This means: Have you bestowed understanding upon any of the elect, so that when the Holy Spirit blows, he might expand the wings of his thoughts, to the extent that he casts off the weights of old habits and takes up the <term>feathers of virtues</term> <note>a metaphor for spiritual growth and molting away the old self</note> for the practice of a new flight? Through this hawk, the renewed Gentiles <note>non-Jewish nations converted to Christianity</note> can also be signified. 

*How the hawk ought to grow feathers.*

For **domesticated** hawks, so that they might grow feathers better, quiet and secluded places are required. Quiet places are the enclosures <note>original: "claustra," which can also mean cloisters or monastic settings</note> in which the wild hawk is placed so that it might become domestic and is held in confinement. There, the old feathers...

<summary>This page describes the etymology and behavior of the hawk, emphasizing its "harsh" parenting as a metaphor for spiritual discipline and its molting as a symbol of the soul's renewal through the Holy Spirit.</summary>
<keywords>hawk, accipiter, Job, spiritual discipline, molting, Holy Spirit, virtues, allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical description of the hawk, transitioning from its physical molting process to the symbolic meanings of its equipment—the perch and the shackles—within the context of monastic discipline.</meta>

...loses its old feathers and takes up new ones. Just as any person in a cloister <note>original: "claustralis"</note>, when they are stripped of the stain of vice, is adorned with the virtues of the "new man." However, it does not fly out unless, after the old feathers have been cast off, the new ones have grown solid; once it has flown firmly for a year after being cast out, it comes back to the hand. Similarly, if any convert leaves the cloister, it is necessary that he approach the hand of "good work"; and having been sent forth from there, he may fly so that he raises himself with the whole effort of his mind toward the desired heavenly things.

<margin>Song of Songs 2</margin>
**The Hawk** is usually carried on the left hand, or it is released from the right to fly and capture something. <margin>Because the hawk is in...</margin> "His left hand," she says, "is under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me" <note>original: "Leva eius sub capite meo, et dextera illius amplexabitur me," quoting Song of Songs 2:6</note>. The left hand represents temporal goods <note>physical, worldly things</note>; the right, however, represents eternal things. On the left hand sits he who presides over light, temporal matters. But on the right flies he who desires eternal things with the whole affection of his mind. There the hawk captures the dove, which is to say, whoever is changed for the better will receive grace through hope.

### On the Perch of the Hawk

**The Perch** <note>original: "pertica"</note> of the hawk designates for us the uprightness of the regular life <term>regular life: a life lived according to a monastic Rule, or 'regula'</term>. It is suspended high up because this kind of life is separated far from earthly desires. On this perch sits the one bound who firmly holds to the statutes of the regular life. The perch is said to cling to two walls, by which it is supported on either side. The two walls which support the perch are the active and contemplative life <note>the medieval distinction between 'labor' and 'prayer'</note>, which together support the uprightness of life.

### On the Shackles

**Shackles** <note>original: "compedes"</note> are customarily placed on the feet <margin>of the hawk</margin> of the hawk so that it cannot fly away whenever it wants if some occasion arises. The feet of the hawk are humbled or bound as if in shackles, which press upon weary minds with the love of judgment and the pain of punishment. And "they humbled his feet in shackles" <note>quoting Psalm 105:18</note>. Joseph's feet were humbled in shackles, which, by bringing back to memory full misery and eternal...

<summary>This page uses the hawk’s molting and falconry equipment (perch and shackles) as metaphors for the monastic life, comparing spiritual renewal to the growth of new feathers and the monastic Rule to a high perch.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the allegorical interpretation of the hawk’s equipment, specifically the leash or shackle, and begins the entries for the bat and the nightingale. ...it binds the affections of the soul so that they do not wander off toward the punishments they have adopted <note>The text suggests that spiritual discipline prevents the mind from straying into sinful habits that lead to spiritual "punishment."</note>. <margin>On the hawk's shackle.</margin>

**The Shackle** <note>original: "corrigia," a leather strap or thong used to tether a bird of prey</note> by which the hawk is tied to the perch is the mortification of the flesh <note>The practice of self-denial or "killing" worldly desires</note>. By this shackle, any professed monk is held in the regular life <term>regular life: a life lived according to a monastic Rule, or "regula"</term>. Indeed, the shackle, which is made from the leather of a dead animal, signifies the mortification of the flesh. This shackle is not broken, but it is loosened when the hawk is prompted to go out and catch something. Similarly, if any brother goes out for the sake of some temporal gain <note>necessary worldly business for the monastery</note>, the rule is not broken, as has been stated. But when he returns, he is bound more firmly by that same shackle which was written of before.

<image-desc>A circular miniature depicting a bat with gray fur and large, ribbed, webbed wings spread wide. The bat is shown from a dorsal view against a solid blue background, enclosed by a thick orange-red circular border. To the right of the frame, the name is written in red rubric.</image-desc>
<margin>The Bat.</margin>

**The Bat** <note>original: "Vespalio," a variant of "Vespertilio"</note> is a lowly animal that takes its name from the evening <note>original: "a uespe," likely referring to "vesper" or evening</note>. It is a flying creature and yet also a four-footed one, and it has teeth, which is not usually found in other birds. It gives birth like a four-footed animal; it does not produce eggs, but brings forth live young. It flies, however, not with any true flight of feathers, but supported by the rowing motion of its membrane, by which it is suspended and raised up and moved, just like a flight of feathers. This lowly animal also has this trait: they cling to one another and hang from a certain place like a cluster of grapes <term>cluster of grapes: original "botrio," referring to how bats huddle together in a mass</term>; and if the last one lets go, they all fall away. This happens by a certain gift of charity <note>original: "caritatis," brotherly love</note>, which is difficult to find among men of this kind.

The bird is sitting in a brown, bowl-like nest. The background is a solid pinkish-beige, enclosed by a thick blue circular border. To the right of the frame, the name is written in red rubric.</image-desc>
<margin>The Nightingale.</margin>

**The Nightingale** <note>original: "Lucina," a variant of "Luscinia"</note> takes its name from the fact that its song signifies the rising of the day, as if it were the light of dawn <note>original: "lucina"</note>. For it is a watchful guardian when it warms its eggs in a certain fold of its body and its breast...

<meta>This page concludes the description of the Nightingale—though the bird's name is missing from the top of the fragment—and introduces the section on the Raven. ...[the nightingale] <note>The preceding text, missing here, describes the nightingale's habit of singing through the night.</note> watches in sleep, and is consoled for the labor of the long night by the sweetness of her song. From this, she seems to find her greatest joy in that time when she can, with no less sweet melodies, cherish and animate the offspring of her body and warm her eggs. The modest woman, following this example, dragging the weight of a millstone with her arm so that the nourishment of bread might not be lacking for her little ones, soothes the sad feeling of poverty with a nightly song; and although she cannot imitate the bird in the sweetness of her voice, she has nonetheless imitated her in the diligence of her devotion.

->The Raven.<-

<margin>☩</margin> **The Raven**, or *corax* <note>original: "Corvus sive corax"</note>, takes its name from the sound of its throat, because it croaks with its voice <note>The author is using "coracinet," an onomatopoeic Latin word for the raven's "caw," to explain the origin of the name "corax."</note>. It is said of this bird that it does not provide food fully to its hatched chicks until it recognizes in them, through the blackness of their feathers, a likeness to its own color. However, after it sees them dark with feathers, it acknowledges them fully and feeds them more abundantly. This bird first seeks the eye in carcasses <note>A common medieval belief that ravens always peck out the eyes of the dead first, symbolizing how demons blind the "eyes" of the soul.</note>. The raven signifies the blackness of sinners or demons, as it says in Solomon <note>Proverbs 30:17</note>: 
> let the ravens of the valleys pick it out.

Likewise, the raven signifies the Gentile people <note>Non-Christian nations or people outside the faith</note>, as it says in Job <note>Job 38:41</note>:
> Who provides for the raven its food, when its young ones cry to God, wandering about because they have no meat?

Food is given to the chicks—the children of the ravens, that is, the Gentiles—when their emptiness is restored by our way of life <term>way of life: original "conversacione," referring specifically to the conduct and spiritual discipline of the monastic life</term>. On the other hand, the raven is also used in a positive sense, as it says in the Song of Songs <note>Song of Solomon 5:11</note>:
> His locks are like the shoots of palm trees, black as a raven.

The blackness of the raven represents sin or lack of faith, as in the Psalm <note>Psalm 147:9</note>:
> ...and to the young ravens that call upon him.

The raven is depicted as a complex symbol representing both the "blackness" of sinners and the conversion of the Gentiles to the faith.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Raven, specifically its symbolic role as a procrastinating sinner, before transitioning into a lengthy entry on the Crow. The text shifts from the Raven's physical traits (covered on the previous page) to the moral lessons derived from the Crow’s legendary devotion to its young, contrasted sharply with the perceived failings of human parents.</meta>

<page-num>lxxxij</page-num>

The **Raven** <note>original: "Corvus"</note> signifies the slowness of the sinner toward repentance, and those who postpone from day to day with vain hope. As it says in the book of Zephaniah <note>original: "Sophonia"</note>: *The voice of one singing in the window; the raven on its lintels.* <note>Zephaniah 2:14. In medieval bestiaries, the raven's "cras, cras" (Latin for "tomorrow") was often compared to the sinner who says "I will repent tomorrow."</note>

<margin>The Crow.</margin>
<image-desc>A circular miniature framed by a red and gold border depicting a black crow (cornix) standing on blue ground against a salmon-pink background.</image-desc>

The **Crow** <note>original: "Cornix"</note> is a long-lived bird called by the Greeks' name among the Latins <note>The Latin *cornix* is related to the Greek *korone*.</note>. However, since people believe that crows manage human cares through omens, show ways to those lying in wait, and predict the future, it would be a great wickedness to believe that God entrusts His private counsels to crows. Yet many attribute many signs to this bird, such as portending rain with its voice. From this comes that saying: *Then the crow with a persistent voice calls for the rain.* <note>A quote from Virgil’s *Georgics* (I.388), a famous Roman instructional poem on farming.</note> Let men learn to love their children from the practice and devotion of crows, which even follow their flying children with diligent company; they solicitously provide food lest the tender ones perhaps fail, and they do not abandon the duty of nurturing them for a very long space of time. 

But truly, women of our own kind quickly wean even those they love, and they feel a loathing to suckle those they bring forth. If they are poorer, they cast away and expose their little ones, and deny them when they are found. Even the wealthy themselves, lest their inheritance be divided among many, kill their own offspring in the womb; with parricidal potions <note>original: "paricidaliis"</note> they strangle their own pledges <note>original: "pignora," a poetic Latin term for children as "pledges of love."</note> in the very womb that created them, taking away their life before they are even delivered. Who taught any but man to renounce his children? Who discovered laws so hostile to a father? Why has the fellowship of nature's womb now made brothers unequal? The riches of one father fall to a son of a different lot. To some, the face of the fatherland changes with differing fortunes; to others, wealth...

<summary>This page concludes the Raven's entry by linking its cry to the procrastinating sinner and introduces the Crow, praising its maternal devotion as a rebuke to human parents who abandon or harm their children.</summary>
<keywords>Raven, Crow, Zephaniah, parenting, repentance, Virgil, maternal care, inheritance</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moralizing section on the Crow, specifically criticizing human greed regarding inheritance in contrast to the bird's natural devotion. It then begins a new, major section on the Dove (Columba), which serves as an extensive allegory for the ideal Christian preacher. This text is characteristic of Hugh of Fouilloy’s "Aviarium," a 12th-century treatise that uses birds as symbols for monastic and clerical life.</meta>

<page-num>Lxxxiiii.</page-num>

...deplores the paternal inheritance as exhausted and his own portion as meager. Did nature ever divide the merits of sons? From the father, she grants to all that they might have the substance for being born and for living. Let nature herself teach you not to make distinctions in the inheritance <note>original: "patrimonio"</note>, for you were made equals in the brotherhood of the womb <note>original: "uticulo" (utriculo), referring to the shared womb of a mother.</note>. Indeed, why should those to whom you have given the common gift of existence not have the substance of life in common, without envy?

->The Dove<-

<image-desc>A framed rectangular miniature of a columbarium (dovecote). The top features three red conical roofs or domes with golden finials. Below, a grid of niches is depicted. The middle-bottom row shows three white doves standing in their respective arched niches, facing left. The frame consists of red and blue decorative borders.</image-desc>

The **Dove** <note>original: "Columba"</note> is a simple bird; it lacks gall <note>original: "felle caret." Medieval natural history believed the dove lacked a gallbladder, the source of "bitter" humors, making the bird physically incapable of malice or anger.</note> and arouses love with a kiss. In the same way, **preachers** <note>original: "predicatores"</note> lack anger and bitterness; for although they may be angry, it is not called "anger" when they are angry for a rational cause. The dove has a groan instead of a song. In the same way, preachers—far from the love of [secular] song and the love of the world—groan for their own sins and the sins of others. 

It does not tear with its beak. This well suits preachers, for they do not corrupt the scriptures as the heretics do. The dove chooses the better grains; in the same way, preachers choose the better meanings <note>original: "sententias"</note> from the scriptures. It nourishes the chicks of others. In this way, preachers nourish the children of this world—those alienated from God through sin—with their preaching, drawing them toward Christ. 

The dove sits beside streams so that, having seen a hawk, it may dive in and thus escape. Similarly, holy men and preachers dwell within the scriptures, so that when they see the attack and temptation of the devil, they may submerge themselves in those scriptures through their actions—that is, according to the commands of those same scriptures—and thus escape. They defend themselves with their wings. In the same way, preachers fortify and defend themselves with the testimony of the **Fathers** <note>The "patruum" refers to the Church Fathers, the foundational theologians of early Christianity.</note>. They nest in the holes of the rock; and so preachers [nest] in the wounds—that is, in... <note>This sentence likely concludes on the next page with "in the wounds of Christ."</note>

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on parental fairness using the Crow as a model before introducing the Dove as a multifaceted symbol for the Christian preacher. The Dove’s physical traits, such as its lack of gall and its nesting habits, are interpreted as metaphors for the virtues of those who teach the faith.</summary>

<keywords>Dove, preachers, allegory, gall, scriptures, inheritance, parental devotion, Aviarium, spiritual protection</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the Dove, specifically its nesting habits and social behavior as a model for the clergy. It then transitions into the section on the Turtledove, emphasizing themes of solitude, protection of the young, and lifelong marital fidelity as a moral example for human widows.</meta>

<page-num>Lxxxvj.</page-num>

...through faith in the wounds of Christ, of whom it is said: *the rock was Christ* <note>original: "petra autem erat Christus." A reference to 1 Corinthians 10:4, comparing the clefts in a rock where doves nest to the wounds of Christ where the faithful find refuge.</note>. In two [openings], they make a defense for themselves and for others. The dove also has this nature: it may recover lost vision. In the same way, the preachers of the Church, if some gift of the Holy Spirit has been lost through some sin, they recover it—just as David recovered the spirit of prophecy which he had lost <note>Referring to King David’s loss of divine inspiration following his sin with Bathsheba, and its restoration after his repentance.</note>. It also has this nature: it flies in a flock. In the same way, preachers, holding to the flock of the faith, advance by the steps of good works and virtues. For when they perform good works, we draw near to God by just as many steps.

->The Turtledove<-

<image-desc>A circular miniature with a red border containing two stylized turtledoves facing each other. The birds are pale with gray plumage details. They are perched on a brown branch that splits at the top into two stylized floral ornaments in red and blue. The background of the roundel is gold, set within a larger reddish-brown square frame that intersects with the main text block.</image-desc>

The **Turtledove** <note>original: "Turtur"</note> is named for its voice; it is a modest bird, and always dwells upon the ridges of mountains and in deserted solitudes. For it flees the roofs of men and their companionship, and stays in the woods. It is said that even in the winter time, having lost its feathers <note>Medieval naturalists believed some birds hibernated in hollow trees and lost their plumage during the cold months.</note>, it lives in the nearby hollows of trees.

The turtledove also places leaves of **squill** <term>squill: a Mediterranean plant (Drimia maritima) used in the Middle Ages as a repellent against pests and predators.</term> over its nest so that a wolf may not attack its chicks; for it knows that wolves are accustomed to flee from leaves of this kind. Furthermore, if this turtledove has been widowed by the loss of its own mate, it scorns the use of the marriage bed and the name of marriage. This is because its first love failed it—deceived by the death of the beloved—since that love proved unfaithful to permanence and was bitter to grace, generating more pain from death than sweetness from affection.

Therefore, it refuses a second union, nor does it dissolve the laws of modesty or the covenants agreed upon with its husband. For him alone it reserves its chastity; for him it guards the name of "wife." Let women learn how great is the grace of widowhood, which is preached even among the birds. Who, then, gave these laws to the turtledove?

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the Dove as a resilient preacher and introduces the Turtledove as a symbol of solitude, maternal protection, and perpetual widowhood.</summary>
<keywords>Dove, Turtledove, Christ's wounds, King David, repentance, squill, widowhood, chastity, medieval natural history</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moralizing section on the Turtledove, contrasting animal instinct with human weakness, and begins the entry for the Swallow. It picks up from the previous page's description of the turtledove's preference for solitude.</meta>

<page-num>Lxxxvj.</page-num>

...and stays <note>Continuing from the previous page: "stays in deserted solitudes."</note> in high places. If I seek a man [who follows this example], I do not find one. For no man is bold enough—not even **Paul** was bold enough—to prescribe laws for maintaining widowhood. 

For he himself says: *I wish therefore for the younger women to marry, to bear children, to be mothers of families, and to give no occasion to the adversary for slander* <note>original: "Volo ergo iuniores nubere..." from 1 Timothy 5:14. The author notes that even the Apostle Paul suggests remarriage for the young rather than risking the "scandal" of a failed attempt at celibacy.</note>. It is good for them if they remain so. *But if they do not restrain themselves, let them marry; for it is better to marry than to burn* <note>original: "melius est enim nubere quam uri." A famous quote from 1 Corinthians 7:9 regarding the struggle with desire.</note>. Paul wishes for women that which perseveres in both [virtue and status]. And elsewhere he exhorts the younger to marry because our women can scarcely fulfill the modesty of the flesh.

Therefore, God has infused this grace or disposition into turtledoves; he gave them this virtue of continence, which he alone can prescribe for all to follow. The turtledove does not change with the flower of youth, nor is it tempted by the enticement of opportunity. The turtledove does not know how to make void its first faith, because it knows how to keep the chastity promised in the first lot of marriage.

<margin>**Swallow** <note>original: "Hyrundo"</note></margin>

<image-desc>A rectangular framed miniature depicts a stylized building with a blue-shingled roof and red structural beams. Inside the building, one swallow is shown in flight, while another is perched in a mud nest built against the roof beam. The background within the building is white with a central red support pillar. The illustration is outlined by a thin red border.</image-desc>

The **Swallow** is named because it does not take its food while resting on the ground <note>original: "ab humo non sumit." This reflects a medieval folk etymology linking the name *hirundo* to the bird not feeding from the earth (humus).</note>, but catches and eats its food in the air. It is a talkative bird; it passes through the circle of the sun and winding flight circuits; it is most skillful in constructing nests and rearing its young. It even possesses a certain foresight, for it deserts buildings that are about to fall and does not seek their heights. It is not hindered by other birds, nor is it ever prey; it flies over the seas and stays there in winter. The swallow is a bird tiny in body, but outstanding in its lofty spirit; though lacking in all things, it builds nests more precious than gold, because it nests wisely. For the nest of wisdom is more precious than gold. For what is wiser than that it should enjoy the wandering freedom of flight, and entrust its little ones and its roofs to the dwellings of men, ubi...

<summary>This page concludes the lesson on the Turtledove's lifelong fidelity—contrasting it with human frailty—and introduces the Swallow as a symbol of wisdom and foresight.</summary>
<keywords>Turtledove, Swallow, Widowhood, Apostle Paul, Chastity, Wisdom, Nesting, Foresight</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Swallow, focusing on its nesting habits and the medicinal care of its young, before beginning a new entry on Cranes. It follows the previous page's introduction to the Swallow as a bird that dwells among humans.</meta>

...so that no one should attack the offspring. For it is a beautiful thing that from their very birth, the swallow accustoms its chicks to the habits of human companionship <note>original: "humane usu conversationis." Swallows were famously known for nesting in the eaves of human houses, making them "companions" to people.</note>; and for that reason, it removes them from the snares of enemy birds. Then there is that remarkable fact: that it builds a square house for itself without any helper, like a skilled artisan. For it gathers stalks in its beak and smears them with mud so that it can glue them together. But because it cannot carry mud with its feet, it soaks the tips of its feathers in water so that dust may easily adhere to them and become mud, with which it gradually gathers the stalks or tiny twigs and makes them stick together. And with innate skill, it builds the structure so that, as if on a paved floor, its chicks may move about within their home without injury, lest any chick should hurt its feet, or its first <gloss>each</gloss> feathers, or be afflicted by tender sores. But this duty is properly common to many birds; however, that which is unique is the swallow's remarkable caring devotion, its prudent understanding, and its distinguished knowledge. Furthermore, there is a certain skill in the medical art: for if any of its chicks should be clouded by blindness, it has a certain type of healing by which it can restore their sight and light to their vision <note>The author is referring to the legend that swallows use the herb celandine (named *chelidonia* after the Greek word for swallow, *chelidon*) to heal the eyes of their young.</note>.

---

### Cranes

<image-desc>An illumination depicting two cranes within interlinked circular roundels with red and blue borders. The crane in the left roundel is turned to look backward over its wing. The crane on the right is shown with its long neck curved downward toward its chest. The birds are rendered with stylized feathers and long, thin legs characteristic of medieval bird illustrations.</image-desc>

Cranes are named from the sound of their voice <note>The Latin *Grues* is an onomatopoeia for the "gru-gru" sound the birds make.</note>, which those who speak the original tongue call *gaa*. This is because they first gather themselves into small congregations when the time for arrival comes. For when summer is driven away, they cross the seas in a formation called a column <note>original: "gomena," likely a corruption of *agmen* or a similar term for a marching line.</note>, which a leader guides. Seeing this formation approaching, the hawk tries to seize them; and for that reason, it is the concern of all the birds to keep a careful watch for the approach of a stranger, through which they might avoid the first dangers. These birds are extremely hardy and eat the seeds of poisonous plants <note>original: "semina venenoz." Medieval lore suggested cranes could eat things that would kill other animals.</note>; for which reason the ancients called them "vipers" <note>original: "vesa," likely a corruption of *vipereas*.</note>. For only this...

<summary>This page describes the architectural skill of the swallow in building its nest with mud and its legendary ability to heal the eyes of its blind chicks, followed by the start of a chapter on cranes and their migratory discipline.</summary>
<keywords>swallow, cranes, animal architecture, medicinal herbs, migration, bestiary, medieval natural history</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing allegory of the swallow's habits from the previous page, transitioning into a discussion on the migration of birds as a metaphor for the soul's journey, the leadership of the quail, and the beginning of the entry on the goose.</meta>

...should know evil, and thus man suffers from the falling sickness <note>original: "caducum morbum." This refers to epilepsy, which in the Middle Ages was often used as a spiritual metaphor for the "fall" into sin.</note>. Swallows have specific times for their arrival, for they fly across the seas when summer has been driven away. The heat of summer represents the fire of charity; the cold of winter is the temptation of a mind grown cold. Therefore, let the just man pass from the love of neighbor, through the sea of this world, to the love of God, so that he may always remain in a warm region—he who always burns within himself with the heat of love, so that he may overcome the cold of winter, namely the storms and winds in the pursuit of temptation.

The Quail <note>original: "Ortygomes," referring to the *Ortygometra* or "leader of the quails," often identified in medieval bestiaries as the Corncrake which was thought to lead the quail migration.</note> is so named because it leads the flock. The raptor, seeing it approaching the earth, seizes it. The "Earth" represents earthly desires and the various sins of the world. The Hawk is the lurking Devil who tempts through suggestion. He seizes whoever approaches the earth, and seeing them, he snatches them away; for he brings those who seek earthly things entirely into his own service. Or he seeks them out of necessity, according to those things he seeks, lest he himself be caught; for that same Devil snatches those who do not cease to gape at earthly things instead of spiritual goals.

Therefore, the care of the whole flock is to be concerned with the leader of the "external kind," <note>The "external kind" refers to those outside the faith or the "perverse," used here as a negative example to be avoided.</note> through whom they might beware of the first dangers. There are two kinds of men: namely, the good and the bad. The external kind are perverse men. The just, therefore, place the wicked before themselves as examples, while they diligently observe their falls and outcomes. While they attentively consider these things, they avoid the first dangers of sinning by such consideration.

However, let it be known that man suffers the falling sickness because the spiritual man, just like the carnal man, is sometimes prohibited from sinning <note>The text suggests that even the spiritual person is susceptible to "falling" or failing.</note>. Nor does he die every time he sins, because the grace of repentance is not denied to him. Whence it is written: *The just man falls seven times a day.* And yet he does not cease to be just. For as often as the just man sins, he turns back willingly so that he may rise again.

->The Goose<-

The goose bears witness to the watches of the night with the persistence of its honking.

<summary>This page uses the migration of swallows and quails as an allegory for the Christian soul avoiding the "cold" of sin for the "warmth" of divine love, while also explaining the "falling sickness" as a metaphor for the recurring nature of human sin and repentance.</summary>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on birds from the previous entry, moving from the leadership of the quail and the corncrake to the entry on the goose. It focuses on the goose's legendary vigilance and uses it as a metaphor for spiritual watchfulness within the monastic community.</meta>

No animal, however, detects the scent of humans as well as the goose <note>original: "anser."</note>. Whence also, by its cry, the ascent of the Gauls onto the Capitol was detected. According to Rabanus <note>Rabanus Maurus (c. 780–856), a prolific Frankish monk whose encyclopedia "On the Nature of Things" was a primary source for medieval animal lore.</note>, this can signify prudent men and those who have kept watch well over their guardianship. There are two species of geese: the domestic and the wild goose <note>original: "campestris," literally "of the fields."</note>.

<image-desc>A circular medallion with a red border containing an illustration of four geese against a blue background. Two larger geese are in the rear, one with its neck extended to the right, and two smaller goslings are in the foreground.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A marginal illustration of a lithe, four-legged creature with a long neck and tail, colored in shades of orange and white, looking upwards with its mouth open and tongue protruding.</image-desc>

Wild geese fly high and in an orderly fashion; they represent those who, removed from earthly things <note>original: "remoti a trenis [terrenis]."</note>, maintain the order of living well. Domestic geese, however, live together in villages; they cry out much and tear at themselves with their beaks. They represent those who, although they love communal life <note>original: "conventus," referring to assemblies or monastic gatherings.</note>, devote themselves to talkativeness and slander <note>original: "detractioni," specifically the sin of "detraction" or damaging another's reputation.</note>. Wild geese are entirely ash-colored, even if you see some of them being variegated or snow-white. In domestic ones, however, there is not only this ash-color, but also variegated or white. In the wild ones, the ash-color signifies those who remove themselves from the world and live in the humility <note>original: "vilitate," implying the lowliness or "cheapness" of their lifestyle.</note> of penance. Those, however, who live in cities or villages wear clothing of a more beautiful color.

The goose, above all other animals, senses the scent of an approaching human. It does not cease to cry out at night, just as a discerning man recognizes others—though far removed—through their bad or good reputation. When, therefore, the goose senses the scent of one approaching, it does not cease to cry at night; likewise, when a discerning man sees the negligence of ignorance in others, he ought to cry out. 

Once, the cry of the goose was of use to the Romans on the Capitol <note>A reference to the 390 BC Siege of Rome, where sacred geese warned the Romans of a nighttime Gallic invasion.</note>. In the "capitol" of the heart, the cry of a discerning brother is profitable when they see negligences. The cry of the goose repelled the Gallic enemy from the Capitol; and from the chapter <note>original: "capitulo." The author makes a pun here between the "Capitol" of Rome and the monastic "Chapter" or governing assembly of a monastery.</note>, the cry of a discerning brother repels the "Ancient Enemy" <note>The Devil.</note>. The cry of the goose [protected] the Roman city from the attack of enemies...

<summary>This page explores the goose as a symbol of vigilance, contrasting the wild goose's orderly, ascetic life with the domestic goose's noisy, social habits. It uses the Roman legend of the Geese of the Capitol to emphasize the duty of "discerning brothers" to sound the alarm against spiritual negligence and the Devil.</summary>

<keywords>goose, vigilance, Capitol, Gauls, Rabanus Maurus, wild vs domestic, allegory, monastic life, slander, penance</keywords>

.lxxv. v

...kept it immune. Its cry knows how to learn so that it is not disturbed; it guards with intention from boys. Perhaps divine providence <note>original: "divina providentia"</note> would not have set before us the natures of birds unless it wished them to benefit us in some way. **The Peacock.**

<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in red and gold containing a peacock. The peacock is depicted in profile, facing right, with its magnificent tail feathers fully fanned out in a circular display behind it. The feathers are a pale gold/parchment color, decorated with numerous "eyes" consisting of concentric circles in blue and white. The bird's body is dark blue with brown wings and a small crest on its head.</image-desc>

The peacock <note>original: "Pauo"</note> takes its name from the sound of its voice. Its flesh is so hard that it scarcely feels decay <note>A legend popularized by St. Augustine, who claimed to have tested a piece of peacock meat and found it did not rot over a year.</note>, nor is it easily cooked. Concerning which someone said: "As often as it unfolds its gem-studded wings, wonder at it. If you can, hand over this hard bird to a harsh cook." The peacock indeed, because it was brought to Solomon <note>Reference to 1 Kings 10:22, where Solomon's fleet brought back gold, silver, ivory, apes, and peacocks.</note> from distant regions and has diverse colors in its feathers, signifies the Gentile people <note>The "Gentiles" refers to non-Jewish nations who converted to Christianity.</note> coming from far-off parts to Christ, who also shines by His grace with the adornment of many virtues.

### The Screech Owl.

<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in red depicting a bird identified as an owl (ulula), though it features a long, curved neck and a long beak. The bird is set against a dark blue background representing the night sky, dotted with several white six-pointed stars. The bird is colored in light grey and white, shown in profile but with its head turned to look back over its shoulder. At the bottom right of the frame, there is a small green floral ornament.</image-desc>

The screech owl <note>original: "Vlula"</note> is a bird named from wailing and mourning <note>original: "apoty olōȳzū," a transliteration of the Greek "ἀπὸ τοῦ ὀλολύζειν" (apo tou ololyzein), meaning "from the act of howling or wailing."</note>. For when it cries out, it imitates either weeping or groaning. Whence also among the augurs <note>Ancient Roman observers of bird signs.</note>, it is said that its silence shows sadness, while if it is quiet it is said to show prosperity. This bird can signify the wailing of sinners in hell. Hence the prophet, when mention is made of the devastation of Babylon, says that screech owls shall dwell in its houses <note>A reference to Isaiah 13:21, describing the ruins of Babylon.</note>; and so too the pelican <note>original: "onocrotalus"</note> and the hedgehog <note>original: "ericuis," likely a misspelling of "ericius."</note>.

<summary>This page describes the peacock as a symbol of the Gentile converts to Christianity due to its exotic origin and beauty, and the screech owl as a symbol of mourning and the fate of sinners in the ruins of Babylon.</summary>
<keywords>Peacock, Screech Owl, Solomon, Symbolism, Gentiles, Babylon, Christian Allegory, Bestiary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the bestiary's descriptions of birds and their moral lessons. Following the entries on the Peacock and Screech Owl from the previous page, this section discusses the Hoopoe—focusing on both its perceived "filthy" habits and its "pious" care for its elderly parents—and begins the entry for the Rooster.</meta>

<page-num>90.</page-num>

<margin>The hoopoe</margin>

<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in orange and black depicts a bird with a long, thin beak and a prominent feather crest, identified by the text as a hoopoe. It stands on a light-colored rectangular structure, possibly a tomb or altar. The background within the circle transitions from a dark blue ground to a reddish-pink sky containing several six-pointed stars.</image-desc>

The Greeks named the **hoopoe** <note>original: "Vpupam/Hupupa." The name is likely onomatopoeic, mimicking the bird's "oop-oop" call.</note> because it considers human excrement and feeds on stinking dung. It is a most filthy bird, crested and "helmeted" unlike others. It often lingers in tombs and amidst human stench. It is said that if someone anoints themselves with its blood before going to sleep, they will see demons suffocating them. 

Wherefore **Rabanus** <note>Rabanus Maurus, a 9th-century German Benedictine abbot and scholar who wrote a famous encyclopedia.</note> says: This bird signifies wicked men and sinners who continually delight in lingering in the filth of their sins. The hoopoe is also said to love mourning, because according to justice it brings about spiritual death. For this reason, it is necessary for him who loves God to always rejoice, to pray without ceasing, and in all things to give thanks, because joy is a spiritual fruit. 

Concerning the hoopoe, the **Physiologus** <note>An ancient Greek text that served as the primary source for medieval bestiaries, using animal lore to teach Christian morals.</note> also says that when the parent bird has grown old and can no longer fly, its children come to it and pluck the oldest feathers from its body; they do not cease to keep it warm until new feathers grow back. They support them, as nature dictates, until the parent regains its former strength and is able to fly once more. 

They provide this example against wicked men who expel their fathers from their homes when they grow old, and who refuse to support them in their frailty, even though those parents raised them when they were still little. Let rational man therefore see what he owes his father or mother, since even the <term>irrational creature</term> <note>A common medieval term for animals, which were believed to lack a "rational soul" but could still provide moral examples for humans.</note> we mentioned returns the favor to its parents in their necessity when they have grown old.

### The Rooster.

The **Rooster** <note>original: "Gallus"</note> is named from "castration." <note>This is a folk etymology from Isidore of Seville. He mistakenly linked the Latin "Gallus" (rooster) to the "Galli," the eunuch priests of Cybele who were castrated.</note> For among other birds...

<summary>This page describes the hoopoe, contrasting its "filthy" nature as a consumer of dung with its "pious" nature in caring for its elderly parents. It concludes by beginning the entry for the rooster.</summary>

<keywords>hoopoe, Rabanus Maurus, Physiologus, filial piety, rooster, bestiary, moral allegory, demons, spiritual death</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the bestiary's exploration of birds, moving from the physical characteristics and etymology of the rooster to a deeply poetic and theological reflection on its crowing. The text draws heavily from Saint Ambrose’s *Hexameron* and his famous morning hymn, *Aeterne rerum conditor*. It concludes by introducing the symbolic meaning of the hen.</meta>

# 27.

...from this bird alone the testicles are removed <note>original: "adimuntur." The text refers to the practice of caponization, the making of a capon.</note>. They called those who had been removed "roosters" <note>original: "gallos." In medieval etymology, there was often a confusing link made between the word for rooster and the word for castrated priest (Galli).</note>. Just as from "lion" <note>leo</note> comes "lioness" <note>leena</note>, and from "dragon" <note>draco</note> comes "dragoness" <note>dracena</note>, so from "rooster" <note>gallus</note> comes **hen** <note>gallina</note>. Some say that if its limbs are mixed with liquid gold, they are consumed.

<margin>The Rooster</margin>
<image-desc>A circular miniature showing a rooster with a red comb, orange neck feathers, and blue-grey body plumage. He stands in profile facing left on a green grassy mound within a blue-framed circle.</image-desc>

The **crowing of the rooster** <note>original: "gallicantus"</note> is also sweet in the nights, and not only sweet but also useful. Like a good housemate, he both awakens the sleeper and warns the anxious, and he comforts the traveler, marking the progress of the night with his melodic signal. 

When he crows, the thief abandons his ambushes. At this time, the **morning star** <note>original: "lucifer." In this context, it refers to the "light-bringer" or the planet Venus appearing at dawn, signaling the end of the night's terrors.</note>, being awakened, rises and illuminates the sky. When he crows, the fearful sailor sets aside his sorrow, and every storm, calmed by the frequent blowing of the western winds, grows mild. At his crowing, the devout heart is raised to prayer, and the duty of reading <note>original: "legendi munus." This refers to the Matins or the Night Office, where monks would read sacred texts.</note> is restored. 

Finally, when he crows, the very **Rock of the Church** <note>Saint Peter. The text alludes to the biblical account where Peter denies Jesus three times before the rooster crows.</note> washed away his own fault, for before the rooster crowed, he had been convicted of his denial. By his crowing, hope returns to all people: the sick find relief from their distress, the pain of wounds is diminished, the burning of fevers is mitigated, and faith returns to those who have fallen. Jesus looks upon those who are watching; he corrects those who wander. Finally, he looked upon Peter, and immediately his error departed; his denial was driven away, and confession followed. This did not happen by chance, but the reading teaches us it was according to the Lord’s judgment.

<margin>The Hen</margin>
<image-desc>A circular miniature depicting a hen in profile, facing left, with several small yellow chicks. One chick rests on the hen's back while others are gathered on the ground beneath her. The scene is set against a blue background with gold decorative motifs.</image-desc>

By the name of the **Hen**, the **wisdom of God** is designated; for thus it is said by her in the Gospel: "Jerusalem, Jeru—" <note>A reference to Matthew 23:37: "Jerusalem, Jerusalem... how often I have longed to gather your children together, as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings."</note>

<summary>This page describes the rooster not just as a farm animal, but as a spiritual timekeeper whose song drives away evil and signaled Saint Peter’s repentance. It also introduces the hen as a symbol of Divine Wisdom and Christ's protection.</summary>
<keywords>rooster, hen, Saint Peter, Morning Star, Saint Ambrose, repentance, Divine Wisdom, bestiary symbolism, Matins</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hen, transitioning from its physical nature to its symbolic role as a figure of Christ, before moving on to describe the duck and the goose. It maintains the theme of etymological origins and the moral qualities of animals.</meta>

...Jerusalem, you who kill the prophets! How often I wanted to gather your children together **just as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings**, and you were not willing <note>original: "Jerusalem que occidis pphas quoti uolui congregare filios tuos quemadmodum gallina congregat pullos suos sub alas & noluisti." A direct quote from Matthew 23:37.</note>. The name of the hen is not an insult.

Observe other birds. Many birds breed before our eyes and keep their chicks warm, but no other bird becomes so weakened <note>original: "infirmatur." The author suggests the hen physically suffers or expresses distress more visibly than other birds when caring for young.</note> along with her chicks as the **hen** does. We see **swallows**, **geese**, and **storks** in houses outside their nests, and we cannot tell whether they have offspring. But indeed, we recognize the hen by the weakness of her voice and the drooping of her feathers. She is completely changed by her affection for her chicks. Because they are weak, she makes herself weak. Because we too were weak, the **Wisdom of God** made itself weak for us, because "the Word was made flesh and dwelt among us."

---

<margin>The Duck</margin>
<image-desc>A circular miniature illustration framed in red, depicting two waterfowl swimming on stylized blue wavy water. The bird in the foreground is grey with a black head, a white neck ring, and a yellow beak. Behind it, a darker bird is visible. The background is a solid red field decorated with small white six-pointed stars.</image-desc>

The **duck** <note>original: "Anas."</note> received its name from its "constant swimming" <note>original: "ab assiduitate natandi." The author is making an etymological link between the Latin "Anas" and "assiduitas."</note>. From this genus, certain birds in **Germany** are named because they swim more than the others. The duck also gave the **goose** <note>original: "Anser."</note> its name through a derivation, either due to their similarity or because the goose also swims frequently.

The goose bears witness to the watches of the night with its constant honking. No animal senses the scent of a human as the goose does. Because of this, the ascent of the **Gauls** <note>original: "gallorum." This is a pun in Latin, as "galli" can mean both "roosters" and "Gauls." The text refers to the 390 BC Siege of Rome, where sacred geese alerted the Romans to a night attack on the Capitol hill.</note> on the **Capitol** was discovered by its honking. 

All kinds of birds swim well. First, they produce **eggs**; then, they are formed and given life by the heat of the mother’s body. Eggs <note>original: "oua."</note> are named for being full of moisture on the inside. For that which has moisture on the outside is called "humid," but that which has it on the inside is called "wet" <note>original: "uuidum." The author derives "ova" (eggs) from "uvidum" (wet/moist).</note>. Some, however, think the word "egg" has a Greek origin.

<summary>This page concludes a theological reflection on the hen as a symbol of Christ's protective love and introduces the duck and goose, highlighting the goose's historical role in saving Rome.</summary>

Eman <note>Likely a fragment of a name or reference, possibly related to the source of the etymology.</note>

...of the name. For they call them **oa**, with the letter "u" removed <note>original: "oa. u. littera ablata." The author is explaining that the Latin word for eggs, *ova*, is derived from a simpler form, *oa*.</note>. Some **eggs** are conceived by the "empty wind" <note>original: "inani uento concipiunt." A reference to the ancient belief in "wind-eggs" or unfertilized eggs produced without a mate.</note>, but they are not capable of generating life unless they have been conceived through male intercourse and penetrated by the seminal spirit. They say the power of eggs is so great that wood soaked in them will not burn, nor will even a garment be scorched against them. It is also said that when mixed with lime, egg whites can glue together fragments of glass. This can signify **pure men** who are well-vigilant in their own self-protection.

### Sparrows

<image-desc>A circular illumination with a thick orange-red border. The interior features a dark blue background scattered with small white six-pointed stars. Inside the circle are eight birds, likely sparrows, depicted in various naturalistic poses. They have light brown plumage on their backs and wings, with greyish-white underparts.</image-desc>

**Sparrows** <note>original: "Passeres."</note> are tiny flying creatures named for their smallness <note>original: "a paruitate uocata." A common medieval etymological link between *passer* (sparrow) and *parvus* (small).</note>, from which they are also called "tiny" and "small." Concerning the sparrow, it is written in the **Psalm**: *The sparrow has found a home for itself* <note>Psalm 84:3</note>, and elsewhere: *Like a solitary sparrow on a building* <note>Psalm 102:7</note>; and in another part of the **Psalter**: *I have kept watch on the mountain like a sparrow.* Also, sparrows are in the Psalter here: *There the sparrows shall build their nests* <note>Psalm 104:17</note>. The sparrow is extremely swift due to its excessive speed; it does not endure living in the woods, but longingly seeks out a home for itself in the holes of walls. When it finds one, it leaps lightly with great joy, because it believes it is no longer exposed to the various traps of its enemies. In this way, the **soul** is to be named when it senses that its dwelling place has been prepared in the kingdom of heaven. 

Likewise, those sparrows which were ordered to be offered for the **cleansing of the leper** in **Leviticus** <note>referencing Leviticus 14, where two birds were used in a purification ritual</note> represent the sacrament of the **Passion of Christ**, through which we are cleansed from the filth of our sins. This is what **Paul** shows when he says: *All of us who were baptized into Christ Jesus were baptized into his death* <note>Romans 6:3</note>. For in this, the living sparrow...

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the nature of birds. The previous page concluded with a description of the sparrow.
