...slowly inhabiting the dwellings assigned for the duty of producing honey. They compose their homes with inexpressible art and build them from various flowers. And they fill the waxen hives <note>original: "castra," literally "camps." The author uses military terminology to describe the hive.</note> with wax and countless offspring. They have armies and kings; they wage battles. They flee smoke; they are provoked by impurities. 

Many have experienced that these are born from the carcasses of oxen <note>This refers to the ancient "bugonia" myth, where bees were thought to be spontaneously generated from rotting cattle.</note>. For, to create them, the flesh of slaughtered calves is beaten so that from the putrefied blood worms are created, which later become **bees**. They are specifically called bees <note>original: "apes"</note> because they arise from oxen <note>original: "bobus." The author is making an etymological link between the Latin words for bees and oxen.</note>, just as hornets come from horses, drones from mules, and wasps from donkeys. 

The Greeks call them **castros** <note>original: "Castros." A term for the leaders of the hive.</note>; these are the larger ones created at the edges of the honeycombs, which some think are called "kings" because they lead the camp. Alone among all kinds of living creatures, bees have offspring common to all; they all inhabit one mansion; they are enclosed by the boundary of a single fatherland. Labor is common to all; common food; common work; common use and enjoyment; common flight. 

What more? Generation is common to all; the integrity of a virginal body is common to all, as is birth, since they do not mingle with each other in any sexual intercourse <term>concubitu: the act of lying together or mating</term>, nor are they weakened by lust, nor are they shaken by the pains of birth. Suddenly they send forth a massive swarm of children, gathering their offspring with their mouths from leaves and herbs <note>Medieval naturalists believed bees did not give birth but collected their young from plants.</note>. They ordain a king for themselves; they create their own peoples; and although placed under a king, they are nonetheless free. For they hold both the prerogative of judgment and the affection of faithful devotion, because they love him as if he were their own substance and honor him with such great respect. 

Moreover, the king is not chosen by lot, because in a lot there is chance, not judgment. By that often unreasonable accident of the lot, a flawed individual is often preferred over better ones. For bees, however, the king is by nature...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the bee (Latin: apis), specifically focusing on the hierarchy within the hive and the architectural marvel of the honeycomb. It follows a section describing the legendary origins of bees from the carcasses of oxen.</meta>

...[The king] is formed with clear markings, distinguished by the size of his body as well as his appearance. Furthermore, what is most exceptional in the king is the gentleness of his character <note>original: "moru mansuetudine." The author is likely drawing from Saint Ambrose’s *Hexameron*, which uses the bee as a moral example for human rulers.</note>. For even if he possesses a stinger, he does not use it for vengeance; for there are laws of nature, not written in letters but impressed upon characters, so that those who lead with the greatest power should be gentler in punishing. 

Indeed, even those bees which do not obey the laws of the king fine themselves with a condemnation of repentance, so that they die from the wound of their own stinger. The Persian people are said to observe this even today: that by an agreement for an offense committed, they carry out the sentence of their own death upon themselves. Therefore, no one observes their king—neither the Persians, who have the most just laws for their subjects, nor the Indians, nor the Sarmatian people <note>The Sarmatians were nomadic tribes of the Iranian area, often used in classical texts as examples of disciplined or fierce societies.</note>—with as much reverence and devotion as the bees. 

They do not dare to leave their homes or go out to any feeding grounds unless the king has gone out first and claimed the leadership of the flight for himself. They are called forth by the fragrant morning dew, where they breathe upon the flowers with their mouths, where the streams flow through the greenery, and where the banks are pleasant. There is the play of eager youth; there is the lap of the field; there is the release from cares. 

The work itself is sweet, made from flowers and sweet herbs. They lay the first foundations of their **camps** <note>original: "castrorum." The author consistently uses military terminology to describe the hive.</note>. For honeycombs are nothing other than a kind of camp. Finally, the drone is kept away from these enclosures of the bees. Even square camps cannot contain such organized spaces and groups as do the structures of the honeycombs, in which tiny and round cells support one another by their mutual connection. 

What architect taught them to compose those **hexagonal** <note>original: "hexagona." The author marvels at the mathematical precision of the six-sided cell.</note> cells with such distinct equality of sides? Or to suspend thin waxen partitions between the chambers of their houses, to pack away the honey, and to swell their granaries—filled from flowers—with a certain nectar? All may be seen to compete in their duty: some keep watch...

**P**erindens <note>original: "Perindens." This is a variation of the "Peridexion" tree, a legendary tree of India frequently described in medieval bestiaries as a symbol of protection against evil.</note> is a tree in India. The fruit of this tree is sweet—that is, life-giving—and exceedingly pleasant. Now, doves delight in the fruits of this tree and live within it, feeding upon its fruit. The dragon, however, is an enemy to the doves; he fears the tree and its shadow where the doves remain, and he cannot approach the tree nor its shadow. For if the shadow of the tree falls to the west, the dragon flees to the east; and if its shadow falls to the east, he flees to the west. But if it should happen that a dove is found outside the tree or its shadow, the dragon kills her. 

Understand the tree to be God the Father. The shadow is the Son, just as the angel Gabriel said to Mary: "The Holy Spirit shall come upon you, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow you" <note>original: "Spiritus sanctus superveniet in te et virtus altissimi obumbrabit tibi." A quotation from the Gospel of Luke 1:35.</note>. The celestial fruit is wisdom—that is, the Lord. The dove is the Holy Spirit. 

See therefore, O man, that after you have received the Holy Spirit—that is, the spiritual understanding descending from heaven and remaining upon you—you do not become a brother estranged from charity, away from the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit; otherwise, the dragon—that is, the devil—may invade you. For if you possess the Holy Spirit, the dragon cannot approach you. Pay heed, therefore, O man, and remain in the universal faith; dwell there and persevere there in the one universal Church. Take care as much as you can lest you be found outside the house of the spring <note>original: "domum fontis," likely referring to the source of life or the baptismal font within the Church.</note>, and that dragon, the ancient serpent, seize you and devour you just as he did Judas, who, as soon as he went out from the Lord and his brothers the apostles, was immediately devoured by the demon and perished.

# God be Mercifull unto me And blesse me And show the light of his Countynance upon me Amen /

When it is said that the lip is held so that it might be thought a serpent <note>This likely refers to a missing transition from the previous page's allegory, perhaps suggesting how the devil (the serpent) is recognized by his speech or "lip."</note>. Now, the serpent <note>original: "Serpens."</note> received its name because it creeps <term>serpit: to crawl or glide</term> by hidden approaches; it does not move with open steps, but crawls by the smallest exertions of its scales. But serpents are those reptiles that crawl on their belly and breast. Of these, there are as many kinds of poison as there are species; as many deaths as there are pains; and as many varieties as there are colors.

Yet he is conquered by weasels <note>original: "mustelis"</note>, which people bring into the caves where the basilisks hide. Thus, once the basilisk is seen, he flees, but the weasel pursues and kills him. For the Parent of all things <note>A reference to God the Creator</note> established nothing without a remedy. 

The Basilisk is half a foot in length and marked with white spots. These "little kings" <note>original: "Reguli," a Latin translation of the Greek "basiliskos," meaning "little king"</note>, like scorpions, seek out sandy places, and once they reach water, they make those who drink it rabid <note>original: "idrophobas," those who fear water</note> and mad. The *Sibilus* <note>The "hisser"</note> is the same as the "little king," for he kills with a hiss <note>original: "Sibilo"</note> before he can even bite or burn his victim. 

The Basilisk signifies the Devil, who infects the unwary with poison, though not always openly. But just as the beast is overcome by other animals, the Devil is overcome by the soldier of Christ <note>original: "milite christi"</note> who places all his hope in Him, whose virtue has overcome and trampled all adversarial powers. Concerning this, the prophet also says in the Psalms:

> You shall walk upon the asp and the basilisk, 
> and you shall trample the lion and the dragon. 
> <note>A translation of Psalm 91:13</note>

Here, such divine power is expressed, which has commanded such great and poisonous things. For all these names are fittingly applied to the Devil. The **Asp** represents him when he strikes in secret; the **Basilisk** when he spreads his poison openly. The **Lion** represents him when he pursues the innocent; the **Dragon** when he swallows the negligent with sheer voracity. Truly, all these creatures lay prostrate beneath the feet of the Lord at His glorious arrival. For He alone was able to subdue such ferocity—He who is proven to exist as co-eternal and consubstantial with the Father according to His divinity. # The Viper

<image-desc>A circular miniature framed in red and gold. Within the circle, a large, mottled brown and white serpent (a viper) is coiled. Its head is turned back toward its body. Three smaller serpent heads are shown bursting out from the middle of its coiled length, illustrating the medieval belief that young vipers were born by gnawing through their mother's side.</image-desc>

The Viper <note>original: "Vipa" / "Vipera"</note> is so named because it gives birth by force <note>original: "vi pariat." Medieval scholars mistakenly believed the word "vipera" came from "vi" (force) and "parere" (to give birth), because they thought the young forced their way out of the mother's body.</note>. For when her womb is ready for birth, the young—not waiting for the natural release of nature—gnaw through her sides and burst out, resulting in the death of the mother. It is also said that the male spits his seed into the mouth of the viper. She, however, turning from pleasure into a frenzy, bites off the head of her mate which she has received in her mouth. Thus it happens that both parents perish: the male during mating and the female during birth. 

Regarding the viper, Saint Ambrose <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Milan whose writings on the natural world were frequently quoted in bestiaries.</note> says that it is not the largest kind of beast, yet it is more cunning than all other types of serpents. When it is seized by a desire for mating, it seeks out a union with a sea-murena <term>Murena: The Moray eel</term> known to it, or prepares a new one. Going down to the shore, it announces its presence with a hiss to call the eel to a marital embrace. The invited murena does not fail to appear, and seeks out the union of the venomous serpent for her own impure joining. 

What this discourse means to convey to us is that the behavior of spouses should not be like this. Even if a spouse is absent, their presence is to be sought with longing; [one should not be] deceitful or slippery in drunken instability. Why does the murena not flee the venom in her mate? When called, she does not fail to come, and she embraces the slippery serpent with diligent affection. He carries your faults and the levity of feminine ease; you, woman, must endure your husband if you can. Adam deceived his partner, as did Eve through Adam, whom she called into sin.

<summary>This page describes the Viper's mythical and violent reproductive cycle and its legendary mating with the sea-murena, using these stories as a moral allegory for human marriage and the Fall of Man.</summary>
<keywords>Viper, Murena, Saint Ambrose, Marriage, Morality, Bestiary, Etymology, Original Sin</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues St. Ambrose’s moralized account of the viper. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the viper's "invitation" to the moray eel for their unusual cross-species mating. Here, Ambrose moves from the natural (or mythological) behavior of the viper to a direct sermon on the duties of husbands and wives.</meta>

...tation to the union. It is right for the woman to take him as her guide, so that she does not fall through a certain feminine instability <note>original: "feminea facilitate" — literally "feminine ease" or "facility," here implying a lack of steadfastness</note>. But you say, "He is rough and unrefined." Once it pleased you to marry him. Is a husband to be chosen frequently? Even the ox seeks out its own mate, and the horse loves its own. And even if it were another beast of burden, if it does not know how to pull the yoke, it seeks the mate of another and does not consider itself a fitting partner. 

You repudiate the marriage bond <note>original: "iugale" — referring to the "yoke" of marriage</note> for a year, and you think a spouse must be changed often. And if he should be absent for but a single day, do you bring in a rival? And do you immediately pursue an injury to your modesty for an unknown cause, when the cause ought first to have been known? 

The viper seeks the absent mate; she calls the absent one and cries out with a charming hiss. And when she senses her mate arriving, she vomits up her venom, showing reverence to her husband and feeling a bashful respect for the grace of the marriage. You, woman, repel with insults a husband arriving from a long journey. The viper looks out over the sea and explores the path of her spouse. You block the path of your husband’s life. You stir up quarrels as if they were poisons—not just once, but constantly. You, at the time of the conjugal embrace, boil over with a terrible venom. You do not blush for the marriage, nor do you revere your husband. 

But also you, man—for we can take the lesson this way as well: lay aside the bitterness of your heart and your harshness of manners when your diligent wife meets you. Drive away your indignation when your charming spouse calls you to love. He is not a master <note>original: "dominus"</note> but a husband; he has not obtained a slave-girl but a wife. God willed you to be the guide <note>original: "gubernatorem"</note> of the inferior sex, not an all-powerful tyrant. Repay her devotion in turn; return the favor of her love. If the viper pours out its venom, can you not lay aside the hardness of your mind? 

If one’s nature has a natural stiffness, it ought to soften with the contemplation of marriage, and lay aside the ferocity of the soul out of reverence for the union. It can also be understood in this way: Men, do not seek another's bed; do not lie in wait for another's union. Adultery is a grave sin, a violation of nature... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "minu..." likely "minuit" or "minuens," continuing the warning against adultery.</note>

<summary>St. Ambrose uses the legend of the viper—which supposedly vomits its venom before mating—as a moral lesson urging husbands and wives to set aside harshness and infidelity. He emphasizes that marriage should be a partnership of mutual respect rather than one of mastery or constant quarreling.</summary>

...is against. <note>original: "tra est" — the end of a word from the previous page, likely "contra est" (it is against)</note> In the beginning, God made two, Adam and Eve: that is, one man and one wife. And he made the wife from the man—that is, from the rib of Adam—and he united them both to be in one body and to live in one spirit. What then separates one body? What divides one spirit? This is the work of adultery. But this is taught by the example <note>original: "munere" — literally a gift or duty, here meaning the natural "lesson" provided by the creature</note> of the viper, though she is born not by law but by the heat of lust and an expected embrace. Learn, O men, that a wife is to be sought before a foreign woman. Who would desire to take the companionship of serpents for himself? Only he who has himself become like a serpent. He hastens to the viper, who pours herself into your bosom, entering not by straight paths but through the slippery ways of a devious love. He hastens to her who takes back her venom on the way; who, by the pact of the gift of union, drinks again the venom she had previously vomited up. <note>Ambrose refers to the ancient belief that the female viper vomits her poison before mating out of respect for her husband, but re-absorbs it afterward; he uses this as a warning against the "venomous" nature of adulterous relationships.</note>

## The Asp

<image-desc>A circular miniature framed by red and blue borders. The background is a dark blue night sky filled with white stars. Inside the circle, a serpent with a dragon-like head and a long, winding tail is depicted. To the right, a man in a red tunic and a light skullcap enters the frame, holding a long, flowing white scroll that wraps around him. The scroll bears the inscription: "You shall walk upon the asp and the basilisk" <note>original: "Super aspidem & basilicum ambula" — a reference to Psalm 91:13, signifying triumph over evil</note>.</image-desc>

The Asp <term>Aspis; a venomous snake often used in medieval bestiaries to represent various sins or the refusal to hear the word of God</term> is so named because it injects and scatters venom through its bite. For the Greeks call venom *ios* <note>original: "Ios" — Greek ἰός</note>, and from this comes the name "asp," because it strikes with a poisonous bite. It is said that... <note>original: "Quirit qđ ſe p̄ pa" — the text here is abbreviated and fragmented; it likely refers to the asp protecting itself from the charmer's music</note>

<summary>St. Ambrose concludes his sermon on marriage and adultery by comparing the unfaithful man to a serpent, then begins an encyclopedic entry on the Asp, focusing on its Greek etymology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on serpents, transitioning from the moral lessons drawn from the mating of vipers to the specific lore of the Asp. The text draws heavily from the *Etymologies* of Isidore of Seville and the *Pharsalia* of Lucan, blending folklore with Christian allegory.</meta>

...with a gaping mouth and steaming breath. Of this serpent, there are diverse kinds and species, with various effects for doing harm. It is said, moreover, that when the asp perceives the charmer—who summons it with specific incantations to draw it out from its cavern—if the serpent does not wish to emerge, it presses one ear against the earth and blocks and stops up the other with its tail. Thus, it will not hear those magical voices, even if it is summoned by spells. 

Such indeed are the men of this world who, immersed in earthly desires, press down one ear to the ground. The other ear they block with their sins, lest they hear the voice of the Lord saying: "Whoever does not renounce all that he possesses cannot be my disciple nor my servant." <note>A reference to Luke 14:33. The author is using the legendary behavior of the "deaf adder" to criticize those who ignore spiritual calls in favor of material wealth.</note> The asps do this only to stop their ears; but these men also blind their eyes so that they might not see heaven, nor remember the Lord.

<margin>Dipsas</margin>
The **Dipsas** is a kind of asp, which in Latin is called "bucket" <note>original: "situla"; the name likely refers to the unquenchable thirst of the victim, as if they could drink a whole bucket and still perish.</note> because whoever it bites dies of thirst. The **Hypnalis** <note>original: "Ypnalis"</note> is a kind of asp so named because it kills by sleep. Cleopatra applied this snake to herself, and was released by this death as if by sleep. The **Haemorrhois** <note>original: "Emonoris"</note> asp is so named because the victim sweats blood. Whoever has been bitten by it grows so faint that whatever life is in them is dissolved and drawn out through the blood. For in Greek, blood is called *haema* <note>original: "emach"</note>. 

The **Prester** asp always runs with its mouth open and steaming, of which the poet <note>The Roman poet Lucan in his epic *Pharsalia*.</note> remembers thus: "The greedy prester distending its smoking mouth." <note>original: "Oraque distendens avidus fumantia prester"</note> Whomever this snake has burned, it causes to swell up, and it kills them with an enormous corpulence, for rot follows the heaviness of the body. The **Seps** <note>original: "Spectaficus", a variant or corruption of "Seps" found in other medieval bestiaries.</note> asp is so called because when it bites a man, it immediately consumes him, so that he entirely melts away in the mouth of the serpent.

<margin>Cerastes</margin>
The **Cerastes** serpent is so named because it has horns on its head similar to a ram. In Greek, horns are called *kerata*. <note>original: "cerastes" / "cerata"</note> Moreover, it has four...

->Scitalis<-

<image-desc>A circular miniature with a blue background framed in red and gold. It depicts a Scitalis as a winged, two-legged creature with a long, striped, and coiled serpentine body. Its head is small with a pointed ear, looking upwards.</image-desc>

The serpent called the **Scitalis** <note>Also spelled *Scytale*. The name is derived from the Greek word for a staff or rod, referring to its uniform thickness.</note> is so named because it shines with such a great variety of markings on its back that the beauty of these spots slows down those who look upon it. And because it is slow in crawling, those it cannot catch by speed, it captures as they stand stunned by the wonder of its appearance. It possesses such great internal heat that even in the time of winter, it exposes the glowing vital heat of its body. Of this snake, the poet Lucan writes: *“And the Scitalis alone, even while the frosts still weigh down, is about to cast off its skin.”* <note>original Latin: "Et scitalis pressis etiam nunc sola pruinis, exuvias positura suas." Lucan, Pharsalia 9.707. This refers to the belief that the snake's internal heat allowed it to molt even in winter.</note>

->Amphisbaena<-

<image-desc>A circular miniature with a reddish-pink background framed in red and gold. It depicts an Amphisbaena as a thick-bodied serpent with two small legs and a head at each end of its body. The body forms a circle, bringing both heads into the center where they face one another.</image-desc>

The **Amphisbaena** <note>From the Greek *amphis*, meaning 'both ways', and *bainein*, 'to go'.</note> is so named because it has two heads: one in its usual place, and the other at its tail. It can run from either head with a flexible, jointed movement of its body. This is the only one of the serpents that entrusts itself to the cold, coming out before all others. Of this snake, the same Lucan says: *“And the heavy Amphisbaena, swelling with its twin heads.”* <note>original Latin: "Et gravis ingeminum turgens caput anphisivena." Lucan, Pharsalia 9.719.</note> Its eyes shine like lamps.

### . 101 .

<image-desc>A rectangular miniature illustration of a mythical beast called the Hydrus. The creature is greenish with scales, four clawed legs, and a long tail that curls into a decorative scroll. Three long, green tube-like tongues or tusks emerge from its open mouth. The background is a flat terracotta red, set within a red-ruled border. **E**verything begins with an animal in the river Nile called the **hydrus**, which lives in the water. For the Greeks call water *hydros*. From this, it is called "aquatic." <note>The author uses the Greek word *hydor* (ὕδωρ) to explain the etymology of the creature's name.</note> It is a serpent by which cattle are afflicted with a disease that some call **boa**, because it is cured by the dung of oxen. <note>original: "fimo bouum remedetur." This refers to a folk belief that certain snakes, called boas, followed herds and were linked to cattle ailments or cures.</note>

The **Hydra** is a dragon of many heads, such as the one that lived in the swamp of Lerna in the province of Arcadia. In Latin, this creature is called an **excetra** <term>excetra: a Latin term for a multi-headed snake or water-serpent</term> because when one head was cut off, three more grew in its place. 

However, this is a myth; for it is well-known that the "Hydra" was actually a place that spewed forth water, devastating the neighboring city, where if one opening was closed, many more would burst out. Hercules, seeing this, drained the area and thus closed the flow of the water. <note>This is a "rationalist" interpretation of myth, suggesting Hercules was a hero of hydraulic engineering rather than a monster-slayer.</note> For "Hydra" is named after water. 

This **hydrus** is a great enemy of the **crocodile**, and it has this nature and habit: when it sees a crocodile sleeping on the shore, it opens its mouth and rolls itself in mud, so that it might more easily slip into the jaws... <note>Medieval bestiaries usually complete this story by explaining that the mud-covered hydrus slides down the crocodile's throat and eats its way out of the stomach, symbolizing Christ’s harrowing of Hell.</note>

<summary>This page describes the water-dwelling Hydrus and the multi-headed Hydra, offering a historical explanation for the myth of Hercules and the Hydra as a feat of land drainage. It also introduces the legendary combat between the Hydrus and the crocodile.</summary>

The **Boas** is a serpent of Italy; with its massive bulk, it pursues herds of cattle and oxen. It is nourished by a great deal of milk; it entwines itself around the cows, and by sucking their udders and from this "devastation of cattle," it received the name **Boas**. <note>The name is derived from the Latin *bos*, meaning "ox." They stay in trees, and whenever any animal comes across them, they hurl themselves upon it and kill it. # The **Dipsas** serpent is likewise said to be tiny; <image-desc>A square illustration of a blue-grey serpent biting the heel of a human foot; the word "Dipsa." is written in red ink next to the foot.</image-desc>
so much so that when it is stepped upon, it is not even seen, since its venom kills before it can even be felt. Consequently, the face of the dead does not show sadness before death. # The **Lizard** is a type of reptile named

There are many kinds of lizards: the **ubotrax** <note>This may be a corruption of the Greek *hypotricos* or a variant of *ebitris* found in other medieval texts.</note>, the **salamander**, the **saura** <note>From the Greek *sauros*, simply meaning lizard.</note>, 
<image-desc>A green and brown lizard with black spots and a long tail, depicted within a red-bordered rectangular frame.</image-desc>
and the **Stellio** <note>The "star-lizard," named for the star-like spots on its back.</note>. # The **Salamander** is so named because it prevails against 
fire. Among all venomous creatures, its power is the greatest. For while others strike individuals, this one kills many at once. If it crawls into a tree, it infects all the fruit with its venom and kills those who eat them. Even if it should fall into a well, the power of its venom kills those who drink the water. These creatures resist fire; alone among animals, they extinguish flames. For the salamander lives in the midst of flames without pain or being consumed; it is not only unburned, but it actually puts out the fire.

**S**erpent indeed has three natures. Its first
nature is this: when it has grown old
and its eyes grow dim, and if it
wishes to be made new again, it abstains

<margin>The serpent has three natures.</margin>
Its second nature is that when it comes to a river to drink water, it
does not carry its venom with it, but leaves it behind in a hole.
We too, when we come together to draw the living and
eternal water, listening to the divine word and the heavenly
medicine, must cast away from us our venom—that is, earthly and evil
desires. Its third nature is this: If it sees a naked
man, it fears him; but if it sees him clothed, it leaps upon him.
In this same way, let us understand spiritually that as long as the first man, Adam,
was naked in Paradise, the serpent did not prevail in
leaping upon him. But after he was "clothed"—that is, with the
mortality of the body—then the serpent leaped upon him.
If, therefore, you have within you this mortal garment—
that is, the "old man"—and have grown old in evil days,
the serpent leaps upon you. If, however, you strip yourself of the garment of the first... <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page; it likely concludes by advising the reader to strip off the "garment" of sin to be safe from the devil's reach.</note>

<meta>This page continues the allegorical and biological description of the serpent. ...[of princes] and powers of the darkness of this world. Then that serpent—that is, the devil—will not be able to leap out. The serpent also, through feeding, repels the blindness of old age that has overtaken it. And so, when it feels its eyes being clouded, it seeks out known remedies and is not cheated of the result. <note>Medieval bestiaries often claim that snakes eat fennel to clear their vision when it becomes cloudy with age.</note>

The turtle, having fed on the entrails of serpents, when it feels the poison affecting it, seeks the means of its own health through a medicinal plant. <note>This likely refers to wild marjoram or oregano, which ancient and medieval writers believed turtles used as an antidote to snake venom.</note> If a serpent tastes the spit of a fasting man, it dies. <note>A common folk belief in antiquity, recorded by Pliny the Elder, suggesting that human saliva—especially from someone who hasn't eaten—was toxic to venomous creatures.</note> It is said, however, and believed by Pliny, <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (Pliny the Elder), a 1st-century Roman author whose "Natural History" was the primary source for medieval science.</note> that if someone squeezes a serpent's head even with two fingers, it nevertheless lives. Therefore, they offer their whole body to those who strike them in order to protect their head. 

All snakes have dim vision. They rarely look directly at an opponent, and not without reason, since they have eyes not on the forehead but on the temples; thus they hear more quickly than they see. No animal moves its tongue with such speed as the serpent, so much so that it seems to have a triple tongue, although it is actually only one. The bodies of serpents are moist, so much so that wherever they go, they mark their path with moisture.

The tracks of serpents are such that, although they seem to lack feet, they crawl by the exertion of their ribs and scales. These are arranged in an equal manner from the top of the throat to the bottom of the belly. For the scales lean upon the ribs like claws in place of feet. Hence, if it is struck in any part of the body from the belly to the head, it is rendered weak so that it cannot maintain its course; because wherever that blow falls, it breaks the spine, through which the "feet" of the ribs and the movements of the body were driven. 

Serpents are said to live a long time, so much so that by casting off their old skin, they can shed their old age and return to youth. The coverings of serpents are called <term>exuviae: the cast-off skin or "slough" of a snake</term> because they strip themselves of these when they grow old. They are also called "slough" and "garment," because they are stripped off and put on again. Through the wandering...

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the origin of serpents—specifically a bizarre folk belief linking them to human remains—before moving into the entry for the scorpion. It follows the standard medieval encyclopedic format: first defining the creature's physical nature and etymology (often based on Isidore of Seville), followed by a "moralized" or allegorical interpretation based on scripture.</meta>

[...others] say that a serpent is created from the marrow of a dead man which is in the spine. <note>original: "medulla hominis mortui que in spina est"</note> This is even mentioned by Ovid in his books of *Metamorphoses*, saying: *“There are those who believe that when the spine has rotted in the tomb, human marrow is changed into a snake.”* <note>Ovid, *Metamorphoses* 15.389. This reflects an ancient belief that the spinal cord and its marrow were composed of the same substance as a serpent.</note> If this is believed, it is a wondrous outcome: just as man’s death came through a serpent, so through man’s death a serpent is born.

### **Scorpio**

**The Scorpion** is a worm of the earth. <note>In the medieval system of classification, "worms" (*vermes*) included many small crawling creatures, including insects and arachnids, not just earthworms.</note> It is ascribed to the category of worms rather than serpents; it is an animal armed with a sting, and is named so in Greek because it strikes with its tail and spreads its venom through a curved wound. <note>The text suggests an etymology where "scorpion" relates to "striking" or "stinging" with its tail.</note> It is a particular trait of the scorpion that if it strikes the palm of the hand...

<image-desc>A circular miniature depicting a medieval artistic interpretation of a scorpion. The creature has a frog-like body with four legs and a broad head, but features a long, segmented tail typical of a scorpion. It is rendered in pale green and brown tones against a vibrant blue background, all enclosed within a frame of red and white concentric circles.</image-desc>

The scorpion signifies the devil or his ministers, which is the source of that saying of the Lord in the Gospel: *“Behold,”* he says, *“I have given you the power of treading upon serpents and scorpions, and over all the power of the enemy.”* <note>Luke 10:19. In medieval theology, the scorpion—which carries its threat in its tail—often symbolized flattery or deceit: someone who speaks fair to your face but "stings" you from behind.</note> For elsewhere the Lord says in the Gospel: *“Who is there among you, who if his son asks for bread, will he give him a stone? Or if he asks for a fish, will he give him a serpent? Or if he asks for an egg, will he stretch out to him a scorpion?”* <note>Luke 11:11-12.</note>

Three things are most necessary to us: that is, faith, hope, and charity. <note>The "theological virtues" from 1 Corinthians 13:13.</note> Let us believe faithfully and rightly; let there be hope so that we may always await the promises of God with good patience; and let there be charity, so that we may diligently love Him with all our heart, all our mind, and all our strength, and our neighbors as [ourselves...]

<meta>This page continues the moralizing interpretation of Christ's teachings regarding the fish, the egg, and the bread (Luke 11:11-12) before transitioning into a new entry on the Coluber (Snake) and the Cerastes (Horned Serpent). The provenance note at the top suggests this manuscript was once in the possession of the 17th-century polymath Edward Herbert, 1st Baron Herbert of Cherbury.</meta>

<insert>Edward Lord Herbert his hand Witnesse the same</insert> <note>This inscription indicates that Edward Herbert (1583–1648), a prominent English diplomat, poet, and philosopher, owned or handled this book.</note>

...ourselves. In faith, the fish is signified; in hope, the egg; in charity, the bread. Conversely, hardness is expressed in the stone, unfaithfulness in the serpent, and despair in the scorpion. <note>The author is contrasting the "good gifts" mentioned in the Gospels—bread, fish, and eggs—with their opposites: stones, serpents, and scorpions.</note> In baptism, faith is received, by which one may live in this world; hope is found in the egg, because the chick is not yet seen in the egg, but is hoped for from it. Charity, however, is compared to bread because it overcomes all things. <note>In medieval theology, charity (caritas) is considered the "bread of life" that sustains all other virtues.</note>

### Coluber.
### **The Snake**

<image-desc>A circular miniature with a red and blue border. The upper half depicts an eel-like water snake (coluber) with blue and white stripes swimming in wavy water. The lower half shows a horned serpent (cerastes) with red and white stripes emerging from the earth to bite a bird on its wing. The bird is brown and white and appears to be flying just above the ground.</image-desc>

The snake <term>coluber<note>The text uses the Latin *coluber*, often translated as "snake" or "adder."</note></term> is so named because it "inhabits shadows" <note>original: *colat umbras*—a medieval folk etymology linking the name *coluber* to the words for inhabiting shadows.</note> or because it glides into slippery paths with winding coils. For anything that slips away while being held, like a fish or a serpent, is called "slippery" <note>original: *lubricum*.</note>. <margin>The Horned Serpent.</margin> The **Horned Serpent** <note>original: *Cerastes*.</note> is so called because it has horns on its head like those of rams. For in Greek, horns are called *kerata*. <note>original: *cerasta enim grece cornua dicuntur*. The Greek word is κέρατα (*kerata*).</note> Moreover, it has four small horns; by displaying these, it entices animals as if with bait, and then kills the creatures it has lured. It covers its entire body with sand and gives no sign of its presence, except for the part with which it catches the birds or animals it has invited. It is more flexible than other serpents, so much so that it seems to have no spine. By the names of the snake and the horned serpent, the Devil or the Antichrist is signified; as in the prophecy that the patriarch Jacob spoke concerning his son Dan, saying: *"Let Dan be a snake in the path and a horned serpent in the way, biting the horse’s heels so that its rider falls backward."* <note>Genesis 49:17. In medieval exegesis, the Tribe of Dan was often associated with the Antichrist.</note> For not only does the snake represent the Antichrist...

<summary>This page concludes a symbolic comparison of Christian virtues (faith, hope, and charity) before describing the physical and allegorical nature of the snake and the horned serpent (Cerastes), linking them to the Antichrist through the biblical prophecy of Jacob.</summary>
<keywords>bestiary, coluber, cerastes, horned serpent, symbolism, faith, hope, charity, Jacob's prophecy, Tribe of Dan, Antichrist, Edward Herbert</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of serpents, specifically moving from the general snake (coluber) to the horned serpent (cerastes). The author uses these creatures to interpret the prophecy of Jacob regarding the tribe of Dan (Genesis 49:17), applying it to the future coming of the Antichrist and the spiritual state of the world.</meta>

but it is also called the <term>cerastes<note>The Cerastes is the Saharan horned viper (*Cerastes cerastes*), famous in medieval bestiaries for the small horn-like scales above its eyes.</note></term>. For in Greek, <gloss>cerasta</gloss> <note>original: "cerasta"</note> means "horns," and this serpent is said to be horned, as we mentioned above. Through this creature, the coming of the <term>Antichrist<note>In medieval theology, the Antichrist was a literal figure expected to appear before the Last Judgment, using both deception and brute force to lead the faithful astray.</note></term> is fittingly signified. He is armed against the lives of the faithful not only with the bite of pestilential preaching but also with the "horns" of worldly power. 

Now, who does not know that a "path" <note>original: *semita*</note> is narrower than a "way" <note>original: *uia*</note>? Therefore, the snake is "in the way" because it provokes people to walk in the broadness of this present life, flattering them, as it were, while it gives birth to sin. But it "bites in the way" because it consumes with the poison of its error those to whom it grants a false sense of liberty. 

But the <term>cerastes</term> is "in the path" because it observes the faithful who often constrain themselves to the narrow journeys of the heavenly commandments; it hinders them not only with the wickedness of cunning persuasion but also destroys them with the terror of its power. In the very distress of that journey, after offering the favors of a feigned sweetness, it raises up the "horns" of its authority. 

In this context, the "horse" suggests this world, which foams with its own pride through the motion of passing time. And because the Antichrist strives to seize the very ends of the world, that <term>cerastes</term> is said to "bite the horse's heels." To "bite the hoof of the horse," indeed, is to strike at the very end of the age. 

"So that its rider may fall backward." <note>This is a direct reference to Genesis 49:17: "Dan shall be a serpent by the way, an adder in the path, that biteth the horse heels, so that his rider shall fall backward."</note> The "rider of the horse" is anyone who is puffed up by the rages and honors of this world. He is said to fall "backward" and not "on his face," as <term>Saul<note>Referring to Saul of Tarsus (St. Paul) falling to the ground on the road to Damascus (Acts 9:4).</note></term> is remembered to have fallen. For to "fall on one's face" is to recognize one's own sins in this life and to weep for them in repentance.

<summary>This page provides a moral allegory of the horned serpent (cerastes), identifying it with the Antichrist who uses both deceit and power to corrupt the faithful. The author interprets the biblical prophecy of a serpent biting a horse's heels as the world's pride being brought low at the end of time.</summary>
<keywords>cerastes, horned viper, Antichrist, allegory, Genesis 49:17, Saul of Tarsus, repentance, pride, end of the world</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of serpents to the broader category of "worms" (vermes). In the medieval classification system, this includes insects, larvae, and various small invertebrates, often defined by their mode of birth or habitat.</meta>

. 110 .

**W**orms are animals that mostly arise from flesh, wood, or any earthly substance without any sexual <term>intercourse<note>The text refers to "spontaneous generation," a widely held ancient and medieval belief that small creatures like insects and worms were born directly from decaying matter rather than from parents.</note></term>. However, they are sometimes born from eggs, such as the scorpion. There are various types of worms: some belong to the earth, others to water, air, flesh, leaves, wood, or even clothing.

The **Spider** <note>original: "Aranea"</note> is considered a worm of the air, named either because it is nourished by the air or because it draws out long threads from its tiny body. Always intent upon its web, it never ceases to labor, maintaining a perpetual suspension in its own art.

The **Leech** <note>original: "Sanguisuga"</note> is called a water worm because it "sucks blood" <note>This is a literal translation of the Latin name *sanguis-suga*.</note>. It lies in wait for those drinking from the water. When it glides into their throats or attaches itself anywhere else, it drains their blood; and when it becomes soaked with the gore, it vomits out what it has swallowed so that it may suck fresh blood once again.

The **Cantharides** <note>original: "Cantarida"; likely the Spanish Fly or blister beetle.</note> is an earth worm which, if applied immediately to the human body, creates blisters full of fluid by its burning nature.

The **Millipede** <note>original: "Multipet"</note> is an earth worm named for its "multitude of feet." When it rolls into a ball, it is born under stones from moisture and earth.

The **Slug** <note>original: "Limax"</note> is called a worm of the mud <note>original: "limus"</note> because it is born in or from the mud; for this reason, it is always considered filthy and unclean.

**Silkworms** <note>original: "Bombices"</note> are worms of the leaves, from which silk clothing is made. It is called by this name because it "empties itself" while it produces its threads, and only air remains within it.

The **Caterpillar** <note>original: "Eruca"</note> is a worm of the leaves, found wrapped in vegetables or vine-shoots; its name comes from "eroding" <note>original: "erodendo"</note> or gnawing. The playwright **Plautus** mentions it: <note>original: "Immaturus nequam bestia & malefica"</note> <gloss>An immature, worthless, and malicious beast, wrapped in a vine-leaf.</gloss> This creature entangles itself and does not fly away like the locust, which runs here and there leaving the crop half-eaten; instead, the caterpillar remains, causing ruin to the fruits, consuming everything with its slow crawl and sluggish bites.

<summary>This page categorizes various "worms" (insects and invertebrates) by their habitats and behaviors, drawing on medieval etymologies and classical references like Plautus to describe creatures like spiders, leeches, and silkworms.</summary>

<keywords>bestiary, worms, insects, spontaneous generation, spiders, leeches, silkworms, caterpillars, Plautus, etymology</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of "worms" (vermes) from the previous page, following the encyclopedic tradition of Isidore of Seville. It moves from wood-boring insects to parasites of the human body and domestic environment, concluding with the symbolic and theological interpretations of the worm.</meta>

...takes. The Greeks call wood-boring insects **Teredos** <note>original: "teredonas"</note> because they eat by "wearing away" <note>original: "terendo"</note> the wood. We call these *vermines*. This is the name for woodworms among the Latins, which trees produce when they are cut at an inopportune time. The **Moth** <note>original: "Tinea"</note> is a worm of clothing, so named because it "holds" <note>original: "teneat"</note> and lurks there until it gnaws the fabric away; hence it is also called *panax*, because it drives the material toward the same bareness.

Worms of the flesh also emerge: the intestinal worm, the ascaris, maggots, lice, fleas, nits, the bacon-worm, the tick, the *usia*, and the bedbug.

<margin>E</margin> The **Caterpillar** <note>original: "Eruca"</note> is called a worm of garden-herbs. The **Intestinal Worm** <note>original: "Lumbricus"</note> is named as if it were "slippery" <note>original: "lubricus"</note> because it glides, or perhaps because it resides in the "loins" <note>original: "lumbis"</note>. **Ascarids**, **maggots** <note>original: "costi"</note>, and **lice** <note>original: "pediculi"</note> are worms of the flesh; lice are named after "feet" <note>original: "pedibus"</note>. From this, people are called "lousy" <note>original: "pedulosi"</note> when lice swarm upon their bodies. **Fleas** <note>original: "pulices"</note> are so named because they are primarily nourished by "dust" <note>original: "pulvere"</note>. 

The **Tarmus** is the worm found in bacon. The **Tick** <note>original: "Ricinus"</note> is a dog-worm, named because it clings to the ears of dogs; for in Greek, a dog is called *kyon* <note>original: "Cyenos"</note>. The **Usia** is a worm named because it "burns" <note>original: "urit"</note>. For wherever it bites, the place burns so intensely that blisters form there. The **Bedbug** <note>original: "Cimex"</note> is named for its resemblance to a certain herb whose foul smell it shares. 

Strictly speaking, a "worm" is born in rotting flesh; a **Moth** in clothes; a **Caterpillar** in vegetables; a **Teredo** in wood; and a **Tarmus** in bacon. Therefore, a worm crawls like a serpent by means of its steps or the effort of its scales; for it does not have the rigidity of a spine like a snake, but it achieves movement by gradually stretching out the contracted parts of its tiny body in a straight line, and then contracting the parts that were stretched, and so it glides along. 

In a mystical sense, the **Worm** can be understood as Christ through the humanity he assumed, as it says in the Psalm: "I am a worm and no man" <note>Psalm 22:6 (Psalm 21 in the Vulgate numbering). This was a common medieval metaphor for Christ's humility and his "baiting" of the devil.</note>. Or, it represents the memory of a sinner's soul established in the punishment of Hell, of which it is written: "their worm [shall not die]" <note>Isaiah 66:24 and Mark 9:44.</note>.

<summary>This page categorizes various "worms" (including insects and parasites) based on their habitats—wood, clothing, meat, or the human body—and explains their Latin and Greek etymologies. It concludes by linking the worm to Christ’s humility and the eternal suffering of the soul in Hell.</summary>

<keywords>vermes, etymology, woodworm, moth, lice, fleas, ticks, Christ as worm, Isidore of Seville, medieval science, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "worms" and small creatures, transitioning from physical descriptions (started on the previous page with wood-boring insects and parasites) to their moral and mystical allegories. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a biblical reference to eternal punishment.</meta>

...may die, and their fire shall not be extinguished. <note>A reference to Isaiah 66:24 or Mark 9:48, describing the "worm that dieth not."</note> The **Spider** <note>original: "Aranea"</note> truly signifies human fragility; hence it is written in the Psalm: *and you have made his soul waste away like a spider.* <note>Psalm 39:11. Medieval commentators often used the spider’s web as a metaphor for the temporary, fragile nature of human life.</note> The spider indeed has a thin body; it does not live on the ground, but in higher places it gathers very thin webs by the digestion of its internal organs. Just as silk is said to be spun from certain worms. Therefore, the soul of a convert and an afflicted person is rightly compared to this tiny little body—a soul fatigued by long observances and vigils, deserting earthly things, and performing most subtle operations through divine insight, brought to life by love. After this, it returns to the beginning of its profession; for although it may wither, although it may waste away, yet by the fragility of humanity, it is confounded by the variety of diverse things. Likewise, the spider signifies the deceit of the devil, as in Isaiah: *They have woven the webs of a spider.* <note>Isaiah 59:5.</note>

The **Leech** <note>original: "Sanguisuga"</note> indeed is the devil, hence the saying in Proverbs: *the leech has two daughters, saying: "Give, give."* <note>Proverbs 30:15. In medieval bestiaries, the leech’s thirst for blood was a common symbol for greed or demonic persistence.</note> The daughters of the devil, therefore, are **Lust** and **Greed** <note>original: "luxuria & auaricia"</note>, with which that bloodstained enemy infests the human race. **Rust** <note>original: "Erugo"; here referring to a blight or canker that consumes metal or plants.</note> can similarly signify the devil, who does not cease to gnaw at the budding virtues growing in man with evil persuasions and to uproot them with his vices. In the Holy Scriptures, therefore, where mention is made of the moth and the rust <note>A reference to Matthew 6:19-20.</note>, a mystical sense must be sought in them. For according to the **allegory** <note>original: "allegoria[m]"</note>, rust can signify arrogance and boasting; wherever it may be, it obscures all beauty and the strength of virtues, and scatters the treasure of **spiritual gifts** <note>original: "spualiu[m] donorum"</note>. Hence the prophet says: *The Lord scatters the bones of those who please themselves* <note>Psalm 53:5.</note>, and all arrogance is an abomination before God. The **Moth** <note>original: "Tinea"</note>, moreover, signifies an internal vice of the soul, of which the Lord says in the Gospel: *Store up for yourselves...* <note>The text breaks off as it begins to quote Matthew 6:20: "Store up for yourselves treasures in heaven, where neither moth nor rust consumes."</note>

<summary>This page provides moral and mystical interpretations of small creatures like the spider and the leech, as well as the "rust" and "moth" mentioned in the Gospels, linking them to human fragility, demonic temptation, and vices like pride and greed.</summary>

<keywords>Spider, Leech, Rust, Moth, Allegory, Human Fragility, Devil, Lust, Greed, Arrogance, Spiritual Gifts</keywords>

...treasures in heaven, where neither rust nor moth <note>original: "tinea"</note> destroys, and where thieves do not break in nor steal. <note>Matthew 6:20. The author uses this biblical warning to pivot from the physical nature of pests to their spiritual meaning.</note> The **Moth**, moreover, which secretly gnaws through clothing, signifies **Envy**, which tears apart good zeal and does not cease to destroy by comparing itself to virtue. For the **Thieves** are demons and heretics, of whom the Truth <note>original: "veritas" — a common medieval title for Christ, referring to his words in the Gospels</note> says: *All who have come are thieves and robbers.* <note>John 10:8.</note> <gloss>Fish</gloss> <note>original: "Pisces"</note>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual allegories of insects and pests on the previous page to the classification and etymology of aquatic life. It begins a new major section on "Fish," moving through the medieval taxonomy of the sea.</meta>

### cx.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'P' in red ink, filled with intricate red and blue penwork flourishes and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

**F**ish <note>original: "Pisces"</note> are so named from the same origin as livestock <note>original: "pecus"</note>, namely from "feeding" <note>original: "pascendo"</note>. These creatures that swim are called **reptiles** <note>original: "reptilia"</note> because they have the appearance and nature of creeping <note>original: "reptandi"</note>; for as they submerge themselves into the lowest depths, they "creep" through the water while swimming. Concerning this, David says: *This is the great and wide sea: there are creeping things without number.* <note>Psalm 104:25. Medieval scholars often grouped aquatic creatures with "creeping" things because of their fluid, legless movement through the water.</note>

**Amphibians** <note>original: "Amphibia"</note> are certain kinds of fish named because they have the habit of walking on land and the duty of swimming in the water. For "Amphi" in Greek is used to mean "both" <note>original: "utrumque"</note>: because they live both in the water and on the land, such as seals, crocodiles, and hippopotami—that is, "river horses" <note>original: "equi fluviales"</note>.

Now, humans gave names to livestock, wild beasts, and birds first, since they were seen and recognized earlier. The names for the types of **Fish**, however, were established gradually afterward, either from a likeness to land animals, or from their appearance, or their habits, or color, or shape, or even sex.

They are named from a likeness to land animals, such as:
*   **Frogs** <note>original: "rane"</note>
*   **Calves** <note>original: "uituli"; likely referring to sea-calves or seals</note>
*   **Lions** <note>original: "leones"; sea-lions</note>
*   **Black Merles** <note>original: "nigri meruli"; a type of wrasse</note>
*   **Peacocks** <note>original: "paui"</note>, so called for the divided colors painted on their back and neck
*   **Thrushes** <note>original: "turdi"</note>, dappled with a white color

And there are others that have claimed names for themselves according to the species of land animals.

They are named from the habits of land animals, such as **Sea-dogs** <note>original: "canes in mari"; likely sharks</note>, named after land dogs because they bite; and **Wolves** <note>original: "lupi"; sea-bass</note>, because they pursue others with wicked voracity.

They are named from their color, such as:
*   **Shadow-fish** <note>original: "umbre"</note>, because they are the color of a shadow.
*   **Gilt-heads** <note>original: "aurate"</note>, because they have the color of gold <note>original: "auri"</note> on their heads.
*   **Speckled-fish** <note>original: "uarii"</note>, because of their variety of markings, which the common people call **Trout** <note>original: "tructas"</note>.

They are named from their shape, such as **Globe-fish** <note>original: "orbi"</note>, which are round and consist almost entirely of a head; or the **Sole** <note>original: "solea"</note>, because it is like the soles of shoes <note>original: "calciamentorum soleis"</note>.

<summary>This page explains the etymology and classification of aquatic creatures, arguing that fish names are derived from land animal counterparts based on physical or behavioral similarities.</summary>
<keywords>fish, etymology, amphibians, reptiles, sea-dogs, trout, medieval taxonomy, crocodiles, hippopotami</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the general classification of aquatic life into specific entries for sea creatures. Having established that fish are named after their shapes, habits, or land-based counterparts, the text now introduces the first and most formidable of these: the Aspidochelone, often identified in the medieval mind with the whale.</meta>

There is a **beast** <note>original: "belua"</note> in the sea which in Greek is called the **Aspidochelone** <note>original: "aspido delone"; a compound of the Greek words for "shield" and "turtle."</note>. **Aspis**
in Latin is indeed "shield," and it is called a **turtle** <note>original: "testudo"</note>. It is also called a **whale** <note>original: "Cete"</note> on account of
the immense size of its body <note>original: "immanitatem corporis"</note>. For it is like that creature which received <note>original: "excepit"; this is a common medieval reference to the "great fish" that swallowed the prophet Jonah.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Aspidochelone, a legendary sea monster whose name combines the attributes of a shield and a turtle, emphasizing its vast size and hard, island-like back.</summary>
<keywords>Aspidochelone, whale, sea monster, etymology, Jonah, bestiary, aquatic life</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Aspidochelone, a massive sea monster often confused with an island. The previous page ended by comparing its size to the great fish that swallowed Jonah.</meta>

Jonah. Its belly was of such great size that he thought it was hell <note>original: "infernus"</note>, as Jonah himself said: "He heard me from the belly of hell." This creature raises its back above the waves of the sea in the middle of the ocean, and remains in one place for so long that a level plain is formed from the sand of the sea stirred up by the wind <gloss>so that</gloss> [v] grasses grow there. Wherefore sailors, thinking it to be an island, bring their ships alongside. Then they build fires for themselves; but the beast, feeling the heat of the fire, suddenly plunges into the water and drags the ship with it into the depths. Thus do they suffer who are of an unbelieving mind and are ignorant of the devil's trickeries. Placing their hope in him and binding themselves to his works, they are submerged together with him in the hell of fire <note>original: "gehenna ignis"</note>.

The nature of the beast is such: when it is hungry, it opens its mouth and belches forth a certain sweet-smelling scent from its mouth. When the smaller fish perceive its sweetness, they gather together inside its mouth. But when it feels its mouth is full, it suddenly shuts its mouth and swallows them down. So suffer those who are of little faith, addicted to pleasures and <unclear>lured</unclear> <note>original: "leceantur"; likely a misspelling of "alliciantur," meaning enticed.</note> to the bait; as if by certain scents, they are suddenly swallowed by the devil.

<margin>Whales</margin> **Whales** <note>original: "Balene"</note>, however, are beasts of immense size, named from "emitting" and "pouring" water <note>A medieval etymology linking the name to the casting up of water.</note>. For a **whale** <note>original: "ceti"</note> is a beast of the sea that casts up waves higher than the rest. For "bal" <note>original: "bal"; referring to the Greek verb βάλλειν (ballein), meaning to throw or cast.</note> is said to mean "to emit" in Greek. The male of the whale is the **musculus** <note>original: "musculus"; literally "little mouse," but used in bestiaries to describe a smaller companion-fish to the whale.</note>, through whose mating this beast is said to conceive.

<margin>Sawfish</margin> There is a beast in the sea called the **serra** <note>The "Sawfish," typically depicted in bestiaries as a winged creature that tries to race ships.</note>, which has enormous fins. When it sees a ship sailing on the ocean, it raises its fins above the water and strives to sail against the ship for thirty or forty stadia <note>A stadium was an ancient unit of distance, roughly 185 meters.</note>. And if the ship holds its course, the beast fails from the labor, and lowering its fins, it draws them back to itself.

<meta>This page continues the moralizing allegory of the sea monster (the Sawfish) and begins descriptions of dolphins and other marine creatures, drawing heavily on the *Etymologies* of Isidore of Seville. As noted, the scribe accidentally repeats the section on dolphins at the bottom of the page.</meta>

...whence they carry back the waves of the sea, so weary, to their own place in the depths. This beast has the figure of this world. This indeed represents the example of the righteous, who have crossed through the middle of the storms and tempests of this world without danger or the shipwreck of faith. The **Sawfish** <note>original: "Serra"; a mythical sea creature often depicted with wings and a serrated crest.</note>, however, that is that beast which was not able to sail with the ship, bears the figure of those who at the beginning began to persist in good works, but afterwards, not remaining in them, were overcome by various kinds of vices which submerge them like waves tossing in the sea even to the underworld. For the reward is promised not to those who begin, but to those who persevere.

<margin>**Dolphins**</margin>
**Dolphins** <note>original: "Delfines"</note> have a certain name or designation because they follow human voices, or because they gather in groups at the sound of music <note>original: "symphoniam"; refers to harmonious sound or musical instruments.</note>. Nothing in the sea is swifter than these. For they often fly over ships by leaping; and when they play in the waves and throw themselves with a leap into the masses of water, they are seen to signify storms. These are specifically named **Simones** <note>original: "symones"; a name derived from the Greek "simos," meaning "snub-nosed."</note>. There is also a kind of dolphin in the River Nile with a serrated back, which kill crocodiles by cutting their tender bellies.

<margin>**Sea horses**</margin>
**Sea horses** <note>original: "Equi marini"</note> are so called because in their front part they are horses, but in their rear they end <note>original: "soluuntur," literally "are loosened" or "dissolved"</note> in a fish. They are called **Bocas** and **Sea cows** <note>original: "boues marinos"</note> because they "low" <note>original: "boacatur"; a medieval etymology linking the name of the fish to the sound of cattle.</note>. **Ceruleans** <note>original: "Ceruli"</note> are named from their color. For "cerulean" is green mixed with black, just as the sea is.

Nothing in the sea is swifter than these. For they often fly over ships by leaping. When, however, they play in the waves and throw themselves with a leap into the masses of water, they are seen to signify storms. These are specifically named **Simones**. <margin>God blaspheme and</margin>

...crest, they kill them by cutting their tender bellies. <margin>**Sea Pigs**</margin> **Sea pigs** <note>original: "Porci marini"; likely referring to porpoises or dolphins.</note> are those which are commonly called "swine-fish," because while they seek food, they dig into the earth under the waters in the manner of a pig. For their throat performs the office of a mouth, and unless they plunge their snout into the sands, they cannot gather their fodder. <margin>**Crocodile**</margin> The **Crocodile** <note>original: "Cocodrillus"</note> is named for its saffron <note>original: "croceo"; the medieval etymology links *crocodilus* to *crocus* (saffron), suggesting a yellow color.</note> color. It is born in the Nile; a four-footed animal, powerful both on land and in the waters. It is generally twenty cubits in length, armed with a monstrous size of teeth and claws; and its skin is of such hardness that it does not feel the strike of stones repelled from its back. At night it rests in the waters, and by day on the ground. It warms its eggs in the earth. The male and female take turns guarding them. Certain fish having a serrated crest kill this creature by cutting open its tender belly. It is said to be the only one among animals to move its upper jaw. <margin>**Hippopotamus**</margin> It is called the **Hippopotamus** <note>original: "Ypopotamus"</note> because it is similar to a horse in its back, its mane, and its neighing; it has a turned-up snout, the teeth of a wild boar, a twisted tail, and cloven hooves. By day it stays in the waters; at night it grazes upon the crops. The Nile gives birth to this beast. <margin>**Red Mullet**</margin> The **Mullet** <note>original: "Mullus"</note> is so called because it is soft <note>original: "mollis"</note> and very tender. They say that by eating it, lust is inhibited and the sharpness of the eyesight is dimmed. Indeed, for men who often smell of fish after eating, if a mullet is killed in wine, those who drink from that wine will thereafter have a loathing for it. <margin>**Grey Mullet**</margin> The **Grey Mullet** <note>original: "Mugil"</note> has its name because it is very agile <note>original: "multum agilis"</note>. For when it senses the snares of the fishermen set out, it immediately turns back and leaps over the net so that you would think you see a fish flying. <margin>**Eel**</margin> The **Eel**’s <note>original: "Anguille"</note> similarity to a snake <note>original: "anguis"</note> gave it its name. Its origin is from the mud. Whence, whenever it is caught, it is so slippery that the harder one presses it, the faster it slips away. They say, moreover, that the river Ganges in the East has eels thirty [feet long]...

...was stained with vinegar by sponges, that is: he wiped it away. Nature teaches that it is an animal because blood clings to the rocks; for when it is pulled away, it lets out blood. The hollow **sponge** <note>original: "spungia"</note> truly signifies mystically the deceitful infidelity of the Jews, about whom it is written in the Gospel: "But they, putting a sponge full of vinegar around hyssop, offered it to his mouth." Among these fish-like animals, some are living in the waters and running about like fish; others are immediately fixed, like oysters and now sponges. 

Among these, those of the sea are said to have thin tubes <note>original: "fistulae"</note> and breath. Others are called females because they have larger and straighter tubes. Others are harder, which the Greeks call *tragi* <margin>Greek letters: *tragi*</margin> <note>original: "tragos"; meaning "goats," used here to describe the coarse texture of certain sponges.</note> and we can call "goat-like" <note>original: "hircosas"</note> because of their roughness. The softest kind of these are called *penicilli* <note>original: "penicilli"; literally "little brushes," referring to soft sponges used for medical cleaning.</note> because they are suitable for the humors of the eyes and are effective for wiping away eye inflammations. White sponges are made with care. For during the summer they are set out in the sun, and thus with Punic wax <note>A type of refined beeswax often mentioned in classical texts for its whitening properties.</note> they drink in their whiteness.

**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), whose "Natural History" was the standard encyclopedia for the Middle Ages.</note> says there are one hundred and fifty-four names of all the more living fish, divided into the kinds of sea-beasts <note>original: "beluorum"</note>, serpents common to land and water, crabs, shellfish, lobsters, sea-cattle, octopuses <note>original: "polypporum"</note>, soles, and lizard-fish like the **squid** <note>original: "luligo"</note> and similar things. 

Many of these, through a certain understanding of nature, recognize the order of their seasons; some wander in their own places without change. Among female fish, some conceive through the male and bring forth live young; others first lay eggs conceived without the male, which are then drenched by the male’s subsequent casting of seed. Those which have been touched by this gift are fertile <note>original: "genitalia"</note>; those truly which were not drenched remain sterile or rot. In the Scriptures, **fish** are designated by this name sometimes as good men, and sometimes truly as evil men; as it is in the Gospel where the Savior in a parable...

<margin>

But Lord grant me thy command, thy favor, and thy grace.

<note>This is a marginal inscription in Early Modern English, likely a personal prayer or pen trial by a later owner of the manuscript.</note>
</margin>

He tells of the **dragnet** <note>original: "sagena"; a large weighted net used for fishing by dragging it along the bottom or through the water.</note> cast into the sea, saying: "The kingdom of heaven is like a **dragnet** cast into the sea, gathering fish of every kind; which, when it was full, they drew out, and sitting by the shore, they chose the good into their vessels, but the bad they threw away." Under this parable, the **Holy Church** <note>original: "sancta ecclesia"</note> is compared to a net, because it has been entrusted to fishers <note>Referring to the Apostles as "fishers of men."</note>; and through it, everyone is drawn from the waves of this world toward the eternal kingdom, lest they be drowned in the depth of eternal death. It gathers from every kind of fish because it calls to the forgiveness of sins the wise and the foolish, the free and the enslaved, the rich and the poor, the strong and the weak. 

But then, at the end of the world, the net of the Holy Church shows what it was dragging. Indeed, the "good fish" are the good men who will then be received into vessels—that is, into eternal and lasting mansions—but the bad shall be thrown out into the furnace of fire, namely where there will be "weeping and gnashing of teeth" <note>A common Gospel phrase (Matthew 13:50) describing the fate of the damned.</note>. These fish can also signify the "wise ones" of this world, as in that verse of the **Psalmist** <note>Reference to Psalm 8:8, "The birds of the heavens, and the fish of the sea."</note>: "The birds of the heaven and the fish of the sea, which walk through the paths of the sea." 

The **birds of the heaven** <note>original: "volucres caeli"</note> are those men who, inflated by the wind of boasting, are carried as if through a sea of air; and by despising humble things, they are lifted up in their lofty minds. Birds <note>original: "volucres"</note> are named for their "frequent flight" <note>original: "volatu"; a medieval etymological explanation linking the name of the animal to its action.</note>. Perhaps the **fish of the sea** signify the **philosophers** <note>original: "philosophos"</note>, who travel through the nature of this world with an errant curiosity. For just as the former [the birds] are set against the flood and emerge from the rising inundation of the deep, so these [philosophers], with bowed heads, search out the "veins of things" <note>The underlying principles or causes of natural phenomena.</note> through human reason with unceasing labor. 

But although there are fish of the rivers too, he [the Psalmist] here specifically used the term "of the sea" because of those who seem to themselves to be wise. Truly, such as these are eventually and happily subjected to the feet of the Lord when they come to the disposition of the **Christian religion**...

->· 117 ·<-

...men. Fishers <note>original: "piscatores"</note> are the apostles or other preachers, of whom it is read in the Gospel: "Come after me and I will make you to be fishers of men." And in a negative sense, through Isaiah: "The fishers shall mourn." The **holy fish** in the Gospel are those who filled the net with large fish. The **reprobate fish** in the Gospel are the "bad ones" they threw out; and in Amos: "And if even the fish of the sea shall be gathered." The **Fish** is also the Lord Jesus Christ, roasted in the fire of tribulation; as in the Gospel: "They set before him a piece of roasted fish and a honeycomb." The two fish joined with the five loaves are understood as the two Testaments of the Law, or the two precepts of charity <note>The commands to love God and love one's neighbor.</note>. They are the two persons: the royal and the priestly. In the Gospel: "And he took the five loaves and the two fish." 

The **Hook** <note>original: "hamus"</note> is the divinity of the Son of God, which lay hidden, clothed in flesh. This "hook" is hidden within the "bait," regarding which hook the Lord, speaking to Job, says: "Can you draw out **Leviathan** <note>A primordial sea monster, here representing the Devil.</note> with a hook?" Because that same enemy of the human race, while in the manner of a fish he bit the bait of the Lord’s body during the Passion, he was slain by divine power <note>This "Hook and Bait" theory was a popular medieval explanation for how Christ's death defeated the Devil.</note>. In a negative sense, it refers to the devil, as seen in Habakkuk: "he caught them in his hook."

<margin>
Of Trees.
Of Herbs.
</margin>

**A**rbor <note>The name for **trees**.</note> or **herbs** <note>original: "herbarum"</note>: the name is believed to be derived from the word for "field" <note>original: "arvum"; the author is suggesting an etymological link between *arbor* and *arvum*.</note>, because they are spread upon the earth and cling to it with their roots. Both are almost similar for this reason: that one is born from the other. For when the seed is cast into the earth, the herb first arises. Then, growing stronger, it rises into a tree; and within a short time, where you saw an herb, you may look up at a grove of trees. **Groves** <note>original: "arbusta"</note> are so called because they stand from trees. Others wish to understand a "grove" as a place in which trees are found, such as a **willow-thicket** <note>original: "salictum"</note> or willow-beds and greenery, or a willow-grove. They are called **young shoots** <note>original: "virgultae"</note> and **green shrubs** <note>original: "frutices"</note> for short. While they are "branched," the plural name is **thickets** <note>original: "frutecta"</note>. A **wood** <note>original: "nemus"</note> is named after the "divinities" <note>original: "numinibus"</note> because pagans used to consecrate idols there; these are sacred groves.
