<meta>This page continues the botanical section of the manuscript, likely moving from general plant descriptions into a detailed etymological analysis of tree parts, drawing heavily from Isidore of Seville’s "Etymologies." It shifts from the previous page's allegorical fish to the physical and linguistic nature of trees.</meta>

<margin>
Simon Down
by his own hand
<unclear>u</unclear> then
</margin>

Larger trees are shady with foliage. A **grove** <note>original: "lucus"</note> is a dense, high-standing collection of trees. It is called by this name because it excludes the sun and rises into the heights. **Grafting** <note>original: "inscitio"</note> is the term used when a shoot from a different trunk is inserted into the base of a sterile tree. **Plants** are from trees. **Nurseries** <note>original: "plantaria"</note>, however, are those which are born from seed with roots and are transferred from their own soil.

The **Root** <note>original: "radix"</note> is so called because it is fixed in the earth like certain rays <note>original: "radiis"</note> and enters into the depths. For natural philosophers <note>original: "phisici"; here referring to students of the natural world</note> say that the depth of the roots is equal to the height of the trees. The **Trunk** <note>original: "truncus"</note> is the stature of the tree in its standing position. The ancients used to call the **Bark** *corucem*; it is called bark <note>original: "cortex"</note> because it covers the wood like skin <note>original: "corio"</note>. The **Inner Bark** <note>original: "liber"</note> is the interior part of the bark, so named because the bark is "liberated" <note>original: "liberato"</note>, that is, taken away. It is a certain middle substance between the wood and the bark.

**Branches** <note>original: "rami"</note> are those which flow from the trunk, just as **shoots** <note>original: "surculi"</note> come from the branches. Shoots are named after the "suffering of the earth" <note>original: "passione terre"; an etymological wordplay linking *surculus* to *solum* (soil) and *ex-surgo* (to rise out of)</note>. A **shrub** or **thicket** <note>original: "virgultum"</note> is that which sprouts from the root. A **branch** comes from the main wood of the tree. A **twig** <note>original: "virga"</note> comes from the branches. Specifically, however, a **sucker** <note>original: "uitulum"</note> is the name for that which is born at the root of the tree and is cut away by farmers as useless; and hence it is called a "little vine-shoot" because it is taken from the twig.

A **twig** or **rod** <note>original: "virga"</note> is named either from "virtue" <note>original: "uirute"</note>, because it has much power <note>original: "uim"</note> in itself, or from its "greenness" <note>original: "uiriditate"</note>, or because it bears evidence that it is green. For this reason, magicians use them to pacify serpents among themselves, and therefore enemies hold their own men bound in it; philosophers, kings, teachers, messengers, and legates also use this. The highest parts of trees are called **whips** <note>original: "flagella"</note>, because they are known to endure frequent winds. 

Those which are called "hair" <note>original: "comae"</note> are the leaves; in Greek they are called *hyle* <note>original: "hyle" (ὕλη), meaning wood, matter, or forest</note>. From this they also derive the name for <image-desc>large circular brown stain covering the text, obscuring the word 'silvam' (forest)</image-desc> through derivation. **Flowers** are so called because they quickly "flow" <note>original: "fluunt"</note> from the trees, because they are quickly dissolved. In these there is a double grace of color and scent. The South Wind [dissolves] the flowers...

<summary>This page provides a medieval botanical glossary based on Isidore of Seville, explaining the etymological origins of terms for tree parts like roots, bark, and branches. It includes fascinating historical lore, such as the use of rods by magicians and messengers, and the "scientific" belief that roots grow as deep as the tree is tall.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical etymologies likely derived from Isidore of Seville's "Etymologies" (Book XVII). It transitions from the parts of plants discussed on the previous page to the names of specific fruits and trees, explaining their linguistic origins through medieval eyes.</meta>

->Air comforted and well-praised<-
->Prague<-

...they flow with the Zephyr <note>the West Wind</note>. A **sprout** <note>original: "germen"</note> is called a "pregnant shoot," from the word for "generating" <note>original: "generando"</note>, from which also comes the word "generation." **Fruit** <note>original: "fructus"</note> takes its name from "enjoying" <note>original: "fruendo"</note>, that is, we enjoy it because we feed upon it; from which also comes the word for **produce** <note>original: "fruges"</note>. Strictly speaking, "fruits" are the names given to the produce of fields and trees that we use in this way. In animals, however, the term "fruit" <note>offspring</note> is used loosely and metaphorically. **Pome fruits** <note>original: "poma"</note> are named from "opulent" <note>original: "opimo"</note>, that is, from a great abundance of plenty. We call them **apples** <note>original: "mala"</note> because they are suitable for eating. **Firewood** <note>original: "ligna"</note> is named because when set on fire, it is stirred into **light** <note>original: "lumen"</note>; hence "wood" <note>original: "lignum"</note> is said to be that which gives light. A **torch** <note>original: "torris"</note> is a piece of scorched wood which the common people call a "firebrand" <note>original: "ticionem"</note>, half-burned in the hearth and then extinguished. **Stalks** <note>original: "stipite"</note> are like stubble, tiny shoots dried by the sun.

If we proceed thus concerning the names of the trees themselves: the **palm** <note>original: "palma"</note> is so named because it adorns "victorious hands" <note>original: "manu"</note>, or because it has spreading branches in the manner of a human palm. For it is a tree distinguished by victory, clothed in a beautiful and graceful thicket and long-lasting foliage; its leaves stay green without falling. The Greeks call this tree the **phoenix** <note>original: "phoenicen"</note> because it lasts a long time, named after that bird of Arabia <margin><image-desc>A simple line drawing in the margin of a bird in profile, likely intended to represent the phoenix mentioned in the adjacent text.</image-desc></margin> which is said to live for many years. Its fruits are called **dates** <note>original: "dactili"</note> named after their resemblance to **fingers** <note>original: "digitorum"</note>.

The **laurel** <note>original: "laurus"</note> is named from the "sight of praise" <note>original: "laudis"</note>, as if the heads of victors were being crowned. Later, the letter "b" was removed and replaced by "r" so it was called "laurus"—just as in the word for "bird-snare" <note>original: "auculum"</note> which is now called "auciale," and "medidies" which is now called **midday** <note>original: "meridies"</note>. The Greeks call this tree **Daphne** because it never loses its greenness. For this reason, victors are crowned with it rather than others. This is the only tree that the common people believe is never struck by lightning. The **wild apple** <note>original: "malus agrestis"</note> is so named because its fruit is the roundest of all pome fruits. Whence also these "true apples" <note>original: "vera mala"</note> are exceedingly round. The **honey-apple** <note>original: "malomellus"</note> is named for its sweetness, because its fruit has the flavor of honey or because they are preserved in honey. The **pomegranate** <note>original: "malum punicum"</note> is so called because it comes from the Punic region...

A man with owl-wisdom is worth a wash <note>This marginalia, likely added by a later English reader, is a proverb suggesting that "owl-wisdom" (pretentious or false intelligence) is worthless or needs to be cleansed away.</note>

...of a certain kind assists it. Thus also the **pomegranate** <note>original: *malum punicum*</note>, so called because it rejoices in a multitude of grains <note>seeds</note> within its moderate size. The **fig** <note>original: *ficus*</note> is named in Latin from "fecundity" <note>original: *fecunditate*</note>, for it is believed to be more fertile than other trees; for three or four times throughout each year it produces fruit, and as some wither, others arise. These are also noted as **dried figs** <note>original: *carice*</note> because of their abundance. The **Egyptian fig** <note>the sycamore-fig</note> is said to be even more fertile. Its wood, when thrown into water, immediately sinks, but when it has lain in the mud for a while, it usually rises to the surface; this is contrary to natural law, for once soaked, it ought to remain at the bottom due to the weight of the moisture. In a similar way, **wild bulls** <note>original: *semilibus malo*, likely a corruption of *ferocissimos tauros*</note> tied to a fig tree are said to suddenly become tame. The **mulberry** <note>original: *morus*</note> is so named by the Greeks;

If its leaves are handled, they produce a single [substance].

This tree was chosen by Jupiter; it possesses such power in its fruit that when placed among herbal juices or in the cold, it sweats out, seizes, and extinguishes whatever is poisonous. **Nuts** <note>original: *nuces*</note> is the name given in autumn to all fruits covered by a harder shell, such as pine nuts, hazelnuts, acorns, chestnuts, and almonds. These are also called **kernels** <note>original: *nuclei*</note> because they are fruits covered by a shell. On the contrary, all soft fruits are called **apples** <note>original: *mala*</note>, often with the addition of names indicating where they first originated, such as **peaches** <note>original: *persica*, from Persia</note>, **pomegranates** <note>original: *punica*, from Carthage</note>, and **Matutian apples** <note>named after the Roman agriculturist Gaius Matius</note>. Among all trees, it is the first to clothe itself in blossom, and it anticipates the following bushes in bringing forth its fruit. <meta>This page continues the botanical encyclopedia of Isidore of Seville, focusing on various species of trees—specifically the chestnut, oak, beech, carob, pistachio, pine, fir, and cedar. The text relies on "etymology" (the origin of words) to explain the physical properties or historical uses of these plants, a common method of medieval scientific inquiry.</meta>

## The Chestnut
The **chestnut** <note>original: *castanea*</note> is so named because its twin fruits hide within a casing <note>the prickly burr</note> in the manner of testicles. When these are cast out, they are as if "castrated" <note>original: *castrati*; the author suggests the name *castanea* comes from this word</note>. This tree is similar to the oak and is accustomed to sprouting back like a thicket.

## The Holm Oak
The **holm oak** <note>original: *ilex*</note> is named from the word "chosen" <note>original: *electo*</note>. It bears a tree-fruit <note>the acorn</note> that humans first chose for their own sustenance. Hence the poet says: "The first mortals belched acorn after acorn" <note>The Roman poet Lucretius and others described early humans as "acorn-eaters" before the invention of agriculture</note>. For indeed, they lived on acorns before they had grain or other foods. The **beech** <note>original: *fagus*</note> and the **winter oak** <note>original: *esculus*</note> are called nut-bearing trees because it is believed that people once lived on their fruits; they were the primary food that sustained them. For the *esculus* is named from "food" <note>original: *esca*</note>, while the *fagus* is named from the Greek; for in the Greek tongue, "to eat" <note>original: *phagein*</note> is called such. The **carob** <note>original: *xyloglycon*</note>, which the Latins corruptly called *siliqua*, received its name from the Greeks because the wood of its fruit is sweet. For in the Greek language, *xylon* means "wood" and *glykon* means "sweet." The juice squeezed from the fruit of this tree is called *akana* <note>a medicinal juice</note> by the Greeks.

## The Pistachio
The **pistachio** tree <note>original: *pistatia*</note> is so called because the cluster of its fruit gives off the scent of "pure nard" <note>original: *nardi pisticum*; *pisticus* is a Latinization of the Greek word for "genuine" or "pure," famously used in the Bible to describe expensive ointment</note>.

## The Pine
The **pine** <note>original: *pinus*</note> is a pitch-bearing tree. It is named from the "sharpness" <note>original: *accumen*</note> of its leaves, for the ancients used the word *pinnum* for something sharp. There is another kind, called *peuke* <note>original: *peuten*</note> in Greek, which we call the **pitch pine** <note>original: *picea*</note> because it "sweats" out pitch. In the islands of Germany, the "tears" <note>sap</note> of this tree produce **amber** <note>original: *electrum*</note>. For a flowing drop hardens into a solid state through cold or mild heat and becomes a gemstone; it receives its name from its quality, being called *succinum* <note>the Latin word for amber</note> because it is the "juice" <note>original: *succus*</note> of a tree. The pine is believed to be beneficial to those losing their minds <note>original: *amentibus*</note> who are kept beneath its shade. The **yew** <note>original: *taxus*; the OCR is unclear here, but the source text identifies it</note>, however, is said to be harmful to everyone.

## The Fir
The **fir** <note>original: *abies*</note> is so named because it "goes far" <note>original: *abeat*</note> beyond other trees and stands out on high. When it is grown, it is devoid of moisture and is therefore considered "apt" <note>original: *abilis*</note> or light. Some have called this the "Gallic tree" because of its whiteness. It is, moreover, without knots.

## The Cedar
The **cedar** is what the Greeks called *kedros*. Its leaves have a resemblance to the cypress. Its wood has a pleasant scent and is long-lasting, for moths never breed in it <note>cedar is naturally insect-repellent</note>. Because of this durability, the ceiling panels <note>original: *lacinaria*</note> in temples are made from this wood. The resin of this wood is called *cedria*.

<summary>This page provides the perceived linguistic origins and physical properties of several trees, including the chestnut, oak, pine, and cedar, following the encyclopedic tradition of Isidore of Seville. It highlights ancient beliefs about human diets (acorns) and the medicinal or practical uses of wood and resins (amber, cedar oil).</summary>
<keywords>botany, etymology, Isidore of Seville, chestnut, acorn, amber, pine resin, cedar wood, ancient diet</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical encyclopedia of Isidore of Seville, exploring the symbolic and practical properties of various trees. The previous page discussed the chestnut, carob, and oak; this page moves into the cypress, juniper, plane tree, ash, elm, and poplar. The marginalia on this page consists of later medical notes written in English, likely from the early modern period, focusing on remedies for ailments like gout and jaundice.</meta>

<margin>
John <unclear>Gr...</unclear>
By members then the gland does... <note>original: "pome"</note>
purged <unclear>...</unclear> by the... <note>original: "pr."</note>
for the gout <unclear>...</unclear>
for the yellow jaundice <note>original: "jawndys"</note>
and <unclear>...</unclear> the <unclear>...</unclear>
for the <unclear>...</unclear>
for the dropsy <note>an old term for swelling caused by fluid retention</note>
for the <unclear>...</unclear> by the point
to the stomach to the <unclear>...</unclear>
</margin>

...which is so powerful in preserving books that most things [kept] within it neither suffer from moths nor grow old with time. The **cypress** <note>original: *cypressus*</note> is so called in Greek because its head rises from a round base to a sharp peak. For this reason, it is also called a "cone" <note>original: *conus*</note>, meaning a high roundness. From this, its fruit is also called a cone because its roundness is such that it imitates a cone. This is why trees are called "cone-bearing" <note>coniferous</note>. This wood has a quality nearly equal to the **cedar** for the construction of temples; for this reason, it is worked into an impenetrable solidity. The ancients were accustomed to place cypress branches near a funeral pyre so that, while the corpses were being burned, they might overwhelm the odor with the pleasantness of their own scent.

The **juniper** <note>original: *juniperus*</note> is called such in Greek because it tapers from a wide base to a narrow point like a fire <note>original: *pyr*; Isidore often relies on phonetic similarities for his explanations</note>, or because it holds fire for a long time once it has caught. It does this to such an extent that if coals are covered with its ash, they can last up to a year. For among the Greeks, *pyr* means "fire."

The **plane tree** <note>original: *platanus*</note> is named from the width of its leaves, or because the tree itself is spread out and wide. For in the Greek tongue, they call "wide" *platos*. It is said that the **oak** <note>original: *quercus*</note> is among those trees that live for seven thousand years. As for the plane tree, it is spread out in breadth; for its leaf is similar to the hands of Venus and to soft hands <note>referring to the palm-like shape of the leaf</note>.

The **oak** <note>original: *quercum*</note> is so named because formerly its oracles were uttered before humans sang their responses <note>referring to the ancient belief that the rustling of oak leaves at Dodona provided divine prophecies</note>. It is a very long-lived tree; thus we read of the Oak of Mamre, under which Abraham lived, which is said to have lasted through many centuries almost until the reign of King Constantine. The fruit of this tree is called the **gall** <note>original: *galla*; these nut-like growths on oaks were historically vital for making permanent black ink</note>.

The **ash** <note>original: *fraxinus*</note> is said to be named because it grows more often in rough and mountainous places <note>original: *fragosa*</note>; hence it is a derivation of "broken" <note>original: *fractum*</note>, just as "mountainous" is derived from "mountain." Of this it is said: "And spears made of ash."

The **elm** <note>original: *ulmus*</note> is named because it loves a riverbed <note>original: *alvum*</note>; for it is primarily born near the water. This tree lives easily beside the waves, and is therefore both of the earth and the water, because it is nourished in damp places. The elm took its name because it thrives better in marshy <note>original: *uliginosis*</note> and humid places. For in mountainous and rough areas, it is less flourishing.

The **poplar** <note>original: *populus*</note> is so called because a multitude <note>original: *multitudo*</note> grows from its base <note>Isidore here makes a linguistic connection between the tree and "the people" (*populus*), suggesting the many sprouts represent a crowd</note>.

<summary>This page continues Isidore of Seville's botanical descriptions, providing the linguistic origins and physical properties of trees like the cypress, juniper, plane tree, oak, ash, elm, and poplar. Later English marginalia provides a series of folk medical remedies for conditions such as jaundice and gout.</summary>
<keywords>Isidore of Seville, botany, cypress, juniper, plane tree, oak, ash, elm, poplar, Mamre, medicine, gout, jaundice</keywords>
